Yes. The richest way to forage this territory is not simply “strange programming languages,” but **languages that relocate computation into another cultural form**. Shakespeare puts computation inside drama; Piet puts it inside painting. Once you use that criterion, a much more interesting lineage appears.

| Language | Source code masquerades as / becomes | What makes it interesting |
|---|---|---|
| **Chef** - David Morgan-Mar, 2002 | **Cooking recipe** | Variables become ingredients, stacks become mixing bowls/baking dishes, and operations become cooking instructions. Some programs can simultaneously be executable and approximately usable recipes. This may be the closest conceptual sibling to Shakespeare. ([Esolangs](https://esolangs.org/wiki/Chef?utm_source=chatgpt.com)) |
| **Velato** - Daniel Temkin, 2009 | **Music / MIDI composition** | The MIDI file *is* the source. Pitch intervals relative to a changing command root encode operations, deliberately leaving room for programs that actually sound musical. ([Esolangs](https://esolangs.org/wiki/Velato?utm_source=chatgpt.com)) |
| **Rockstar** - Dylan Beattie, 2018 | **1980s rock lyrics** | Programs are intended to read as songs; its grammar includes unusual freedoms specifically to give the programmer “poetic license.” It began as a joke about “rockstar developers,” but is fully specified and implemented. ([Esolangs](https://esolangs.org/wiki/Rockstar?utm_source=chatgpt.com)) |
| **Beatnik** - Cliff Biffle | **English prose / Beat poetry** | It ignores conventional keywords and computes the **Scrabble value of each word**. Thus word choice simultaneously carries literary meaning and machine instruction. ([Esolangs](https://esolangs.org/wiki/Beatnik?utm_source=chatgpt.com)) |
| **Poetic** | **Poetry** | Instructions are determined by **word lengths**, so the same computational structure can potentially be rewritten through radically different poems. ([Esolangs](https://esolangs.org/wiki/Poetic_%28esolang%29?utm_source=chatgpt.com)) |
| **Whitespace** - Brady & Morris, 2003 | **Nothing visible** | Only spaces, tabs, and linefeeds constitute code; all visible characters are ignored. The computational object occupies what ordinary programming treats as absence. ([Esolangs](https://esolangs.org/wiki/Whitespace?utm_source=chatgpt.com)) |
| **AsciiDots** - Aaron Janse | **ASCII drawing / circuit diagram** | Dots physically travel through paths drawn using ASCII characters, encountering operations along the way. Here **diagrammatic topology literally becomes control flow**. ([Esolangs](https://esolangs.org/wiki/AsciiDots?utm_source=chatgpt.com)) |
| **Befunge** - 1993 | **Spatial typography** | One of the foundational 2-D languages. Instead of progressing line-by-line, an instruction pointer moves through a two-dimensional field and changes direction. The *placement* of characters becomes executable structure. ([Esolangs](https://esolangs.org/wiki/Befunge?utm_source=chatgpt.com)) |
| **Hexagony** - Martin Ender | **Geometric figure** | Source must inhabit a regular **hexagonal grid**. Six instruction pointers move through it and control flow wraps around its geometry. It turns program layout into a navigable space. ([Esolangs](https://esolangs.org/wiki/Hexagony?utm_source=chatgpt.com)) |
| **Cubix** | **Cube** | The source is wrapped around the six faces of a cube; execution crosses from face to face. The program acquires an implied three-dimensional topology. ([Esolangs](https://esolangs.org/wiki/Cubix?utm_source=chatgpt.com)) |
| **Marbelous** - 2014 | **Rube Goldberg / marble machine** | Values are represented as numbered marbles falling through a 2-D board of gates, cloners, deflectors, portals, synchronizers, etc. Computation becomes something you can almost imagine constructing physically. ([Esolangs](https://esolangs.org/wiki/Marbelous?utm_source=chatgpt.com)) |
| **Folders** - Daniel Temkin, 2015 | **File-system architecture** | There is literally no source-code file. The **nested directory structure is the program**; filenames are largely irrelevant. The file system ceases to contain the program and becomes the program. ([Esolangs](https://esolangs.org/wiki/Folders?utm_source=chatgpt.com)) |
| **Olympus** - Daniel Temkin, 2022 | **Prayer / religious invocation** | Instead of commanding a machine, the programmer makes elaborate requests to Greek gods. Different gods govern memory, loops, branching, I/O, etc.; insufficient or repetitive praise can offend them. The syntax deliberately reverses the normal master/servant metaphor of programming. ([Esolangs](https://esolangs.org/wiki/Olympus?utm_source=chatgpt.com)) |
| **Entropy** - Daniel Temkin, 2010 | **Decay / glitch** | The important transformation isn't cosmetic. **Every value deteriorates whenever it is accessed**, so programmers have to stop treating precision and control as givens. ([Esolangs](https://esolangs.org/wiki/Entropy?utm_source=chatgpt.com)) |
| **Valence** - Daniel Temkin | **Ambiguous spoken language** | Symbols deliberately have multiple grammatical interpretations-variable, digit, command, type, expression-and **every possible reading executes in parallel** unless explicitly disambiguated. That is a remarkably radical attack on programming's usual insistence that one expression have one syntactic reading. ([Esolangs](https://esolangs.org/wiki/Valence?utm_source=chatgpt.com)) |
| **LOLCODE** - 2007 | **Internet vernacular / LOLcat speech** | Its grammar recreates LOLcat pidgin: `HAI`, `CAN HAS`, `VISIBLE`, `GIMMEH`, etc. Computation gets embedded in an existing online sociolect rather than literary high culture. ([Esolangs](https://esolangs.org/wiki/LOLCODE?utm_source=chatgpt.com)) |
| **ArnoldC** - Lauri Hartikka, 2013 | **Arnold Schwarzenegger dialogue** | The entire instruction vocabulary comes from Schwarzenegger quotations: `STICK AROUND` is a loop, `CHILL` ends it, `GET UP` adds, and so forth. ([Esolangs](https://esolangs.org/wiki/ArnoldC?utm_source=chatgpt.com)) |
| **Ook!** - David Morgan-Mar | **Orangutan utterances** | Brainfuck's eight operations are encoded entirely through combinations of `Ook.`, `Ook?`, and `Ook!`. An extreme example of changing the expressive surface while largely preserving the underlying machine. ([Esolangs](https://esolangs.org/wiki/Ook%21?utm_source=chatgpt.com)) |

### The particularly important distinction

There are actually **three different experiments hiding inside Shakespeare and Piet**.

**1. Code disguised as another text.**  
Shakespeare, Chef, Rockstar, Beatnik, Poetic, ArnoldC. The fascinating question becomes:

> **How much freedom can the human-readable surface retain while remaining executable?**

The strongest members aren't mere keyword substitutions. Beatnik is especially good because *lexical choice itself* has computational consequences through Scrabble values. ([Esolangs](https://esolangs.org/wiki/Beatnik?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

**2. Code whose medium changes.**  
Piet, Velato, Folders, AsciiDots, Befunge, Hexagony, Cubix.

Here “program text” stops being synonymous with text. An image, MIDI sequence, directory tree, diagram, or spatial field becomes a legitimate source representation. Folders is particularly severe: it has **no source files at all**; nested directories encode its parse-tree-like structure. ([Esolangs](https://esolangs.org/wiki/Folders?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

**3. Code whose metaphor changes what programming means.**  
Olympus, Entropy, Valence, Marbelous.

These are potentially even more important. They don't merely dress conventional computation differently; they change the **relationship between programmer, instruction, machine, and interpretation**. Olympus makes commands into supplications; Entropy makes information intrinsically perishable; Valence makes ambiguity executable. ([Esolangs](https://esolangs.org/wiki/Olympus?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

And **Valence is a monster** for the question you're circling: ordinary programming languages work desperately to eliminate ambiguity; Valence says a symbol can possess several legitimate readings and executes those readings concurrently. That starts looking less like “code that resembles language” and more like **a machine semantics for polysemy**.

### The mother lode

Daniel Temkin is the person I would forage hardest. His framing is essentially that **programming-language design itself can be an artistic medium**. His work includes Velato, Folders, Entropy, Olympus, and Valence, among many others. His 2025 MIT Press book *Forty-Four Esolangs: The Art of Esoteric Code* explicitly presents esolangs as a way of challenging conventional definitions of language, code, and computer. ([GitHub](https://github.com/rottytooth?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

Temkin makes an especially useful theoretical move: every programming language has both a **syntax**-what qualifies as an utterance in the language-and a **semantics embodied by a virtual machine**-what those utterances cause to happen. Esolangs expose the normally invisible metaphors built into both. ([The MIT Press Reader](https://thereader.mitpress.mit.edu/the-hacker-folk-art-of-esoteric-coding/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)) His academic writing goes further, distinguishing **experiential esolangs**, whose argument becomes apparent by programming in them, from **conceptual esolangs**, whose rules may themselves constitute the work even when useful programs cannot be written. ([Revistas UCP](https://revistas.ucp.pt/index.php/jsta/article/view/7297?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

That gives you a much stronger family tree than simply:

**Shakespeare → language as play**  
**Piet → language as painting**

It becomes:

**Chef → recipe as program**  
**Velato → composition as program**  
**Beatnik → lexical choice as operation**  
**Whitespace → absence as program**  
**AsciiDots → diagram as program**  
**Folders → organization as program**  
**Marbelous → mechanism as program**  
**Olympus → invocation as program**  
**Entropy → decay as semantics**  
**Valence → ambiguity as execution**

The wager hiding underneath all of them is much bigger:

> **What things can be made to count as a description of a computation?**

That is the seam I would dig into rather than “esoteric languages” generally.

---

Yes - and Emojicode helps sharpen the taxonomy because it sits in a slightly different place than Shakespeare or Piet.

**Emojicode changes the sign system more than the computational metaphor.** Its official documentation describes it as a strongly typed, multi-paradigm language with classes, optionals, generics, closures, protocols, packages, threads, and native compilation. Program structure is expressed with emoji: for example, `🏁` marks the executable entry block, while `🍇 … 🍉` delimit code blocks. ([Emojicode](https://www.emojicode.org/docs/reference/welcome?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

So I’d place these along a spectrum:

**Python → Emojicode → Shakespeare → Chef → Piet → Folders → Valence**

Moving rightward, the intervention gets deeper.

- **Emojicode:** replace conventional textual tokens with a pictographic vocabulary.
- **Shakespeare:** make executable syntax inhabit an existing cultural genre.
- **Chef:** make the domain metaphor-ingredients, bowls, recipes-part of the operational semantics.
- **Piet:** abandon textual source as the primary medium; visual arrangement becomes executable.
- **Folders:** abandon the source file itself; filesystem structure becomes syntax.
- **Valence:** attack the deeper assumption that an expression ought to have one unambiguous interpretation.

That suggests a really useful distinction for your notes:

### 1. **Lexical estrangement**
The machine is mostly familiar; the vocabulary changes.

**Emojicode, LOLCODE, ArnoldC, Ook!**

The question is:

> What happens when programming's conventional vocabulary is replaced by another symbolic dialect?

### 2. **Genre capture**
Source code becomes recognizable as another kind of human artifact.

**Shakespeare, Chef, Rockstar, Beatnik, Poetic**

Now:

> Can one inscription participate simultaneously in two language games?

A Shakespeare program is both something the compiler interprets and something a human can encounter as theatrical dialogue.

### 3. **Medium displacement**
Programming leaves conventional text.

**Piet, Velato, AsciiDots, Folders, Cubix**

Now the question changes dramatically:

> What substrates can carry operational description?

Image. Music. Diagram. Directory tree. Spatial topology.

### 4. **Semantic estrangement**
The language changes the fundamental rules by which computation is understood.

**Entropy, Valence, Olympus, Befunge**

This is the richest category theoretically because the experiment isn't merely representational. It asks whether programming *must* mean deterministic command, stable values, linear control, and unambiguous interpretation.

And that exposes a weakness in the common definition of an esolang you quoted. Calling them languages made to be “intentionally difficult, humorous, or aesthetically unique” is true of many, but undersells the form. The Esolang Wiki itself gives the broader criterion: languages designed to experiment with unusual ideas, deliberately resist ordinary programming, or operate as jokes rather than practical engineering. ([Esolangs](https://esolangs.org/wiki/Main_Page?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

For your project I would define them more aggressively:

> **An esolang is an executable argument about what programming could be.**

Then the examples become propositions:

**Emojicode:** programming can be pictographic.  
**Shakespeare:** programming can be dramatic speech.  
**Chef:** programming can be procedural instruction from another domain.  
**Piet:** programming can be pictorial composition.  
**Velato:** programming can be musical relation.  
**Whitespace:** programming can inhabit absence.  
**Folders:** programming can be organization.  
**Befunge:** programming can be spatial navigation.  
**Entropy:** computation need not preserve its values.  
**Valence:** programming need not eliminate ambiguity.

And that yields an especially strong bridge to your prompting work:

> **Esolangs ask what may count as program text. Generative AI asks what may count as a program.**

That is a much bigger historical lineage than “prompt engineering appeared with LLMs.” The esolang tradition has been systematically probing the boundary between **description and execution** for decades. Emojicode is one particularly legible point on that trajectory because it demonstrates that even something as culturally loaded and apparently informal as emoji can be stabilized into a compiler-recognized operational notation. ([Emojicode](https://www.emojicode.org/docs/reference/syntax?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

Yes. **Logo, Scratch, p5.js, and Put-That-There belong in the same genealogy**, even though none except perhaps Logo is usefully described as an esolang. They are experiments in **where the program lives and what form an instruction can take**.

| System | Date | What becomes programmable | Crucial move |
|---|---:|---|---|
| **Logo** | 1967 | **Movement** | `FORWARD 50`, `RIGHT 90`: language becomes embodied navigation |
| **Put-That-There** | 1979 | **Speech + gesture + situated reference** | “that” and “there” acquire their values from pointing |
| **Scratch** | 2007 | **Manipulable blocks** | syntax becomes physical-looking objects that can be snapped together |
| **p5.js** | 2013 | **The sketch** | textual programming is reframed as iterative drawing/making |
| Shakespeare PL | 2001 | **Drama** | play dialogue becomes executable |
| Piet | 1991 | **Painting** | color and spatial transition become executable |
| Emojicode | 2016 | **Pictograms** | conventional programming grammar acquires a visual lexicon |

### Logo is much more important here than “a beginner language”

Logo was created in 1967 by Wally Feurzeig, Seymour Papert, and Cynthia Solomon. It belongs to the Lisp family; its first implementation, **Ghost**, was itself written in Lisp on a PDP-1. ([Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Logo_%28programming_language%29?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

But its conceptual breakthrough is turtle geometry.

```logo
FORWARD 100
RIGHT 90
FORWARD 100
```

You don't primarily tell the computer:

> put a line between Cartesian coordinate `(0,0)` and `(100,0)`.

You tell an entity:

> **go forward; turn; go forward.**

The description adopts the turtle's situated point of view.

That is extremely close to your larger problem. Logo makes **deixis-like embodied orientation computational**:

**here**  
**forward**  
**left**  
**right**

The program is partially a description of **what an actor should do from where it presently is**.

---

### Then Put-That-There radicalizes Logo

This is the extraordinary sentence in Schmandt's January 1980 thesis:

> the project explored “natural paths of human communication as the languages of computer interface.”

The system joined a NEC DP-100 connected-speech recognizer to gesture tracking and graphics. ([MIT Media Lab](https://www.media.mit.edu/speech/papers/1980/schmandt_thesis1980_three-dimensional_input.pdf))

And the original SIGGRAPH paper describes something astonishingly close to function application.

The person says:

> **Create a blue square there.**

Bolt explains that the utterance effectively invokes a **Create routine** expecting parameters. The routine remains waiting until the spoken word **“there”** occurs; at that instant, the system takes the x-y coordinates supplied by the pointing gesture. ([MIT Media Lab](https://www.media.mit.edu/speech/papers/1980/bolt_SIGGRAPH80_put-that-there.pdf))

So schematically:

```text
CREATE(
    shape = square,
    color = blue,
    position = THERE
)
```

except:

```text
THERE := where the hand points when "there" is spoken
```

And:

```text
THAT := object intersected by the pointing gesture
```

That's not metaphorical. Bolt explicitly calls pronouns effectively **“temporary variables,”** and describes “that” as ostensively defined by what is being pointed at. ([MIT Media Lab](https://www.media.mit.edu/speech/papers/1980/bolt_SIGGRAPH80_put-that-there.pdf))

This gives you a killer sequence:

> **Logo makes orientation executable.  
> Put-That-There makes deixis executable.  
> Scratch makes syntax manipulable.  
> p5.js makes programming sketchable.  
> Generative AI makes description executable.**

## And yes: what was Put-That-There actually coded in?

I dug into the primary documents because the answer is more interesting than simply “Lisp.”

**The best-supported answer is: the host-side system was running on an Interdata 7/32 and was substantially programmed in PL/I, with additional low-level software running on a Nova minicomputer.**

Schmandt's thesis gives us the hardware architecture directly. The Polhemus ROPAMS tracker performed computation through a **Nova minicomputer**, which communicated over a parallel interface to the lab's **Interdata 7/32 host**. The Interdata then drove the **Ramtek 9300 frame buffer** and communicated separately with the NEC DP-100 speech recognizer. ([MIT Media Lab](https://www.media.mit.edu/speech/papers/1980/schmandt_thesis1980_three-dimensional_input.pdf))

And we have unusually good evidence for **PL/I**.

Schmandt later recalled that he entered the Architecture Machine Group in 1977 after seeing a job advertisement specifically for:

> “graphics programming in PL/I”

He had already learned PL/I and went on to program the group's Model 85 framebuffer. ([MIT Media Lab](https://www.media.mit.edu/articles/the-geek/))

More importantly, his actual 1980 thesis prints pieces of the Interdata-side driver. They look like this:

```text
dcl 1 pol-data based (pol-ptr),
    2 psys(2),
        3 az float,
        3 el float,
        3 roll float,
        3 x fix,
        3 y fix,
        3 z fix,
```

That is **PL/I-family declaration syntax**. The appendix identifies this explicitly as the **Interdata Driver Software** that interpreted the raw Polhemus data. ([MIT Media Lab](https://www.media.mit.edu/speech/papers/1980/schmandt_thesis1980_three-dimensional_input.pdf))

Meanwhile, the Nova side was much lower level. Schmandt gives the bootstrap that had to be **toggled into the Nova's front panel**:

```text
DOB, 0, 26
NIOS, 26
NIOP, 26
SKPBZ, 26
JMP, -.1
HALT
```

The Interdata then downloaded the ROPAMS **core image** into the Nova. ([MIT Media Lab](https://www.media.mit.edu/speech/papers/1980/schmandt_thesis1980_three-dimensional_input.pdf))

So I wouldn't write:

> “Put-That-There was written in PL/I.”

as an absolute statement.

I'd write:

> **Put-That-There was a heterogeneous 1979 system centered on an Interdata 7/32 host. Its surviving host-side driver code is PL/I, while a Nova minicomputer ran lower-level code for the Polhemus spatial tracker; dedicated NEC hardware performed connected-speech recognition.**

That is both more accurate and much more interesting.

### Scratch then makes another move

Scratch wasn't merely “Logo with colorful blocks.” Resnick explicitly says Logo and Papert were major inspirations, alongside Squeak Etoys, but Scratch tried to make programming much more **tinkerable**. ([MIT Open Learning](https://openlearning.mit.edu/news/10-sparks-lit-flame-scratch?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

Instead of having to remember:

```text
repeat 10 [
  forward 10
]
```

you manipulate syntax as **things**.

Blocks fit or don't fit.

The grammar becomes perceptible through shape.

MIT described the blocks as snapping together like LEGO, removing much conventional punctuation and syntax. ([MIT News](https://news.mit.edu/2007/resnick-scratch?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

And wonderfully, **the original Scratch itself was implemented in Squeak**, an implementation of Smalltalk-80. The early MIT proposal states this explicitly. ([MIT Media Lab](https://web.media.mit.edu/~mres/papers/scratch-proposal.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

So:

> Logo: **write the operation**  
> Scratch: **handle the operation**

---

### And p5.js makes code a sketch

Lauren Lee McCarthy created p5.js in 2013 as a new interpretation of Processing for the web. It is a JavaScript library rather than a separate language in the strong sense. ([p5.js](https://p5js.org/people?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

Its conceptual unit isn't really “software application.”

It's the **sketch**.

```javascript
function setup() {
  createCanvas(400, 400);
}

function draw() {
  circle(mouseX, mouseY, 40);
}
```

The Processing Foundation describes the project as bringing Processing's philosophy to JavaScript and the browser; p5 itself describes its aim as making sketching with code feel like sketching in a notebook. ([Processing Foundation](https://www.processingfoundation.org/about/history/?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

That's another representational shift:

**program → sketch**

and therefore:

**compile → try**  
**specify → make**  
**finish → iterate**

---

And now the historical line gets extremely clean:

**1967 - Logo**  
*Tell an actor what to do.*

↓  

**1979 - Put-That-There**  
*Tell the computer what to do in ordinary language; let the world complete the reference.*

↓  

**2001 - Shakespeare / 1991 Piet**  
*Ask what other cultural forms can carry computation.*

↓  

**2007 - Scratch**  
*Turn program grammar into objects you manipulate.*

↓  

**2013 - p5.js**  
*Turn programming into sketching.*

↓  

**2020s - multimodal prompting**  
*Describe, point, show, speak, revise-and allow the machine to construct the operational representation.*

Which produces a stronger historical proposition than “natural language is the new programming language”:

> **The history of programming interfaces can be read as a progressive search for operational descriptions closer to the forms humans already use to think and act: movement, pointing, speech, blocks, sketches, pictures, music, and ordinary language.**

And **1979 is the hinge** because Put-That-There already demonstrates that a command need not contain all of its own meaning. **Meaning can be completed by the situation.** That is the connection to prompting I would put at the center.

I’m checking the ad and any primary-source notes around it, because the interesting question is whether the “language” is literal source code, a multimodal prompt layer, or a demo grammar built on top of a model.

The ad is making a very deliberate historical claim. It opens with the old MIT grammar of **“create a yellow circle there”** and then jumps to Astra, where the same kind of utterance no longer needs a hand-built command grammar. The model infers the operation from language, screen state, and the tools it can act through.

> **Interpreting the ad’s language**
>
> I’m distinguishing the model’s undisclosed implementation from the interaction shown. The yellow-circle scene echoes *Put That There*: natural language, visual references, and gestures form a situated command language, translated into structured computer actions.

>
> **Clarified the programming layer**
>

>

Yes. The Astra ad is doing something much more precise than “AI can use a computer.” OpenAI explicitly credits the archival **Put-That-There** clip, and the modern sequence deliberately echoes it: the old system gets “Create a yellow circle there,” then Astra receives ordinary speech such as “Can you draw me a small yellow circle?” followed by “take this and make it the window of a rocket ship.” ([linkedin.com](https://www.linkedin.com/posts/openai_this-gpt-6-astra-activity-7501364499304579072-dm6F?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

If by **“what is it written in?”** you mean Astra itself, OpenAI has **not published a conventional implementation language** like “Astra is written in Python” or C++. The public model identifier is `gpt-6-astra`, and developers access it through the Responses API from ordinary host languages such as JavaScript or Python. More interestingly, OpenAI exposes a **computer-use tool** whose machine-facing vocabulary consists of structured actions: clicks, drags, keyboard operations, screenshots, and related actions. The model receives multimodal input and can emit computer tool calls; after execution, screenshots come back into the loop. ([OpenAI](https://openai.com/index/gpt-6-astra/?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

So the yellow-circle interaction really has **two languages layered on top of each other**:

```text
HUMAN LANGUAGE
"Can you draw me a small yellow circle?"
"Take this and make it the window of a rocket ship."
"Make it more detailed."
"Now make it a 3D model in Blender."

            ↓ interpretation

ACTION LANGUAGE
observe screen
select/open application
click(...)
drag(...)
type(...)
inspect screenshot
decide next action
...
```

OpenAI's API documentation literally describes computer use as typed computer actions plus screenshots returned to the model. ([OpenAI Developers](https://developers.openai.com/api/reference/cli/resources/beta/subresources/responses?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

That makes Astra quite different from p5.js, Scratch, or Logo. **The user doesn't write the lower-level action language.** The model writes it for them.

The fascinating thing is the **pronouns**.

In Bolt's 1980 description of Put-That-There, the system treats things like **“that”** and **“there”** almost like temporary variables. Gesture supplies their values:

```text
"Put THAT THERE."

THAT  = object currently indicated
THERE = position currently indicated
PUT   = transformation
```

Bolt explicitly discusses the power of replacing a verbose description such as “the object to the right of the green square” with the deictic `there`, whose referent is supplied by the concurrent gesture. ([WorryDream](https://worrydream.com/refs/Bolt_1980_-_Put_That_There.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

Astra generalizes this enormously.

In the advertisement:

```text
"Take THIS and make IT the window of a rocket ship."
```

There isn't a declared variable:

```javascript
let this = yellowCircle;
```

Yet `THIS` has a value.

The value comes from the **shared state of the interaction**:

```text
THIS :=
    the artifact we just made
    + the thing currently visible
    + the object under discussion
```

Then:

```text
IT := THIS
```

And:

```text
MAKE IT THE WINDOW OF A ROCKET SHIP
```

is effectively a transformation over that object.

That is why I think calling this merely **natural-language programming** actually misses the most interesting part. The operative language is something closer to **situated language** or **operational deixis**.

Its primitive vocabulary isn't:

```text
if
for
while
function
class
```

It increasingly looks like:

```text
this
that
there
here
make
move
use
keep
change
more
less
like this
same thing
again
```

Those words are terrible programming-language keywords in the classical sense because their meanings are incomplete in isolation.

And that is exactly their strength.

`there` doesn't mean anything without a situation.

`this` doesn't mean anything without history.

`more detailed` doesn't specify an algorithm.

`make it a window` doesn't specify the representation transformation.

A conventional programming language tries to make the program contain enough information that execution can proceed deterministically.

Astra's interface allows the instruction to be **deliberately incomplete because the environment supplies the remainder**.

The comparison with 1979 is therefore extremely clean:

| 1979 Put-That-There | 2026 Astra |
|---|---|
| fixed graphical microworld | arbitrary desktop applications |
| limited recognized vocabulary | open-ended natural language |
| speech | speech/text |
| pointing gesture | visual + conversational state |
| `that` → pointed object | `this` → contextually resolved artifact |
| `there` → gesture coordinates | location/target → interpreted from current screen/context |
| hand-coded command routines | model-generated tool/action sequences |
| execute once | observe → act → observe → revise |

Put-That-There essentially did:

```text
UTTERANCE
     ↓
PARSE
     ↓
COMMAND + SLOTS
     ↓
GESTURE FILLS MISSING SLOT
     ↓
GRAPHICS ROUTINE
```

Astra is closer to:

```text
UTTERANCE
   +
CONVERSATION
   +
CURRENT SCREEN
   +
AVAILABLE TOOLS
        ↓
     MODEL
        ↓
ACTION
        ↓
CHANGED WORLD
        ↓
NEW SCREEN
        ↺
```

That last loop is crucial. **It is less like compiling a program and more like continuously interpreting an operational description against a changing world.**

And that suddenly connects your whole lineage:

**Logo:** `FORWARD 50`  
The command gets meaning from the turtle's present position.

**Put-That-There:** `PUT THAT THERE`  
The command gets meaning from gesture and scene.

**Scratch:** drag this block here.  
Meaning emerges partly from spatial connection.

**p5.js:** `circle(mouseX, mouseY, 50)`  
Live state enters the program continuously.

**Astra:** `take this and make it the window of a rocket ship.`  
Language refers directly to a persistent object in a changing computational situation.

So I think the stronger name for what you're seeing is not **prompt language** exactly.

It is a transition from:

> **programming languages whose expressions denote operations**

toward:

> **an operational language whose expressions acquire meaning from a shared computational situation.**

And the Astra ad seems consciously constructed to make precisely that historical claim. OpenAI says Astra is designed for computer use and describes it as maintaining orientation as tasks evolve, incorporating steering instructions without losing the larger goal. ([OpenAI](https://openai.com/index/gpt-6-astra/?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

The yellow circle is therefore a very good choice. It isn't really about drawing a circle. **The circle persists across sentences, intentions, applications, and representations.** First it is a circle. Then **this**. Then **it**. Then a window. Then part of a rocket. Then part of a more detailed image. Then the image becomes a 3D object in Blender.

That is almost a demonstration of a new programming primitive:

```text
<artifact>
    persists

<representation>
    changes

<description>
    changes

<operation>
    follows the description
```

The program is no longer just the sentence.

**The program is the sentence plus the remembered world in which the sentence makes sense.**

That is where the Put-That-There reference gets genuinely consequential rather than nostalgic.

---

**Sources:**

- [GPT-6 Astra: A new generation of intelligence | OpenAI](https://openai.com/index/gpt-6-astra/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)
- [Responses | OpenAI API Reference](https://developers.openai.com/api/reference/cli/resources/beta/subresources/responses?utm_source=chatgpt.com)
- ["Put-That-There":](https://worrydream.com/refs/Bolt_1980_-_Put_That_There.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

Yes. **That is exactly the hard case for an operational audit.** Saying “the prompt drew a yellow circle” collapses several distinct programs into one apparent act.

In the Astra demonstration, the visible application appears to be a conventional paint program, very likely Microsoft Paint. But I cannot find an OpenAI or Microsoft source that identifies the exact Paint build used in that shot, and Microsoft does not publish Paint’s current source code, so I would **not** claim “the circle is ultimately written in C++” or any other implementation language. Microsoft publicly describes the redesigned Windows 11 Paint app, but not its complete implementation stack. ([Windows Blog](https://blogs.windows.com/windows-insider/2022/05/10/reimagining-the-apps-included-with-windows-11/?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

What we *can* audit is the chain.

```text
"Draw me a small yellow circle."
                │
                ▼
        GPT-6 Astra
  interprets desired state
                │
                ▼
 COMPUTER-USE ACTION LANGUAGE
  click / drag / key / screenshot...
                │
                ▼
       COMPUTER HARNESS
 turns those actions into input
                │
                ▼
       OPERATING SYSTEM
 pointer position, buttons, events
                │
                ▼
          PAINT GUI
 tool selection + color + canvas
                │
                ▼
      PAINT'S PROGRAM LOGIC
 interpret gesture as ellipse/stroke
                │
                ▼
       GRAPHICS / RASTERIZER
 determine affected pixel values
                │
                ▼
        FRAMEBUFFER / IMAGE
           yellow pixels
```

OpenAI actually exposes part of that middle layer. Its computer-use API represents actions as typed structures such as clicks, double-clicks and drags, and sends screenshots back to the model. So there really is an intermediate **machine-readable action language** between the English utterance and Paint. ([OpenAI Developers](https://developers.openai.com/api/reference/cli/resources/beta/subresources/responses?utm_source=chatgpt.com)) Astra is explicitly trained for workflows across professional software and computer use. ([OpenAI Developers](https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/guides/latest-model?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

Suppose Astra decides to use Paint's ellipse tool. Conceptually, its transient program could look something like:

```text
observe()

click(x=ellipse_tool_x, y=ellipse_tool_y)

click(x=yellow_swatch_x, y=yellow_swatch_y)

drag([
    (410, 260),
    ...
    (480, 330)
])

observe()
```

That is **not Paint source code**. It is a program *for operating Paint*.

Then Paint receives something more like:

```text
pointer-down at (410,260)
pointer-move ...
pointer-up at (480,330)
```

Paint's own already-written program interprets that sequence according to hidden state:

```text
currentTool  = ellipse
currentColor = yellow
start        = (410,260)
end          = (480,330)
```

and somewhere inside the application logic this becomes the equivalent of:

```text
ellipse(bounds, yellow)
```

which eventually becomes something resembling:

```text
for each pixel p inside shape:
    p := yellow
```

The actual implementation may be radically more complicated. But **that is the representational descent**.

And this is where the Astra/Put-That-There comparison gets much more interesting.

In Put-That-There, you could almost write down the whole translation:

```text
"Create a yellow circle there"

CREATE
    object = CIRCLE
    color  = YELLOW
    x,y    = pointing coordinates
```

The researchers had designed the microworld and therefore knew what `CREATE`, `CIRCLE`, `YELLOW`, and `THERE` meant operationally.

With Astra:

```text
"Draw me a small yellow circle."
```

does **not** necessarily translate to:

```text
DRAW_CIRCLE(yellow, small)
```

Instead Astra might translate it to:

```text
open Paint
find Shapes
find ellipse
choose yellow
move pointer
drag
inspect result
correct if necessary
```

So the astonishing thing is that we have gone **back up a level of abstraction**.

The computer already contains a program capable of making circles.

Paint itself contains something like:

> **circle-making machinery**

But Astra does not necessarily invoke that machinery directly.

It invokes:

> **the human interface to the circle-making machinery.**

That means your phrase is exactly right:

> **code controls a mouse that controls a GUI that controls code that produces the circle.**

And underneath that code is still more code.

```text
ENGLISH
    ↓
model policy
    ↓
computer-action protocol
    ↓
agent harness
    ↓
OS input API
    ↓
GUI event system
    ↓
Paint interaction state machine
    ↓
graphics operation
    ↓
graphics API
    ↓
driver
    ↓
GPU / CPU instructions
    ↓
memory values
    ↓
display pixels
```

There is no single answer to **“what programming language made the yellow circle?”**

At different depths:

**English made it.**

**Astra's action representation made it.**

**The computer-use harness made it.**

**Paint made it.**

**Paint's implementation language made it.**

**Windows graphics code made it.**

**machine instructions made it.**

All are defensible descriptions at different levels.

That exposes what I think should become a central problem for your **operational audit**:

> **At what level do we stop following the description?**

Because if we stop at:

```text
prompt → yellow circle
```

we have learned almost nothing about the operation.

If we stop at:

```text
prompt → mouse gestures → yellow circle
```

we still haven't explained why those gestures produce one.

If we stop at:

```text
prompt → gestures → Paint tool → ellipse operation
```

we haven't explained how the ellipse becomes pixels.

And if we insist on continuing until transistor state changes, the audit becomes useless.

So an operational audit needs a principled stopping rule.

I think the useful unit is the **representation boundary**. Every time one description gets translated into another executable description, record it:

```text
"yellow circle"
        ↓
[goal representation]

"operate Paint"
        ↓
[action representation]

drag from A to B
        ↓
[input representation]

ellipse bounded by A,B
        ↓
[graphics representation]

pixel mask + yellow color
        ↓
[raster representation]
```

That gives you something stronger than a conventional provenance log. You're not merely asking **what happened?**

You're asking:

> **What did the operation become at each layer?**

And then the yellow circle becomes an almost perfect small experimental object because its semantic identity survives every transformation:

```text
YELLOW CIRCLE

spoken intention
↓
model goal
↓
tool actions
↓
pointer trajectory
↓
GUI command
↓
geometric object
↓
pixel region
↓
encoded image
```

The **artifact persists while its operational description changes**.

That is the thing the Astra ad accidentally makes spectacularly visible. The user says perhaps four words. Under those four words sits an enormous stack of older programming languages, graphical systems, device protocols, APIs, state machines, rendering systems, and machine instructions.

So I would make the operational-audit wager sharper:

> **A prompt does not replace programming. It recruits programs.**

Or stronger still:

> **The apparent simplicity of prompting is purchased by hiding an expanding stack of operational translations beneath the sentence.**

Astra's yellow circle is therefore almost the inverse of Piet. Piet makes the program visible **as the picture**. Astra makes an enormous programmatic process disappear **behind the picture**.

---

Exactly. This gives you an unusually clean experiment:

# **THE YELLOW CIRCLE**
### One artifact. Many operational descriptions.

Do **not** compare which system makes the prettiest circle. Fix the circle as tightly as possible and ask:

> **How many fundamentally different descriptions can become this same yellow circle?**

Then preserve the entire descent from utterance to pixels.

I would use two invariants. The **semantic invariant** is simply “a yellow circle there.” The **rendering invariant** can be something brutally specific, such as a 256×256 white field, filled `#FFD400` circle, 96 px diameter, center `(128,128)`, no stroke. If we reconstruct the archival Put-That-There frame, we can replace those arbitrary dimensions with measurements from the original.

Then every run produces both the circle and its **operational trace**.

## 1. Direct graphical languages

These should make the circle through their own native representational machinery.

| Route | Operational description |
|---|---|
| **Logo** | turtle movement creates circumference/fill |
| **Scratch** | blocks + Pen extension draw it |
| **p5.js** | `circle(128,128,96)` |
| **Processing** | `ellipse(...)` |
| **JavaScript Canvas** | `ctx.arc(...)` |
| **SVG** | `<circle cx="128" ...>` |
| **HTML/CSS** | square `div` + `border-radius:50%` |
| **Python Turtle** | turtle drawing operation |
| **Python/Pillow** | raster ellipse primitive |
| **OpenSCAD** | `circle()` rendered/exported |
| **PostScript** | `arc` + `fill` |
| **TikZ** | geometric drawing instruction |
| **GLSL** | every fragment asks whether it lies inside radius |
| **raw PPM** | program literally emits the pixels |

Already these aren't equivalent descriptions.

Compare:

```javascript
circle(128, 128, 96);
```

with:

```svg
<circle cx="128" cy="128" r="48" fill="#FFD400"/>
```

with a shader:

```text
for this pixel:
    distance(pixel, center) < radius ?
        yellow
        white
```

The artifact stays constant while **what "circle" means computationally changes**.

## 2. The languages from the esolang forage

This is where it gets delightful.

**Emojicode** can calculate the geometry or simply emit SVG.

**Shakespeare Programming Language** can produce an SVG or PPM file, except the description of those bytes/numbers has to be staged as characters speaking to one another.

**Piet** can compute the image by navigating color regions. The program that produces the yellow circle is itself an image.

**Whitespace** can emit the SVG/PPM while its visible source appears empty.

**Chef** can make ingredients and cooking operations eventually produce the textual image representation.

**Beatnik** can produce it through word choices whose Scrabble values encode the computation.

**Rockstar** can produce it from something resembling lyrics.

**Befunge** can generate it by moving through a two-dimensional instruction field.

**Folders** can encode the program in directory structure and eventually emit the circle representation.

This creates a particularly useful distinction:

> **native circle** versus **translated circle**.

P5 has a native concept approximately corresponding to `circle`.

Shakespeare doesn't.

So Shakespeare might produce:

```text
Shakespeare

numbers/characters
    ↓
SVG
    ↓
browser SVG renderer
    ↓
circle
```

That extra bridge is **not contamination**. It is data.

```text
CIRCLE slot

THERE waits for deixis

pointing coordinates
```

or a shell-script/accessibility route.

That's a separate family.

```text
tools:
    mouse_move
    mouse_down
    mouse_up
    screenshot
```

Another has:

```text

    shell
```

Another has:

```text

    browser
```

Another has no tools but can return an image.

The sentence is identical.

Its operational meaning changes because **the available verbs change**.

That is huge.

The actual executable object is more accurately:

```text
PROMPT
+
MODEL
+
SYSTEM INSTRUCTIONS
+
TOOL VOCABULARY
+
CURRENT WORLD STATE
+
HISTORY
+
EXECUTION LOOP
```

not:

```text

```

```text
YELLOW CIRCLE / YC-037

Human description:
    "Create a yellow circle there."

Model:
    GPT-X

Harness:
    computer-use

Available operations:
    screenshot
    click
    drag
    type

Environment:
    Microsoft Paint

Intermediate representations:
    natural language
    inferred goal
    screen coordinates
    pointer events
    GUI state
    ellipse representation
    raster pixels

Final artifact:
    circle.png

Corrections:
    2

Human interventions:
    0
```

## And measure the **operational distance**

This may be the most useful quantitative concept in the whole experiment.

Consider:

```text
SVG:
yellow circle
→ <circle>
→ rasterizer
→ pixels
```

versus:

```text
Astra:
yellow circle
→ model interpretation
→ screenshot
→ locate Paint control
→ mouse
→ tool state
→ drag
→ Paint geometry
→ graphics API
→ pixels
```

The second doesn't necessarily have a poorer language.

It has a **longer operational path**.

Measure at least:

**number of representational translations**  
**number of intermediary programs recruited**  
**number of hidden state dependencies**  
**number of actions**  
**number of feedback cycles**  
**number of human corrections**  
**amount of description supplied by human**  
**amount inferred by machine**  
**pixel error from target**  
**semantic success/failure**

And one metric I think is especially yours:

### **Description ratio**

```text
human description supplied
──────────────────────────
operational specification required
```

Put-That-There:

```text
"yellow circle there" + point
```

contains astonishingly little compared with everything required to actually illuminate those pixels.

A raw PPM program contains almost everything.

Astra contains almost nothing explicitly but recruits an enormous stack.

That gives you a continuum from:

> **description carries operation**

to

> **environment carries operation**

to

> **model reconstructs operation**

And there is a beautiful final test.

After every successful run, ask:

> **Where, exactly, is the circle?**

In Logo it might be implicit in a turtle trajectory.

In SVG it exists explicitly as a `<circle>`.

In CSS there is **no circle at all**, only a rectangular box with a 50% border radius.

In a shader it is an inequality.

In Paint it may just be colored pixels after rasterization.

In an agent's reasoning it first exists as an intention.

In Put-That-There it temporarily exists as the combination of **noun + adjective + pointing event**.

In Piet the program that makes the circle is itself an image.

That is the experiment.

Not **“can all these systems draw a circle?”**

But:

> **How many different things can “make a yellow circle there” mean operationally while producing an artifact we recognize as the same thing?**

I would literally call the corpus **YELLOW CIRCLE / PUT THAT THERE** and make the original 1979 interaction specimen `YC-000`. Every subsequent circle is another operational lineage descended from that utterance.

## 1. <Initial Interpretation>

The task is not “draw a yellow circle in thirty languages.”

The <program theory problem> is:

> Hold one <artifact> constant while changing the <operational description> capable of bringing it into existence.

Our canonical object is:

```text
<YELLOW-CIRCLE> :=
{
  <field>      = 256 × 256
  <background> = white
  <center>     = (128,128)
  <radius>     = 48
  <fill>       = #FFD400
  <stroke>     = none
}
```

And the canonical human instruction is:

```text
"PUT A YELLOW CIRCLE THERE."
```

where:

```text
<THERE> := (128,128)
```

The real-world activity being modeled is therefore not merely <drawing>. It is:

```text
<intention>
    [is expressed through]
<representation A>
    [is interpreted as]
<operation>
    [changes]
<world>
    [until it contains]
<YELLOW-CIRCLE>
```

The experiment asks what every programming system must believe a <circle>, <yellow>, <there>, and [put] are.

---

# 2. <Theory Skeleton>

### <entities>

```text

<circle>
<yellow>
<there>
<canvas>
<center>
<radius>
<geometry>
<pixel>
<program-text>
<interpreter>
<renderer>
<GUI>
<pointer>
<model>
<harness>
<artifact>
```

### [operations]

```text
[refer]
[locate]
[construct]
[draw]
[fill]
[rasterize]
[emit]
[interpret]
[compile]
[move]
[click]
[drag]
[serialize]
[delegate]
[observe]
[correct]
```

### <states>

```text
<absent>
<described>
<represented>
<executing>
<geometric>
<rasterized>
<visible>
<verified>
```

### <constraints>

Every implementation must eventually produce the same canonical visual target.

The path may differ radically.

### <invariants>

The crucial invariant is **not source code**.

It is:

```text
<artifact-identity> = YELLOW-CIRCLE
```

while:

```text
<representation>
<syntax>
<execution-model>
<intermediary-programs>
<degree-of-deixis>
<degree-of-inference>
```

may all change.

# 3. <Assumption Ledger>

**<safe>** We can use SVG as a common serialization target for languages that have text output but no graphics primitives.

**<safe>** A language that emits SVG has still produced the circle, but indirectly:

```text
<language> → <SVG description> → <SVG renderer> → <pixels>
```

**<safe>** This indirection should be recorded rather than treated as a defect.

**<uncertain>** Different Logo, Processing, Piet, Chef, Shakespeare, etc. implementations have dialect differences.

**<uncertain>** Some historical/esoteric systems do not have practical modern graphics libraries.

**<requires-user-decision>** Eventually we should decide whether exact pixel identity is required or whether perceptual equivalence counts.

For now I would make **both**:

```text
<semantic-equivalence>
AND
<pixel-distance>
```

separate measurements.

---

# 4. <Operational Description>

There are at least **six different theories of the yellow circle** hiding in these languages.

### {A. primitive theory}

The language already knows a <circle>.

```text
<circle parameters>
    [invoke]
<circle primitive>
    [produces]
<geometry>
```

Examples: p5.js, Processing, SVG, OpenSCAD.

### {B. constructive theory}

There is no circle object. A circle is something reconstructed through motion or mathematics.

```text
<rule>
    [generates]
<trajectory / pixel predicate>
    [appears as]
<circle>
```

Examples: Logo turtle, GLSL.

### {C. styling theory}

A circle is actually some other object under a transformation.

```text
<square>
    [round corners maximally]
<circle>
```

HTML/CSS is beautiful here.

### {D. serialization theory}

The language does not understand circles whatsoever.

It understands characters or numbers.

```text
<program>
    [emits]
"<circle ...>"
    [is interpreted by]
<SVG renderer>
```

Examples: Shakespeare, Emojicode, Whitespace, Chef, Beatnik, Rockstar, LOLCODE, ArnoldC, Ook!.

### {E. spatial-program theory}

The organization of the program itself carries execution.

```text
<space / image / topology>
    [guides execution]
<byte output or drawing>
```

Examples: Piet, Befunge, Hexagony, Cubix, AsciiDots, Folders.

Befunge is explicitly a two-dimensional instruction field; Folders makes directory nesting itself the program; Shakespeare makes characters into variables and dialogue into operations; Whitespace makes only spaces, tabs and newlines significant. ([Esolangs](https://esolangs.org/wiki/Folders?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

### {F. situated-operation theory}

The program cannot be understood without the current world.

```text
"PUT THAT THERE"
+
<scene>
+
<gesture>
=
<operation>
```

Put-That-There and Astra belong here.

---

# 5. <Failure Description>

There are several different failures, and they must not collapse into one `FAIL`.

```text
<parse-failure>
    program cannot be understood

<execution-failure>
    program is valid but does not terminate correctly

<bridge-failure>
    program successfully emits SVG but renderer fails

<reference-failure>
    THERE cannot be resolved

<geometry-failure>
    circle is misplaced / wrong radius

<appearance-failure>
    geometry correct, color wrong

<operational-failure>
    final artifact succeeds but through an unintended route

<provenance-failure>
    circle exists but we cannot reconstruct how
```

That last one is central to the audit.

A successful circle with no recoverable operational trace is still an experimental failure.

---

### Change scenario A: “Put it over there.”

Change:

```text

```

Languages with explicit coordinates should require a parameter change.

Logo may require a positional movement.

Put-That-There changes only the gesture.

Astra may change nothing in the utterance at all because <there> comes from situated context.

That difference is experimentally valuable.

### Change scenario B: “Make it twice as large.”

Change:

```text
<radius> := 96
```

In SVG this means changing one scalar.

In CSS, width and height change.

In turtle Logo, path geometry changes.

In a raw raster loop, the inequality changes.

In Paint-through-Astra, the model must infer an entirely new drag trajectory.

If a theory survives this modification cleanly, it has captured <circle> rather than merely memorized one drawing.

Call that `<S>`.

# 8. <Program Text>

## YC-001 - Logo

### <theory-of-the-program>

For Logo, a <circle> is fundamentally **motion relative to an embodied agent**.

```text
<turtle>
    [moves]
<small-distance>
    [turns]
<small-angle>
    [repeats]
<closed-path>
```

`THERE` is not a screen pixel so much as a place the turtle can [go-to].

The circle is therefore a **history of motion**.

Conceptually:

```logo
penup
setxy 128 80
pendown
repeat 360 [
  forward 0.8378
  right 1
]
```

The important theoretical fact is:

```text
<circle> != primitive object
<circle> = accumulated turning
```

---

## YC-002 - Scratch

### <theory-of-the-program>

Scratch treats the program itself as **manipulable objects**.

The circle can retain Logo's turtle theory:

```text
<sprite>
    [moves]
    [turns]
    [leaves pen trace]
```

Scratch-block representation:

```text
erase all
go to x: (128) y: (80)
pen down
repeat (360)
    move (0.8378) steps
    turn cw (1) degrees
end
pen up
```

Here both program and picture are spatial constructions.

---

## YC-003 - p5.js

### <theory-of-the-program>

p5.js already contains a <circle> concept.

```text
<circle>
    = <center> + <diameter>
```

Program:

```javascript
function setup() {
  createCanvas(256, 256);
  background(255);
  noStroke();
  fill('#FFD400');
  circle(128, 128, 96);
}
```

Operational path:

```text
circle(...)
→ p5 graphics abstraction
→ Canvas
→ browser graphics
→ pixels
```

---

## YC-004 - Processing

Same ontology as p5.js, but the sketch executes in Processing's environment.

```java
void setup() {
  size(256, 256);
  background(255);
  noStroke();
  fill(255, 212, 0);
  circle(128, 128, 96);
}
```

Theory:

```text
<circle> = native drawing command
```

---

## YC-005 - JavaScript Canvas

Canvas knows paths rather than semantic objects that persist as circles.

```javascript
const canvas = document.querySelector("canvas");
const ctx = canvas.getContext("2d");

ctx.fillStyle = "#FFFFFF";
ctx.fillRect(0, 0, 256, 256);

ctx.beginPath();
ctx.arc(128, 128, 48, 0, Math.PI * 2);
ctx.fillStyle = "#FFD400";
ctx.fill();
```

Theory:

```text
<circle>
    [becomes]
<closed path>
    [becomes]
<filled raster region>
```

---

## YC-006 - SVG

SVG gives us perhaps the cleanest **declarative** circle.

```xml
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg"
     width="256" height="256">
  <rect width="256" height="256" fill="white"/>
  <circle cx="128" cy="128" r="48" fill="#FFD400"/>
</svg>
```

Its theory is almost literally:

```text
THERE = (128,128)
WHAT  = circle
SIZE  = radius 48
COLOR = yellow
```

No drawing gesture is represented.

The source declares that the circle **is there**.

---

## YC-007 - HTML/CSS

This one is conceptually excellent.

There is no circle.

There is a box.

```html
<div class="circle"></div>

<style>
html, body {
  margin: 0;
}

body {
  width: 256px;
  height: 256px;
  position: relative;
  background: #fff;
}

.circle {
  position: absolute;
  width: 96px;
  height: 96px;
  left: 80px;
  top: 80px;
  border-radius: 50%;
  background: #FFD400;
}
</style>
```

Theory:

```text
<rectangle>
    [has boundary curvature transformed]
<apparently circular region>
```

So:

> CSS does not necessarily put a circle there. It puts a sufficiently rounded box there.

That distinction belongs in the audit.

---

## YC-008 - Python Turtle

```python
import turtle

t = turtle.Turtle()
t.speed(0)
t.penup()
t.goto(128, 80)
t.setheading(0)
t.color("#FFD400")
t.begin_fill()
t.pendown()
t.circle(48)
t.end_fill()
turtle.done()
```

Theory:

```text
<circle> = constrained movement of <turtle>
```

But unlike primitive Logo movement, the library now packages that motion into `circle(48)`.

This specimen reveals an abstraction layer **inside the same metaphor**.

---

## YC-009 - Pillow

Pillow's world is already raster space.

```python
from PIL import Image, ImageDraw

image = Image.new("RGB", (256, 256), "#FFFFFF")
draw = ImageDraw.Draw(image)
draw.ellipse((80, 80, 176, 176), fill="#FFD400")
image.save("yellow-circle.png")
```

Theory:

```text
<circle>
    = <ellipse inscribed inside bounding rectangle>
```

Interesting already: the programmer supplies a **box**, not center-radius geometry.

---

## YC-010 - OpenSCAD

```scad
color("#FFD400")
translate([128,128,0])
circle(r=48, $fn=360);
```

Theory:

```text
<circle> = geometric model
```

The program does not intrinsically mean pixels.

Rendering/export subsequently translates geometric space into an image.

---

## YC-011 - PostScript

```postscript
%!PS
1 1 1 setrgbcolor
0 0 256 256 rectfill

1 0.8314 0 setrgbcolor
newpath
128 128 48 0 360 arc
closepath
fill
showpage
```

Theory:

```text
<graphics-state>
+
<path construction>
+
[fill]
```

A circle is an instruction in a page-description machine.

---

## YC-012 - TikZ

```latex
\begin{tikzpicture}[x=1pt,y=1pt]
  \fill[white] (0,0) rectangle (256,256);
  \fill[yellow] (128,128) circle[radius=48];
\end{tikzpicture}
```

Theory:

```text
<circle-description>
    [passes through]
<TeX/TikZ>
    [produces]
<vector/page representation>
```

---

## YC-013 - GLSL

This is one of the best theoretical contrasts.

There is **no circle object and no circle path**.

Each pixel independently answers a proposition:

```glsl
vec2 p = gl_FragCoord.xy;
vec2 c = vec2(128.0, 128.0);

float inside = step(length(p - c), 48.0);

vec3 white  = vec3(1.0);
vec3 yellow = vec3(1.0, 212.0/255.0, 0.0);

gl_FragColor = vec4(mix(white, yellow, inside), 1.0);
```

Theory:

```text
<pixel>
    [asks]
distance(pixel, THERE) <= radius ?
```

The circle is a **truth condition**.

That one deserves a major place in the experiment.

---

## YC-014 - Raw PPM

Raw raster theory abolishes the circle entirely.

For every coordinate:

```text
if (x-128)² + (y-128)² <= 48²
    pixel = (255,212,0)
else
    pixel = (255,255,255)
```

Theory:

```text
<circle> = set of pixels satisfying an inequality
```

The final file knows nothing about circles.

Only colored cells remain.

---

# THE EMITTER LANGUAGES

Here the common theory changes.

They do not need graphics at all.

Their job is:

```text
<program>
    [emit]
<S>
```

Then:

```text
<S>
    [interpreted-by]
<SVG renderer>
    [becomes]
<YELLOW-CIRCLE>
```

That extra interpreter is part of the trace.

---

## YC-015 - Emojicode

Emojicode's documented start block is `🏁 🍇 … 🍉`, and strings can print themselves with `😀`. ([Emojicode](https://www.emojicode.org/docs/reference/syntax?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

```text
🏁 🍇
  😀 🔤<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" width="256" height="256"><rect width="256" height="256" fill="#FFFFFF"/><circle cx="128" cy="128" r="48" fill="#FFD400"/></svg>🔤❗️
🍉
```

Theory:

```text
<emoji syntax>
→ <string>
→ SVG
→ circle
```

The emoji themselves do **not** pictorially constitute the circle.

Their computational role is lexical.

---

## YC-016 - Shakespeare Programming Language

SPL's key ontology is:

```text
<character> = mutable numeric state
<dialogue>  = computation over that state
<stage>     = active variable context
```

Characters are variables and manipulation happens through dramatic dialogue. ([Esolangs](https://esolangs.org/wiki/Shakespeare?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

For the circle experiment, the clean theory is:

```text
<play>
    [computes character codes]
<SVG text>
    [is spoken outward]
<renderer>
    [draws]
<circle>
```

So the important program text is not “Hamlet draws a circle.”

It is:

```text
Shakespearean drama
→ ASCII/Unicode character stream representing <S>
```

A full literal `<S>` emitter in SPL becomes intentionally enormous because every character code must be constructed through its dramatic numerical machinery. **That verbosity is the experimental datum.**

---

## YC-017 - Whitespace

Whitespace's machine only recognizes spaces, tabs and linefeeds; it is stack-based and includes character output. ([Esolangs](https://esolangs.org/wiki/Whitespace?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

Theory:

```text
<invisible source>
→ push integer character code
→ output character
→ repeat
→ <S>
→ circle
```

So each SVG byte becomes approximately:

```text
[PUSH ASCII("<")]
[OUTPUT CHAR]
[PUSH ASCII("s")]
[OUTPUT CHAR]
...
```

The operational irony is perfect:

> An almost visually empty source creates a visually definite yellow circle.

---

## YC-018 - Chef

Chef interprets values as ingredients and mixing bowls/baking dishes as stacks. Liquid outputs may become character output in implementations of the language. ([GitHub](https://github.com/YeyoM/chef_interpreter/?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

Theory:

```text
<ingredients>
    [are manipulated through]
<recipe>
    [become]
<character values>
    [served as]
<SVG>
```

The circle therefore becomes **a served textual dish**.

Not geometry.

Not pixels.

A recipe whose product happens to be executable by another interpreter.

---

## YC-019 - Beatnik

Beatnik scores English words using Scrabble values; score `5` pushes a value and score `9` outputs the popped value as a character. ([Esolangs](https://esolangs.org/wiki/Beatnik?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

Theory:

```text
<word>
    [Scrabble-score]
<number>
    [interpreted-as]
<stack instruction>
```

To generate `<S>`:

```text
for character c in S:
    <word scoring 5>
    <word scoring ord(c)>
    <word scoring 9>
```

The extraordinary part is:

```text
literary lexical choice
=
machine instruction
```

A different poem-like surface can emit **the identical SVG**.

---

Rockstar deliberately makes programs resemble rock lyrics. Its current implementation retains that core design. ([Code with Rockstar](https://codewithrockstar.com/))

```text
<lyric>
→ variables / expressions / output
→ SVG character stream
→ circle
```

This specimen should be written as a genuine song rather than simply stuffing the SVG into one ugly literal.

That gives us an important measurement:

```text
<aesthetic freedom>
while
<output invariant>
```

LOLCODE has direct string output through `VISIBLE`. ([Esolangs](https://esolangs.org/wiki/LOLCODE?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

```text
<meme dialect>
```

Ook! is a direct substitution over Brainfuck's eight operations, including byte output. ([Esolangs](https://esolangs.org/wiki/Ook%21?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

```text
<orangutan utterance>
```

# SPATIAL PROGRAMS

## YC-024 - Piet

Piet source **is an image**, and execution arises from transitions between colored regions. ([GitHub](https://github.com/tessi/rpiet?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

Theory:

```text
<Piet image>
    [navigated-by]
<instruction pointer>
    [manipulates]
<stack>
    [emits]
<SVG>
```

The experimental inversion is outstanding:

```text
IMAGE₁ = program
IMAGE₂ = yellow-circle
```

A picture produces another picture, but the first picture is interpreted operationally while the second is interpreted pictorially.

## YC-025 - Befunge

Befunge's program exists on a 2-D grid and its instruction pointer can travel in all cardinal directions. ([Cat's Eye Technologies](https://git.catseye.tc/Befunge-93/?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

A minimal direct-string-output pattern can conceptually be arranged:

```text
> "..." v
^      , <
```

with the SVG bytes traversed through the 2-D field.

Theory:

```text
<program position>
    [determines]
<execution order>
```

Thus the **topology of source** contributes to production of the circle.

## YC-026 - Hexagony

Theory:

```text
<hexagonal program space>
+
<six possible instruction directions>
→ character computation
→ SVG
```

Here the circle's operational precursor has a geometry unrelated to circles.

That is useful: **source geometry ≠ artifact geometry**.

## YC-027 - Cubix

Theory:

```text
<cube>

<faces>

SVG
```

## YC-028 - AsciiDots

```text
<value-as-dot>
    [moves-through]
<ASCII track>
    [encounters]
<operators>
```

To produce the circle:

```text
moving values
→ character codes
→ SVG
```

The program is best understood as a **little machine diagram** rather than a sentence.

## YC-029 - Marbelous

Theory:

```text

    [falls-through]
<board>

<gates>
```

Then:

```text
marble values
→ character values
→ SVG
```

This one makes computation read like physical causation.

## YC-030 - Folders

Folders does not use source files: directory nesting itself encodes its parse structure, and files are ignored. ([Esolangs](https://esolangs.org/wiki/Folders?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

Theory:

```text
<directory hierarchy>
    [is parsed as]
<program>
    [produces]
<SVG>
```

So the circle is produced by something that **cannot meaningfully be printed as source text**.

Its program text is its filesystem topology.

That challenges our experimental vocabulary itself.

We should record:

```text
<program-representation> = directory tree
```

rather than forcing it into `.txt`.

## YC-031 - Velato

```text

    [functions-as]
<instruction>
```

A melody computes the byte sequence of `<S>`.

Hence:

```text
music heard by human
=
program interpreted by machine
```

while producing an unrelated visual object.

## YC-032 - Poetic

Theory:

```text
<word length>
    [functions-as]
<numeric/instructional information>
```

The same operational program can therefore be rewritten through different lexical surfaces while preserving the circle.

This lets us measure:

## YC-033 - Olympus

Theory:

```text

    [petitions]
<computational agents/gods>
```

rather than:

```text
<machine>
```

For the yellow circle, the interesting question is whether:

becomes an imperative or a successful invocation.

The computational relation itself changes.

## YC-034 - Entropy

Theory:

```text

    [changes through observation/access]
```

Now our invariant is under attack.

The circle specification itself can decay.

```text

48  → 47.8 → ...
```

So the program tests:

> Can the same artifact survive unstable internal description?

This is an especially good adversarial case.

## YC-035 - Valence

Theory:


rather than enforcing one parse.

Thus:


The experiment becomes:

> Can ambiguity converge on one visual invariant?

That might be one of the strongest specimens in the entire corpus.

# SITUATED PROGRAMMING

## YC-000 - Put-That-There

This should remain specimen zero.

Theory:

```text
<utterance> = "CREATE A YELLOW CIRCLE THERE"
<gesture>    = pointing at (128,128)
```

Then:

```text
<CIRCLE>
    [fills]
<object-type slot>

<YELLOW>
    [fills]
<appearance slot>

<THERE>
    [suspends until gesture supplies]
<location>
```

The program exists across **two modalities**.

Neither alone is complete.

```text
speech + gesture → complete operation
```

This is fundamentally different from SVG:

```text
<circle cx="128" ...>
```

because `(128,128)` never appears in the utterance.

The world supplies it.

# YC-036 - Human using Paint

Theory:

```text
<human intention>
→ visually locate ellipse tool
→ visually locate yellow
→ configure GUI state
→ drag from one boundary to another
→ Paint interprets pointer trajectory
→ circle
```

There is no conventional textual program.

But the **action sequence is program-like**.

```text
SELECT ellipse
SELECT yellow
DRAG (80,80) → (176,176)
```

That becomes our <action-program>.

# YC-037 - Python controlling Paint

Now a conventional program controls a program.

```text
<Python>
→ OS pointer API
→ Paint GUI
→ Paint geometry engine
→ circle
```

Conceptually:

```python
click(ellipse_tool)
click(yellow_swatch)
moveTo(80, 80)
dragTo(176, 176)
```

The crucial theoretical statement:

```text
Python does not draw <circle>.
Python programs <Paint>.
Paint programs <pixels>.
```

```text

→ model

→ pixels
```

Theory:

```text
<natural language>
    [generates]
<formal operational description>
```

Here the model acts as a **compiler whose target language is p5.js**, although its translation is probabilistic rather than conventionally specified.

# YC-039 - Model generates SVG

```text
prompt
→ model
→ declarative SVG
→ browser
→ circle
```

This should likely be one of the shortest operational paths involving an LLM.

The model can translate:

```text
yellow circle there
```

almost directly into:

```xml
<circle cx="128" cy="128" r="48" fill="#FFD400"/>
```

---

# YC-040 - Model generates GLSL

Now the same English sentence produces an entirely different circle ontology:

```text
"circle"
→ model
→ distance equation
→ per-fragment predicate
→ pixels
```

This is important because the **same model** can instantiate incompatible theories of the object depending on target language.

---

# YC-041 - Astra / computer-use harness

Here the prompt produces neither graphics code nor geometric primitives directly.

```text
"Put a yellow circle there."
↓
<model>
↓
<screen interpretation>
↓
<action tokens>
↓
<mouse/keyboard events>
↓
<Paint>
↓
<graphics machinery>
↓
<pixels>
```

The crucial <entities> become:

```text
<screen>
<tool>
<button>
<pointer>
<application-state>
<target>
```

and the available [operations] become something like:

```text
[observe]
[click]
[drag]
[type]
[wait]
[observe-again]
```

The circle is no longer programmed through a circle primitive.

It is programmed by **operating a program that contains one**.

---

# 9. <Theory-Code Mapping>

Now the comparison becomes much sharper.

| Program | What is <circle>? | What is <there>? | Principal [operation] |
|---|---|---|---|
| Logo | trajectory | turtle-relative location | [move]/[turn] |
| Scratch | sprite/pen trajectory | stage coordinate | [snap]/[move] |
| p5.js | graphics primitive | `(x,y)` | `[circle]` |
| Processing | graphics primitive | `(x,y)` | `[circle]` |
| Canvas | path | `(x,y)` | `[arc]` |
| SVG | declared geometric entity | `cx,cy` | [declare] |
| CSS | rounded rectangle | layout position | [style] |
| Turtle | path generated by agent | turtle position | [move] |
| Pillow | raster ellipse in bounds | bounding box | [rasterize] |
| OpenSCAD | mathematical geometry | transform | [construct] |
| PostScript | path in graphics state | page coordinate | [arc]/[fill] |
| TikZ | geometric specification | drawing coordinate | [declare] |
| GLSL | pixel predicate | center vector | [classify] |
| PPM | collection of colored pixels | pixel indices | [emit] |
| Emojicode | SVG character sequence | encoded text | [print] |
| Shakespeare | numerical drama emitting chars | encoded text | [speak] |
| Whitespace | byte sequence | encoded text | [push]/[output] |
| Chef | values cooked into output | encoded text | [mix]/[serve] |
| Beatnik | Scrabble-governed character generation | encoded text | [score]/[output] |
| Rockstar | lyrical computation | encoded text | [say] |
| LOLCODE | string | encoded text | [VISIBLE] |
| ArnoldC | string | encoded text | [TALK TO THE HAND] |
| Ook! | tape-generated bytes | encoded text | [output] |
| Piet | result of colored control flow | encoded text | [navigate] |
| Befunge | result of 2-D control flow | encoded text | [navigate] |
| Hexagony | result of hexagonal execution | encoded text | [navigate] |
| Cubix | result of cubic execution | encoded text | [navigate] |
| AsciiDots | moving values in diagram | encoded text | [flow] |
| Marbelous | falling value-marble | encoded text | [fall] |
| Folders | filesystem parse tree | encoded text | [traverse] |
| Put-That-There | explicitly named geometric object | pointing gesture | [create] |
| Paint | GUI shape operation | drag geometry | [direct-manipulate] |
| Astra | inferred goal delegated to GUI | contextual/deictic target | [observe]/[act] |

And this gives us an even more interesting classification than “programming languages.”

### <types>

The languages disagree about the type of the circle:

```text
Logo       : <trajectory>
SVG        : <geometry>
CSS        : <styled-box>
GLSL       : <predicate>
PPM        : <pixel-set>
Shakespeare: <future-output-of-a-play>
Piet       : <future-output-of-an-image-program>
Paint      : <result-of-GUI-state-and-gesture>
Astra      : <desired-world-state>
```

That is the killer comparison.

### <functions>

Each exposes different verbs:

```text
Logo       → move, turn
SVG        → declare
Canvas     → beginPath, arc, fill
GLSL       → calculate, compare
Beatnik    → score, stack, output
Paint      → select, drag
Astra      → observe, infer, manipulate, verify
```

### <classes>

Most of these do not need classes at all. That absence is useful. Object-oriented architecture is irrelevant to the invariant.

### <tests>

Every specimen receives the same end test:

```text
assert perceptually_is_yellow_circle(output)
assert center ≈ (128,128)
assert radius ≈ 48
assert color ≈ #FFD400
```

But it also needs a more interesting test:

```text
assert operational_trace_is_reconstructable(run)
```

### <configuration>

Configuration should contain the invariant rather than burying it in each implementation:

```text
CANVAS_W = 256
CANVAS_H = 256
CX       = 128
CY       = 128
R        = 48
YELLOW   = #FFD400
```

---

# 10. <Residual Human Theory>

There is something none of these programs captures:

> **Why do we call all of these results “the same yellow circle”?**

The machine can compare pixels.

It can compare SVG structure.

It can compare coordinates.

But the experimental identity:

```text
Logo trajectory
=
CSS rounded box
=
GLSL inequality
=
Paint gesture
=
Astra goal
```

is a **human theoretical assertion**.

And that is where this stops being a cute polyglot exercise.

The experiment exposes that the apparent noun **circle** conceals radically different operational ontologies.

More importantly, it lets us separate three things that are usually collapsed:

```text
<what is requested>
<what the executing system represents>
<what eventually appears>
```

For SVG:

```text
circle → circle → circle
```

For CSS:

```text
circle → rounded rectangle → circle
```

For GLSL:

```text
circle → distance predicate → circle
```

For Logo:

```text
circle → movement → circle
```

For Shakespeare:

```text
circle → dramatic arithmetic → bytes → SVG → circle
```

For Astra:

```text
circle
→ inferred desired state
→ GUI action sequence
→ another program's geometry
→ pixels
→ circle
```

That suggests the strongest formulation of the entire **Yellow Circle operational audit**:

> **The same human description does not identify the same program. It recruits a different theory of the world at every representational layer.**

And the experimental object should therefore not merely be the final `circle.png`.

It should be:

```text
<YELLOW-CIRCLE-SPECIMEN> :=
{
    <utterance>,
    <program-theory>,
    <program-representation>,
    <execution-trace>,
    <representation-transitions>,
    <final-artifact>
}
```

Then `YC-000 Put-That-There` becomes the ancestor, and every later specimen answers the same tiny command:

> **PUT A YELLOW CIRCLE THERE.**

But each one has to decide for itself what **PUT**, **YELLOW**, **CIRCLE**, and **THERE** mean.

---
