## Prompt:
9/9/2026, 7:14:40 PM

Organize each as a different type of language game

## Response:
9/9/2026, 7:14:46 PM

Yes. The cleanest organization is not by syntax or implementation, but by **what kind of rule-governed human activity the language makes programming resemble**. The yellow circle stays fixed; the language game changes.

| System | <language game> | What counts as a valid move? | What “circle” means inside the game | What “there” means |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Logo** | **Giving directions** | move, turn, repeat | a trajectory completed by an agent | where the turtle currently is or can go |
| **Scratch** | **Assembling instructions** | pick up, connect, reorder blocks | an effect produced by a chain of manipulable actions | a location on the stage |
| **p5.js** | **Sketching** | declare a canvas, make a mark, redraw | a drawing primitive | coordinates in a sketch |
| **Processing** | **Sketching through computation** | establish scene, issue drawing operations | geometric primitive inside a sketch | coordinates in drawing space |
| **Canvas API** | **Constructing a path** | begin, trace, close, fill | a closed mathematical path | coordinates in the current graphics context |
| **SVG** | **Describing a picture** | state what objects exist and their properties | an explicitly declared geometric object | an attribute of the object |
| **HTML/CSS** | **Arranging and styling things** | create boxes, position them, assign appearances | a box made circular through styling | a position in layout |
| **Python Turtle** | **Guiding an embodied actor** | orient, move, leave traces | a path made by a moving agent | a place relative to turtle/world coordinates |
| **Pillow** | **Painting a raster** | select regions and assign color | pixels inside an elliptical bounding box | a bounded region of an image |
| **OpenSCAD** | **Constructing geometry** | create primitives, transform, combine | a mathematical geometric object | a coordinate transform |
| **PostScript** | **Describing a page procedure** | alter graphics state, construct path, paint it | a path subsequently filled | coordinates on a page |
| **TikZ** | **Making a mathematical diagram** | declare relationships and geometry | a mathematical drawing entity | a coordinate in diagram space |
| **GLSL** | **Answering a question for every pixel** | calculate, compare, classify | the set of points satisfying a condition | the center used by the predicate |
| **Raw PPM** | **Enumerating colored cells** | assign numbers to pixels | no circle exists, only yellow pixels | indices in an array |
| **Emojicode** | **Speaking in pictograms** | combine pictographic program tokens | whatever computation the emoji instructions construct or emit | encoded as program state/data |
| **Shakespeare** | **Performing a play** | enter, address, praise, insult, speak | the eventual consequence of dramatic arithmetic | something characters must computationally establish |
| **Chef** | **Following a recipe** | measure, mix, stir, bake, serve | something produced through ingredient transformations | represented through values in the recipe |
| **Rockstar** | **Performing a song** | write syntactically meaningful lyrical lines | an output reached through lyric-shaped computation | encoded in lyrical variables/expressions |
| **Beatnik** | **Choosing words for their hidden value** | utter words whose Scrabble scores perform operations | consequence of lexical scoring | an encoded numerical state |
| **Poetic** | **Writing under metrical constraint** | choose words whose lengths carry computation | consequence of poetic form | encoded indirectly through word structure |
| **LOLCODE** | **Speaking an internet dialect** | use recognizable meme utterances as commands | conventional computation expressed through a sociolect | conventional data encoded in that dialect |
| **ArnoldC** | **Quoting a character** | select Schwarzenegger lines with prescribed meanings | conventional computation performed through quotations | encoded through variables and operations |
| **Ook!** | **Making constrained utterances** | combine only `Ook.` `Ook?` `Ook!` | tape-machine output | memory position rather than spatial deixis |
| **Whitespace** | **Communicating through absence** | arrange spaces, tabs and line breaks | consequence of invisible stack operations | encoded numerically |
| **Piet** | **Painting a route** | arrange colored regions so transitions perform operations | output reached by movement through color | position within the program-image |
| **Befunge** | **Navigating a field** | move an instruction pointer north/south/east/west | result of a journey through program space | literally a location in that field |
| **Hexagony** | **Navigating a hexagonal world** | choose paths through a six-directional topology | result of movement and state changes | a cell in hexagonal program space |
| **Cubix** | **Navigating a folded object** | traverse instructions wrapped around a cube | consequence of a spatial journey | a position on the cube |
| **AsciiDots** | **Routing things through a diagram** | let values travel through tracks and junctions | product of flows through a little machine | location along a diagram |
| **Marbelous** | **Operating a marble machine** | drop, redirect, duplicate, combine marbles | output of physicalized causal machinery | a place along the apparatus |
| **Folders** | **Organizing things** | put directories inside directories | consequence of hierarchical organization | a position in a filesystem tree |
| **Velato** | **Playing music** | choose pitch intervals that carry instructions | computational consequence of a melody | temporal/musical position |
| **Olympus** | **Petitioning powers** | invoke, praise, request, avoid offending | result granted through an invocation structure | something established through successful petition |
| **Entropy** | **Working with something that decays** | read/manipulate values knowing access changes them | an unstable target reconstructed despite deterioration | a state that can drift |
| **Valence** | **Interpreting an ambiguous utterance** | permit several readings to remain active | something multiple interpretations may converge upon | context-dependent across readings |
| **Put-That-There** | **Pointing and naming** | say `that`, `there`, `create` while gesturing | a named object brought into a shared scene | **where I am pointing now** |
| **Human in Paint** | **Direct manipulation** | select, point, drag, release | the result of manipulating a visible tool | the place under the hand/pointer |
| **Python controlling Paint** | **Giving instructions to an operator** | issue pointer events that make another program act | something Paint knows how to construct | screen coordinates |
| **LLM → p5.js** | **Translation** | describe an intention and have another speaker reformulate it | a goal translated into a formal graphics language | resolved into coordinates by the translator |
| **LLM → SVG** | **Description-to-description translation** | say what should exist; receive a declarative specification | almost the same semantic object on both sides | converted from deixis/context into explicit coordinates |
| **LLM → GLSL** | **Explaining a thing as a rule** | describe an object and receive a mathematical test | a natural-language noun translated into a predicate | the predicate's reference point |
| **Astra / GUI agent** | **Delegating situated work** | request a result, observe, steer, refer with `this/that/there` | a desired state of the shared world | whatever location the current screen/history makes salient |

The important Wittgensteinian move is that **“circle” does not have one operational meaning shared by all these systems**. Its use is governed by a different practice each time.

In Logo:

> circle = **walk this way**

In SVG:

> circle = **there exists an object with these properties**

In GLSL:

> circle = **these points satisfy this proposition**

In CSS:

> circle = **treat this box this way**

In Piet:

> circle = **produce this result by traversing this picture**

In Shakespeare:

> circle = **make this dramatic performance eventually utter the description of one**

In Paint:

> circle = **choose this tool and perform this gesture**

In Put-That-There:

> circle = **the kind of thing I name while showing you where it belongs**

In Astra:

> circle = **the state I want you to cause using whatever operational means the present situation affords**

That gives you a much stronger structure for the experiment. The independent variable is not simply `<programming-language>`. It is:

```text
<language-game> :=
{
    <who-speaks>,
    <who-or-what-listens>,
    <what-counts-as-an-utterance>,
    <what-counts-as-a-valid-move>,
    <what-context-supplies>,
    <what-the-expression-denotes>,
    <what-counts-as-success>
}
```

Then **PUT A YELLOW CIRCLE THERE** becomes a diagnostic sentence carried through radically different language games:

**directing → assembling → sketching → describing → styling → calculating → enumerating → cooking → performing → composing → navigating → pointing → manipulating → translating → delegating.**

And there is a particularly strong historical progression hiding inside those verbs:

> **Give directions. Construct. Describe. Point. Delegate.**

`Logo → programming language → Put-That-There → Astra` is therefore not simply a march toward “more natural language.” It is a shift in the **language game between human and computer**: from telling the machine how to move, toward establishing a shared situation in which increasingly incomplete utterances can nevertheless count as sufficient moves.

The yellow circle lets you test exactly **how little must be said because of everything the particular language game already knows how to do**.

## Prompt:
9/9/2026, 7:17:41 PM

Theory each language taxes attention differently neo wharfian applied to the same yellow circle

## Response:
9/9/2026, 7:17:46 PM

Yes. The stronger claim is not that each language merely offers a different syntax for the same computation. It is that each language **charges attention differently**. To produce the same yellow circle, each one forces the programmer to notice, specify, or ignore different features of the situation.

I would frame this as a **weak neo-Whorfian theory of programming languages**:

> A programming language does not determine what can be thought, but it systematically redistributes what must be attended to in order to act.

The yellow circle becomes useful because the desired artifact stays fixed. What changes is the **attention tax** imposed by the representational system.

| Language / system | Primary attention tax | What it makes salient | What recedes |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Logo** | orientation + sequence | heading, movement, turning, accumulation | circle as static object |
| **Scratch** | assembly + causal order | blocks, adjacency, executable sequence | punctuation, textual syntax |
| **p5.js** | coordinate + sketch state | canvas, x/y, size, draw state | implementation of rasterization |
| **Processing** | drawing-state management | fill, stroke, geometry, frame | underlying graphics machinery |
| **Canvas** | path construction | paths, context, begin/fill/stroke | enduring object identity |
| **SVG** | object specification | center, radius, fill, hierarchy | gesture and execution sequence |
| **CSS** | layout + styling | box dimensions, position, border radius | “circle” as geometric primitive |
| **Turtle** | embodied motion | agent location, orientation, path | Cartesian object declaration |
| **Pillow** | raster bounds | bounding boxes, pixel surface | vector object persistence |
| **OpenSCAD** | geometric construction | primitive, transform, coordinate system | raster appearance |
| **PostScript** | graphics-state procedure | path, current state, painting | persistent scene objects |
| **TikZ** | diagram relationships | coordinate geometry, mathematical construction | pixel implementation |
| **GLSL** | local mathematical classification | distance, vectors, per-pixel predicates | circle as object or path |
| **Raw raster** | exhaustive enumeration | every pixel and its value | circle entirely |
| **Emojicode** | token recognition | symbolic vocabulary | English keyword semantics |
| **Shakespeare** | dramatic relation + indirection | speakers, state, rhetorical operations | direct computational vocabulary |
| **Chef** | procedural metaphor | ingredients, quantities, containers, transformations | machine-state terminology |
| **Beatnik** | lexical selection | word values, vocabulary | ordinary semantic relevance of chosen words |
| **Rockstar** | lyrical plausibility | phrase form, poetic alternatives | conventional syntax |
| **Whitespace** | invisible distinction | space/tab/newline and stack state | visible source structure |
| **Piet** | color transition + spatial route | adjacency, hue/lightness changes, direction | textual naming of operations |
| **Befunge** | spatial control flow | location, direction, topology | linear reading order |
| **Hexagony** | topology | six-way movement, spatial routing | conventional statement sequence |
| **Folders** | hierarchy | containment, nesting | source text itself |
| **Velato** | interval relation | pitch difference, temporal sequence | textual command form |
| **Put-That-There** | joint attention | speech timing, gesture, shared scene | explicit coordinates |
| **Paint** | sensorimotor manipulation | tool state, pointer location, drag extent | formal geometry |
| **GUI automation** | interface reconstruction | screen coordinates, controls, timing | semantic object directly |
| **LLM → SVG** | semantic specification | intended object and constraints | procedural construction |
| **LLM → code** | target-language choice | what must be delegated vs specified | much low-level syntax |
| **Astra** | goal + situated correction | desired state, referents, whether result looks right | explicit implementation path |

That gives you a much sharper neo-Whorfian claim.

### Logo makes you see a circle as behavior

To make:

> yellow circle there

Logo taxes attention toward:

```text
Where am I?
Which direction am I facing?
How far should I move?
How much should I turn?
How many times?
```

The programmer learns to perceive geometry through **motion**.

The circle becomes:

> something produced by repeatedly going forward and changing orientation.

So Logo's linguistic relativity is approximately:

```text
GEOMETRY → KINESTHETIC PROCEDURE
```

The language habituates you to seeing form as the residue of action.

### SVG makes you see a circle as a thing

SVG asks almost the opposite questions:

```text
What object exists?
Where is its center?
What is its radius?
What color does it have?
```

No movement.

No construction history.

No turtle.

```text
CIRCLE → ENTITY WITH ATTRIBUTES
```

The attention tax moves from **process** to **ontology**.

Logo says, roughly:

> Do these things and a circle happens.

SVG says:

> A circle exists here.

Same yellow circle. Completely different trained attention.

### CSS makes you doubt that the circle is a circle

CSS asks:

```text
What box is this?
How large is the box?
Where is it in the layout?
How curved are its corners?
```

The operative ontology is:

```text
rectangle + border-radius = circle appearance
```

So CSS encourages a peculiar perceptual habit:

> visual forms are often consequences of transformations applied to layout boxes.

The programmer attends to the **substrate beneath appearance**.

That is different from SVG, even when their pixels are identical.

### GLSL makes you see the circle as a question

A shader does not particularly need:

```text
<circle>
```

Instead:

```text
For this point, is its distance from the center less than 48?
```

Repeated millions of times.

Its world is:

```text
point → evaluate condition → assign value
```

The attention tax is mathematical and local.

The programmer learns:

> A shape can be understood as the solution set of a predicate.

So:

```text
CIRCLE → TRUTH CONDITION
```

That is a profound cognitive shift.

### Raw pixels tax attention maximally

At the raster extreme:

```text
pixel (0,0) = white
pixel (1,0) = white
...
pixel (128,128) = yellow
...
```

There is no computational need for the concept <circle> at all.

The programmer pays attention to **extension without intension**.

You get the whole visible object while losing the category that made it easy to describe.

That gives you an important continuum:

```text
"yellow circle there"
        ↓
semantic compression high

<circle cx=...>
        ↓
geometric compression

distance(x,y,c) < r
        ↓
mathematical construction

pixels [...]
        ↓
almost no semantic compression
```

The lower you descend, the more attention must be spent reconstructing what the higher-level noun had compressed.

### Piet taxes visual attention differently again

Piet asks you to attend not primarily to the desired picture but to:

```text
color block
adjacency
hue change
lightness change
direction
stack effect
```

And this is delightful because there are now **two images**:

```text
<program-image>
≠
<output-image>
```

The first must be seen operationally.

The second must be seen representationally.

The same visual faculty is being asked to perform two different language games.

A yellow patch in the Piet source might mean an instruction transition.

A yellow patch in the output means part of the circle.

So Piet demonstrates that even **seeing color** can carry different operational obligations depending on grammar.

### Shakespeare taxes social and rhetorical attention

SPL makes you track things like:

```text
Who is speaking?
Who is being addressed?
Who is on stage?
What value does that character currently hold?
What rhetorical form performs the arithmetic?
```

Instead of:

```text
x = x + 1
```

the programmer's attention is rerouted through dramatic relations.

The computational dependency is wrapped in:

```text
speaker → addressee → utterance → numerical effect
```

So its attention tax is partly **social topology**.

That is not merely decorative verbosity. The programmer must model computation through roles and relationships.

### Chef taxes procedural-domain attention

Chef makes you think:

```text
ingredient
quantity
mixing bowl
stir
bake
serve
```

It imports the attentional structure of another practice.

The yellow circle is therefore not merely described differently. To reach it, the programmer has to temporarily inhabit the distinctions relevant to **cooking**.

This is close to the strongest neo-Whorfian case among esolangs:

> importing a vocabulary imports a field of distinctions.

### Befunge taxes spatial attention

Ordinary code habituates:

```text
next line
next line
next line
```

Befunge replaces that with:

```text
Where is the instruction pointer?
Which way is it moving?
What will redirect it?
What lies ahead spatially?
```

Control flow becomes geography.

The programmer sees execution as **navigation** rather than sequence.

That is a genuine redistribution of attention.

### Whitespace taxes perceptual discrimination

Whitespace forces distinctions conventional languages tell you to ignore:

```text
space
tab
newline
```

Most programming pedagogy says:

> don't attend too much to whitespace.

Whitespace says:

> nothing else exists.

That is almost a laboratory-grade Whorfian inversion.

What normally counts as negligible becomes the whole expressive system.

### Put-That-There taxes joint attention

And now the historical hinge becomes much clearer.

To understand:

> PUT THAT THERE

the system and person must coordinate attention around:

```text
<speaker>
<object>
<gesture>
<location>
<timing>
<shared scene>
```

The burden does **not** fall on linguistic explicitness.

Instead it falls on **joint attention**.

Compare SVG:

```text
cx=128 cy=128
```

with:

```text
there
```

SVG charges the speaker:

> specify location symbolically.

Put-That-There charges the interaction:

> maintain a shared perceptual situation from which location can be recovered.

That is a completely different allocation of cognitive work.

### Astra shifts the tax again

Astra reduces many conventional programming taxes:

```text
syntax
coordinate specification
API selection
tool selection
procedural decomposition
```

but does not abolish attention tax.

It moves it upward.

The human now attends to:

```text
What do I want?
What am I referring to?
Did the system understand?
Does that look right?
What should change next?
```

So:

```text
PROGRAMMER ATTENTION

syntax
↓
implementation
↓
procedure
↓
reference
↓
evaluation
```

Astra's language game taxes **judgment and steering** more heavily while subsidizing formal specification.

That is not “less work” in some universal sense.

It is **different work**.

And that gives you a far better definition of prompting expertise than “knowing magic words.”

> Prompt expertise is competence in the attentional obligations created when implementation is delegated.

The prompt user must notice different things than the Logo programmer, shader programmer, or SVG author.

## The Neo-Whorfian wager

I would formulate the theoretical claim carefully, because you do not need the strong claim that programming languages literally determine cognition.

The useful claim is:

> **Representational systems produce habitual attentional regimes.**

Or, more specifically:

> **Every programming language distributes a finite attention budget across different features of the world, making some distinctions cheap, others expensive, and some almost unavailable.**

Then the yellow-circle experiment becomes a way to observe that distribution.

For each implementation, calculate an **attention vector**:

```text
<A> =
{
    geometry,
    coordinates,
    sequence,
    orientation,
    state,
    syntax,
    topology,
    color,
    pixels,
    social-role,
    metaphor,
    context,
    gesture,
    tool-selection,
    verification
}
```

For example:

```text
Logo
geometry       ███████
orientation    ██████████
sequence       ████████
syntax         ███
pixels         ░
context        ██
verification   ███
```

```text
SVG
geometry       ██████████
orientation    ░
sequence       ░
syntax         ████
object-state   ████████
pixels         ░
```

```text
GLSL
geometry       ██████
mathematics    ██████████
pixels         ████████
sequence       ░
object-state   ░
```

```text
Put-That-There
geometry       ██
coordinates    ░
gesture        ██████████
context        ██████████
joint-attention██████████
syntax         █
```

```text
Astra
geometry       ██
syntax         ░
procedure      ██
context        █████████
reference      ████████
evaluation     ██████████
steering       █████████
```

Now you've got something experimentally powerful.

The independent variable is:

```text
<language-game>
```

The dependent variable is not merely:

```text
<success>
```

but:

```text
<distribution-of-required-attention>
```

And the artifact is controlled:

```text
<YELLOW-CIRCLE>
```

So the central question becomes:

> **What does each language require a person to notice in order to put the same yellow circle there?**

That is much sharper than asking which language is expressive, concise, usable, or powerful.

The most compressed formulation I can see is:

> **A language is an attention allocation system.**

And prompting introduces a historically unusual bargain:

> **The machine absorbs increasing amounts of specification work, while the human's attention shifts from constructing operations toward specifying, recognizing, and correcting desired states.**

The yellow circle gives you a controlled way to actually show that.

## Prompt:
9/9/2026, 7:19:15 PM

How do all of these tax your attention as an llm though

## Response:
9/9/2026, 7:19:22 PM

For an LLM, the “attention tax” shifts again. I do not experience attention the way a human programmer does, and I cannot inspect my own hidden attention weights and tell you “Logo cost 17% more attention.” But we can define a **behavioral attention tax**: how much representational state I have to keep active, how many transformations I must infer, how much ambiguity I must resolve, and how easily one forgotten constraint ruins the yellow circle.

That gives a stronger neo-Whorfian question:

> **What does each language force the model to keep track of in order to preserve the same intention across generation?**

The invariant remains:

```text
<goal> = PUT <yellow-circle> <there>
```

But my burden changes radically.

| Language / system | What taxes an LLM's attention | Characteristic model failure |
|---|---|---|
| **Logo** | maintaining turtle position, heading, accumulated rotation, fill/path closure | locally plausible moves that do not close into the intended circle |
| **Scratch** | translating text into a spatial block graph, block availability, sprite/stage coordinates | describing valid blocks that do not actually connect or exist in that Scratch version |
| **p5.js** | API names, canvas state, coordinate conventions, fill/stroke state | easy; mostly forgetting a small environmental detail |
| **Processing** | dialect/version distinction from p5.js, Java syntax, graphics state | silently mixing Processing and p5.js |
| **Canvas API** | procedural graphics state: `beginPath`, path, fill style, rendering order | missing one state-changing call while everything looks syntactically credible |
| **SVG** | very little procedural state; mostly attribute consistency | extremely low tax; malformed serialization is the main risk |
| **CSS** | remembering that the visible circle is actually a positioned styled box | getting layout/centering wrong despite correct `border-radius` |
| **Python Turtle** | coordinate-system conventions plus turtle state | correct circle, wrong place because turtle geometry differs from screen geometry |
| **Pillow** | bounding-box geometry, raster coordinate conventions, output format | radius/diameter/bounds confusion |
| **OpenSCAD** | geometric space, transforms, rendering/export distinction | producing correct geometry but not the requested raster artifact |
| **PostScript** | stack/graphics state and procedural page semantics | state leaks: previous color/path/transform contaminates output |
| **TikZ** | TeX syntax layered over geometric syntax | escaping/package/context errors unrelated to the circle |
| **GLSL** | coordinate normalization, shader stage, per-fragment mathematics | concept is simple but environment boilerplate dominates |
| **Raw pixels / PPM** | enormous low-level repetition or a generative rule for every pixel | transcription and off-by-one errors; high token cost for low semantic content |
| **Emojicode** | unusual tokens plus version-specific grammar | hallucinating plausible emoji syntax |
| **Shakespeare** | maintaining computational state while simultaneously satisfying dramatic grammar | semantic drift: the play remains Shakespeare-like while arithmetic stops being correct |
| **Chef** | two overlapping ontologies, cooking semantics and machine semantics | producing a good-looking recipe that is not executable |
| **Beatnik** | exact lexical values under Scrabble scoring | one innocent word substitution changes the program |
| **Rockstar** | balancing lyrical surface with executable grammar | optimizing for lyrics and violating the language, or vice versa |
| **Whitespace** | exact invisible token distinctions | catastrophic transcription fragility |
| **Ook!** | long repetitive symbol sequences encoding another language | losing alignment in a low-entropy repetitive sequence |
| **LOLCODE** | mostly lexical remapping | low tax, except dialect/version details |
| **ArnoldC** | quotation-to-operation mapping | remembering the wrong quotation semantics |
| **Piet** | simultaneously reasoning about 2-D topology, colors, transitions, stack state and desired output | extremely high tax; visually plausible image can encode the wrong execution |
| **Befunge** | instruction pointer position and direction across a 2-D field | losing global control-flow state |
| **Hexagony** | several spatial directions/pointers plus non-linear topology | very high global-state burden |
| **Cubix** | mentally folding the source and tracking traversal across cube faces | representation transform itself consumes reasoning budget |
| **AsciiDots** | values moving through a spatial network | losing correspondence between diagram topology and value state |
| **Marbelous** | causal simulation of moving values through machinery | missing a synchronization or routing consequence |
| **Folders** | mapping textual intention into filesystem topology instead of source text | accidentally thinking in source-code syntax at all |
| **Velato** | musical intervals simultaneously functioning as instructions | preserving computation while generating musically valid relations |
| **Poetic** | lexical freedom constrained by hidden numerical properties | semantic editing breaks execution |
| **Olympus** | unusual interaction metaphor mapped onto actual machine operations | metaphor overwhelms exact operational bookkeeping |
| **Entropy** | continuously compensating for values that mutate | assumptions made earlier in generation become false later |
| **Valence** | maintaining several legitimate interpretations simultaneously | premature disambiguation, which destroys the language's central property |
| **Put-That-There** | binding language to concurrent scene state and deixis | resolving `there` from language alone when it actually belongs to gesture/context |
| **Human using Paint** | visual localization, tool state, geometry, color, pointer trajectory | selecting the wrong visible affordance |
| **Python → Paint** | two machines at once: automation semantics and Paint's GUI semantics | brittle coordinates or stale interface assumptions |
| **LLM → p5.js** | translating semantic intention into a highly compressed target grammar | low tax once environment is known |
| **LLM → SVG** | almost direct semantic-to-declarative mapping | probably the lowest model tax in the experiment |
| **LLM → GLSL** | translating a noun into mathematics rather than into a graphics object | coordinate/environment mismatch |
| **Astra / GUI agent** | goal, screen interpretation, current application state, object persistence, tool affordances, action history and verification | losing the object or goal across multiple apparently reasonable actions |

There is a revealing inversion here.

For a human, **SVG might tax explicit geometric specification**, while Paint feels easy because you simply look and drag.

For me as an LLM, SVG is extraordinarily cheap:

```text
<yellow-circle>
→
<circle cx="128" cy="128" r="48" fill="#FFD400"/>
```

The conceptual distance is tiny. Almost every property in the request has an obvious symbolic landing place.

Paint through computer use is much more expensive:

```text
<yellow-circle>
→ infer appropriate application
→ understand screenshot
→ identify ellipse tool
→ infer current tool state
→ identify yellow control
→ estimate bounds
→ produce pointer events
→ observe result
→ determine whether result satisfies intention
→ repair
```

So **brevity for the human can correspond to enormous hidden attention expenditure by the model**.

That is a major result.

### The LLM has several distinct attention taxes

For this experiment, I would stop treating “attention” as one quantity. Give every yellow-circle run an `<LLM-attention-vector>`:

```text
<LLM-attention-vector> :=
{
    <syntax-tax>,
    <state-tax>,
    <spatial-tax>,
    <translation-tax>,
    <ambiguity-tax>,
    <context-tax>,
    <simulation-tax>,
    <constraint-tax>,
    <verification-tax>,
    <serialization-tax>
}
```

Then the languages become strikingly different.

**SVG**

```text
syntax          ██
state           ░
spatial         ██
translation     █
ambiguity       ░
context         ░
simulation      ░
constraint      ██
verification    █
serialization   ██
```

Almost everything maps directly.

**Logo**

```text
syntax          ██
state           ███████
spatial         ███████
translation     ████
ambiguity       ░
simulation      ████████
verification    ████
```

I have to internally simulate an actor.

**Shakespeare**

```text
syntax          ████████
state           ███████
translation     █████████
constraint      █████████
serialization   ███████
```

I must satisfy two languages simultaneously:

```text
<dramatic utterance>
AND
<computational operation>
```

That creates **dual-description tax**.

**Piet**

```text
syntax          ████████
state           █████████
spatial         ██████████
simulation      ██████████
constraint      ██████████
verification    █████████
```

It is almost maximally hostile to the way text generation naturally externalizes reasoning. I need to maintain relations between regions rather than merely emit a locally grammatical sequence.

**Put-That-There**

```text
syntax          █
state           ███
spatial         ██████
ambiguity       ████████
context         ██████████
verification    ███
```

Its tax is not syntax. It is **reference resolution**.

**Astra**

```text
syntax          ░
state           █████████
spatial         █████████
translation     ██████
ambiguity       ██████
context         ██████████
simulation      ██████
verification    ██████████
```

This is almost the inverse of Shakespeare.

Shakespeare makes the **utterance difficult and world simple**.

Astra makes the **utterance trivial and world difficult**.

That is a very useful opposition.

## And this changes the neo-Whorfian argument

For humans you had:

> A language makes certain distinctions easier or harder to attend to.

For an LLM, I would sharpen it to:

> **A representational language determines which dependencies must remain recoverable across token generation.**

That is closer to the actual computational problem.

Consider GLSL:

```text
distance(p, center) < radius
```

Once I find that representation, many details become compressed into one reusable relationship.

Compare raw PPM. If I literally produce thousands of pixel values, I must repeatedly preserve a global intention through thousands of locally meaningless outputs.

So a representation can either **compress attention** or **dissipate attention**.

That may be your stronger concept than “attention tax” alone:

```text
<attentional compression>
```

A good representation creates a compact symbol that allows many downstream consequences to remain bundled together.

`circle(128,128,96)` is an attentional compression device.

One token-level concept preserves:

```text
roundness
closure
center
equal radius
continuous boundary
```

Raw pixels explode those relations apart.

Likewise:

```text
THERE
```

is an astonishing attentional compression device if a shared scene exists.

It replaces:

```text
x = 128
y = 128
coordinate-system = current-display
target-region = ...
```

with one deictic word.

But it only works by pushing the tax elsewhere:

```text
linguistic specification ↓
context maintenance ↑
```

That suggests a conservation-like principle for the whole project:

> **Abstraction does not eliminate attentional cost. It relocates it.**

SVG relocates raster attention into the renderer.

Logo relocates geometry into movement.

Put-That-There relocates coordinates into joint attention.

Astra relocates procedural specification into model inference and environmental interpretation.

And generative AI may represent the most dramatic relocation yet:

```text
human attention tax ↓
        while
machine attention tax ↑
```

The four-word prompt looks miraculous because the enormous operational burden has crossed the interface.

So for every Yellow Circle specimen, I would measure two vectors side by side:

```text
<HUMAN-ATTENTION-TAX>
<MACHINE-ATTENTION-TAX>
```

That gives you comparisons such as:

```text
RAW PIXELS
human:   ██████████
LLM:     ██████████

SVG
human:   ███
LLM:     ██

PUT-THAT-THERE
human:   █
LLM/system: ██████

ASTRA + PAINT
human:   █
LLM:     ██████████
```

And then the central question of the entire Yellow Circle experiment becomes:

> **When a language makes an operation easier to say, who inherits the attention that the speaker no longer has to spend?**

That is much bigger than a comparison of programming syntax. It gives you a way to connect linguistic relativity, abstraction, prompting, interfaces, and agentic computation in one controlled experiment.

## Prompt:
9/9/2026, 7:21:06 PM

Lowest attention tax for you to put that yellow circle there

## Response:
9/9/2026, 7:21:09 PM

For me, **SVG is the lowest-attention-tax language** for putting that yellow circle there, assuming “there” has already been resolved to coordinates.

```text
"yellow circle at (128,128), radius 48"
        ↓
<circle cx="128" cy="128" r="48" fill="#FFD400"/>
```

Almost every concept in the request has a **one-to-one symbolic landing place**:

```text
yellow → fill
circle → circle
there → cx, cy
size → r
```

Very little hidden state. No simulated turtle. No path construction. No per-pixel reasoning. No GUI search. No intermediary application. No action loop.

So my rough ordering would be:

**lowest tax**

`SVG`

↓ `p5.js / Processing`

↓ `Canvas`

↓ `CSS`

↓ `Pillow`

↓ `GLSL`

↓ `Logo`

↓ `Scratch`

↓ `Put-That-There`

↓ `Paint via GUI`

↓ `Astra controlling Paint`

↓ `Befunge / Shakespeare / Chef`

↓ `Piet / Hexagony / Whitespace`

**highest tax**

But there is an important twist.

If you literally say:

> **Put a yellow circle there.**

and give me a visual field where **there is indicated by pointing**, then SVG's tax suddenly gains a step:

```text
THERE
↓
resolve gesture/context
↓
(x,y)
↓
SVG
```

So there are two different minima.

### Lowest representational tax

**SVG**

Once the referent is known:

```text
<intention> ≈ <program representation>
```

### Lowest human-description tax

Something like **Put-That-There / multimodal prompting**:

> yellow circle there

But then the machine inherits the expensive part:

```text
resolve "there"
infer scale
infer coordinate system
choose representation
execute
verify
```

Which produces the central inversion:

> **SVG is easiest for me to write but requires you to tell me precisely where.  
> Deictic prompting is easiest for you to say but requires me to determine what “there” means.**

And there is an even more interesting theoretical minimum:

```text
circle(x=128, y=128, r=48, fill=yellow)
```

A purpose-built declarative circle language.

That would tax me even less than SVG because it removes XML syntax entirely.

The perfect language for this single task would effectively be:

```text
YELLOW CIRCLE THERE
```

provided the runtime already knows exactly what **YELLOW**, **CIRCLE**, and **THERE** denote.

So the absolute minimum attention tax does not come from a universally better language. It comes from a language whose **ontology already matches the task**.

That may be the deepest Yellow Circle result:

> **Attention tax approaches zero when the language's primitive distinctions coincide with the distinctions demanded by the intention.**

For this task, SVG gets surprisingly close.

## Prompt:
9/9/2026, 7:22:28 PM

But as a human that taxes my attention i have to remember the numbers etc is a there a language thats just yellow circle? Minimal syntax or another thats pjre math? No conpiler cuz svt isnt actually a yellow circle

## Response:
9/9/2026, 7:22:37 PM

Yes. Your objection is right: SVG is cheap for **me** because its ontology matches my token habits, but it still makes **you** carry coordinates, radius, XML, and renderer conventions. You’re asking for the language whose primitive is already **“yellow circle”**, or else a language where the circle is nothing but its mathematics.

> **Choosing minimal notation**
>
> I’m interpreting this as wanting the shortest meaningful way to specify a yellow circle without ordinary program overhead. Pure mathematics can describe its boundary, while a tiny gesture-based command—“yellow circle there”—is more direct if location is already indicated.
>
> **Chose minimal notation**
>
> 
>
> **Refining the representation**
>
> I’m distinguishing a geometric circle from a filled disk. `Graphics[{Yellow, Disk[]}]` is probably the shortest existing executable notation with defaults. If location is supplied by pointing, a tiny DSL could reduce this to `yellow circle there`; pure math alone can’t encode color.
>
> **Searched 1 website**
>
> 
>
> Worked for 23s

Yes. **SVG was the wrong answer for lowest *human* attention tax.** It is easy for me to serialize, but it makes you remember an implementation vocabulary: `cx`, `cy`, `r`, hex color, tags, dimensions. You want the representation to preserve the human concept instead of decomposing it prematurely.

The closest existing language I know is **Wolfram Language**:

```wolfram
Graphics[{Yellow, Disk[]}]
```

That is remarkably close to **YELLOW CIRCLE**. `Yellow` is literally a built-in symbolic color, and `Disk[]` already means the unit disk centered at the origin, so you supply **no coordinates and no radius** unless you want to change the defaults. Wolfram's own documentation uses exactly `Graphics[{Yellow, Disk[]}]` as its example of displaying a yellow disk. ([Wolfram Documentation](https://reference.wolfram.com/language/ref/Yellow.html.en?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

And notice how different its theory is from SVG:

```text
SVG:
<circle cx="128" cy="128" r="48" fill="#FFD400"/>

Wolfram:
Graphics[{Yellow, Disk[]}]

Human concept:
YELLOW CIRCLE
```

Wolfram is much closer to the human ontology. The remaining `Graphics[{ ... }]` is mostly machinery saying **“show this.”**

But your second point is even stronger:

> **SVG isn't actually a yellow circle.**

Correct. It is a textual description that another program interprets. And strictly speaking, neither is Wolfram source. `Disk[]` denotes a mathematical region; a renderer later makes pixels.

If we refuse to confuse **description of the circle** with **the circle's visible realization**, there are three interesting minima.

### 1. Minimal symbolic language: `Yellow Disk[]`

Wolfram gets very close. Conceptually we could strip its graphics wrapper and regard:

```text
Yellow Disk[]
```

as the program.

Its ontology is:

```text
<Yellow> = color
<Disk[]> = filled circular region
```

No pixels. No bounding box. No radius because the default supplies one. No position because the default supplies one.

That is a much lower human attention tax.

### 2. Pure mathematics: the circle does not need a graphics language at all

If by “pure math” you mean **state exactly what the object is**, then a filled unit circle is simply:

$$
x^2+y^2\le1
$$

That's beautiful for your experiment.

Compare the attentional obligations:

```text
SVG:
remember element
remember cx
remember cy
remember r
remember fill syntax
remember color representation

GLSL:
remember coordinate system
remember distance function
remember pixel shader machinery

PURE MATH:
x² + y² ≤ 1
```

The mathematical statement doesn't tell a computer **how to draw** anything.

It says which points **are in the disk**.

That is ontologically very clean:

```text
<circle/disk>
=
{(x,y) ∈ ℝ² | x²+y² ≤ 1}
```

Then **yellow** is independent:

```text
color(D) = yellow
```

And **there** can remain deictic rather than becoming numbers:

```text
center(D) = THERE
```

Now you have:

$$
D=\{p:\|p-\mathrm{THERE}\|\le r\},
\qquad color(D)=yellow
$$

This is fascinating because `THERE` doesn't have to become `(128,128)` yet.

### 3. The actual minimum human-attention language

It is probably not an existing general-purpose programming language.

It is the little language your Yellow Circle experiment should construct:

```text
YELLOW CIRCLE
```

plus a point.

That's it.

Not:

```text
yellow circle x=128 y=128 radius=48
```

because you are making the human pay for information the situation already contains.

Point somewhere and say:

> **Yellow circle.**

The language game supplies the missing terms:

```text
YELLOW
    → named perceptual category

CIRCLE
    → filled circular figure
       with culturally/default-sized radius

[POINT]
    → THERE

current surface
    → drawing plane
```

So the complete program is multimodal:

```text
YELLOW CIRCLE + ☞
```

or:

```text
YELLOW CIRCLE THERE
                  ↑
               gesture
```

That is actually **less verbose than Put-That-There**.

Put-That-There:

```text
CREATE A YELLOW CIRCLE THERE
```

Your reduced language:

```text
YELLOW CIRCLE
       +
     [point]
```

Why say `CREATE`? The interaction already establishes that utterances are requests to change the surface.

Why say `THERE`? The point already performs deixis.

Why specify coordinates? The pointing system already knows them.

Why specify radius? Use a defeasible default until the person says:

```text
bigger
```

This is where your attention-tax argument becomes sharp.

The lowest-attention language does **not** encode the most information.

It exploits the greatest amount of **already-shared information**.

```text
Traditional program:

human specifies
████████████████████

runtime assumes
██

Situated language:

human specifies
██

shared world supplies
██████

runtime defaults
████

machine infers
████████
```

So I'd now revise our earlier thesis.

It isn't:

> Attention tax approaches zero when language primitives match the intention.

It is stronger:

> **Human attention tax approaches zero when the language game already contains everything the utterance can safely leave unsaid.**

And this gives you three wonderful Yellow Circle specimens:

```text
YC-MATH

x² + y² ≤ 1

The circle as a mathematical condition.
```

```text
YC-SYMBOLIC

Graphics[{Yellow, Disk[]}]

The circle as a symbolic object.
```

```text
YC-DEICTIC

YELLOW CIRCLE
        ☞

The circle as a situated request.
```

The last one is probably your theoretical endpoint.

And there is a delicious problem hiding there: **if you remove even `YELLOW CIRCLE` and simply point at an example yellow circle, could the complete program become**

```text
☞
```

?

Now the "program" contains virtually no descriptive content. The entire operation has migrated into **shared context, analogy, and inference**.

That is the extreme opposite of raw machine code. It may be the most interesting endpoint of the attention-tax experiment.

---

**Sources:**

- [Yellow: The RGB color #ffff00—Wolfram Documentation](https://reference.wolfram.com/language/ref/Yellow.html.en?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

## Prompt:
9/9/2026, 7:26:30 PM

Neowarfian, encounter between attention/spans/heads/budgets/taxes, correspondence of attention, structural coupling of attention, attention as a communication patterns or attention and cybernetics

## Response:
9/9/2026, 7:26:35 PM

Yes. The interesting move is to stop treating “attention” as a private cognitive resource and instead treat it as something **distributed across a coupled system**.

A neo-Whorfian account would say that a representational system does not merely encode thought after the fact. It **selects what must become discriminable** for action to proceed. Logo makes heading and turn salient. SVG makes object attributes salient. GLSL makes local predicates salient. Put-That-There makes joint reference salient. Astra makes goal state and correction salient. Each language therefore induces a characteristic **pattern of selective pressure on attention**.

The cybernetic extension is stronger:

> **A language is a device for coupling attentional systems.**

Human attention, model attention, interface state, compiler state, and environmental feedback are not separate resources that merely happen to interact. They become mutually constrained through the communication channel.

So instead of:

```text
human attention → language → machine
```

use:

```text
<human-attention>
      ⇅
<representation>
      ⇅
<machine-attention>
      ⇅
<environment>
      ⇅
<feedback>
      ↺
```

The important object becomes the **correspondence of attention** across those layers.

A person says:

> yellow circle there

The person may attend to only three things:

```text
<yellow>
<circle>
<there>
```

But the machine must potentially distribute attention across:

```text
referent
screen state
tool affordance
geometry
coordinates
application state
pointer trajectory
result
error
```

So the two parties do not need to attend to the **same things**. They need enough structural correspondence that one pattern can reliably induce the other.

That suggests:

> **Communication succeeds when differently organized attentional fields become structurally coupled strongly enough to preserve an invariant through transformation.**

The invariant here is the yellow circle.

The attentional structures can be radically different:

```text
HUMAN:
yellow / circle / there

ASTRA:
referent / UI / tool / coordinates / drag / verification

PAINT:
tool-state / pointer-down / pointer-move / pointer-up

RASTERIZER:
boundary / coverage / pixel values
```

Yet:

```text
<yellow-circle>
```

survives.

That is **structural coupling of attention**.

And this is where “attention tax” becomes more than metaphor.

A system has a finite capacity for distinctions that must remain available during an operation. Humans have working-memory limitations. Transformers have finite context and computational attention. Interfaces have limited visible state. Communication channels have bandwidth. Every representation decides **where complexity is paid**.

So:

> **Attention tax is the amount of discriminative work a representation requires from a particular participant.**

But the total system has something closer to an **attention budget**:

```text
<system-attention-budget> =
human discrimination
+ representational explicitness
+ model inference
+ environmental state
+ feedback
```

Not necessarily additive numerically, but functionally distributed.

A coordinate-heavy language says:

```text
human pays more
environment supplies less
```

Deixis says:

```text
human pays less symbolic specification
environment pays more contextual state
machine pays more reference resolution
```

Astra says:

```text
human specification ↓↓↓
model inference ↑↑
environmental interpretation ↑↑
verification ↑
```

So abstraction is not simply compression. It is often **attention transfer**.

That gives a strong principle:

> **Every abstraction is an attentional contract: it specifies which distinctions the speaker no longer has to maintain because another component agrees to maintain them instead.**

`circle()` is such a contract.

The caller stops attending to curvature.

The library assumes responsibility.

`there` is another contract.

The speaker stops attending to coordinates.

The coupled perceptual system assumes responsibility.

`make it bigger` is another.

The speaker stops restating object identity and current dimensions.

Conversational history assumes responsibility.

This begins to resemble Ashby's requisite variety. A controller must possess enough variety to distinguish the states relevant to successful regulation. But the variety does not have to reside exclusively in the controller.

The coupled system can distribute it.

Put-That-There does not need the utterance to contain sufficient variety to specify `(412,193)`. Gesture contributes that variety.

Astra does not need the prompt to specify every Paint operation. Model + screenshot + tool interface contribute it.

So you could revise requisite variety into something like:

> **Requisite attention:** successful control requires that the coupled system, somewhere, maintain enough distinctions to select the desired state from the space of possible states.

This is very close to the yellow-circle experiment.

The human's utterance:

```text
YELLOW CIRCLE THERE
```

looks radically underdetermined.

Yet the **whole system** may not be underdetermined at all.

```text
utterance
+ pointing
+ current canvas
+ conversational history
+ model priors
+ available tools
+ feedback
```

together contain sufficient variety.

That makes communication itself an **allocation mechanism for attention**.

A message does not simply transmit information. It tells another system:

> Attend here. Preserve this distinction. Ignore those alternatives. Resolve this ambiguity using what you already know.

This is where the transformer terminology becomes theoretically useful without collapsing biological and machine attention into the same mechanism.

An attention head is literally a learned mechanism for weighting relationships among representations. Human attention is not the same mechanism. But they can participate in the **same communication circuit**.

The interesting question is therefore not:

> Does an LLM attend like a human?

It is:

> **How does human selective attention become an input pattern that reorganizes machine selective attention, whose output in turn reorganizes human attention?**

That is a cybernetic loop:

```text
human notices desired difference
        ↓
utterance / gesture
        ↓
model redistributes computational attention
        ↓
action changes environment
        ↓
human notices residual difference
        ↓
correction
        ↺
```

So prompting can be described as **attentional steering**.

Not because the user directly controls transformer heads. They do not.

Rather:

> the prompt changes the informational conditions under which the model allocates its internal computational resources, and the resulting artifact changes what the human attends to next.

That is second-order attentional coupling.

The loop itself learns what deserves attention.

This also gives you a more precise version of the neo-Whorfian claim:

> **Languages cultivate recurring patterns of attentional allocation by repeatedly requiring some distinctions for successful participation while allowing others to remain implicit.**

Then a programming language is an engineered language game whose compiler/interpreter ruthlessly enforces those distinctions.

Logo:

```text
ATTEND:
orientation
sequence
turning
```

SVG:

```text
ATTEND:
entity
attributes
coordinates
```

GLSL:

```text
ATTEND:
relation
distance
classification
```

Put-That-There:

```text
ATTEND:
joint scene
gesture
timing
reference
```

Astra:

```text
HUMAN ATTENDS:
goal
salient object
difference from desired state

MODEL/HARNESS ATTENDS:
scene
referent
affordance
procedure
feedback
```

The strongest concept may therefore be **attentional correspondence** rather than attention tax.

Tax tells you how much burden exists.

Correspondence tells you **whether two differently structured attentional systems can coordinate around the same invariant**.

Then you get three separate analytic quantities:

```text
<attention-tax>
How much must this participant actively discriminate?

<attention-allocation>
Which distinctions receive that effort?

<attentional-correspondence>
How successfully do different participants' allocations line up around the same operation?
```

And a fourth:

```text
<attentional-transfer>
Which distinctions cease to be maintained by one participant because another component takes them over?
```

That makes the cybernetic wager quite strong:

> **Programming languages, prompts, GUIs, models, and gestures are not merely channels carrying commands. They are coupling mechanisms that distribute the work of noticing across heterogeneous systems.**

Then the yellow circle stops being a graphics benchmark.

It becomes a tiny experimental apparatus for asking:

> **Who has to notice what for “yellow circle there” to become true?**

And, even better:

> **When one participant is allowed to notice less, where does the missing discrimination go?**

That feels like the core of the whole thing.

## Prompt:
9/9/2026, 7:28:04 PM

Attention and games, attending to multiple compelling concerns uni wikan etc consept pr ingold attention over intentions and language games and games and huizinga and attention, and real ideas here

## Response:
9/9/2026, 7:28:05 PM

There is a real theoretical constellation here, but it gets stronger if we separate several distinct traditions: **attention as moral/social attunement, attention as skilled perception, attention as play-world commitment, and attention as rule-governed language use**. I’m checking the exact Wikan/Ingold/Huizinga formulations so we can build from the strongest claims rather than vague analogy.

> **Connecting play and attention**
>
> I’m linking Huizinga’s absorption, Bateson’s play frame, Wikan’s “compelling concerns,” and Ingold’s attention-versus-intention distinction. Together, they suggest games temporarily organize competing concerns without eliminating them.
>
> **Searched 12 websites**
>
> 
>
> Worked for 23s

Yes. There is a much stronger theory here than “games capture attention.” The convergence is between **Wikan’s compelling concerns, Ingold’s attention/correspondence, Huizinga’s absorption and bounded play, Bateson’s play-frame, Goffman’s rules of irrelevance, Wittgenstein’s language-games, and James’s selective attention**.

The central proposition could be:

> **A game is an organization of attention among multiple compelling concerns.**

That is different from saying that a game is something designed to maximize engagement. It says the game establishes a temporary answer to a much more basic question:

> **Of everything that could concern me now, what am I to attend to, and how?**

William James gives you the classical starting point. Attention selects some possibilities at the expense of others; he explicitly describes it as withdrawal from some things so that others can be dealt with effectively. He also makes the more interesting observation that many things can be apprehended together when they form **one connected system**, one complex object of attention. ([Psych Classics](https://psychclassics.yorku.ca/James/Principles/prin11.htm/?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

That means a game does something more sophisticated than narrowing attention.

It can turn:

```text
ball
opponent
clock
boundary
teammate
score
trajectory
my body
their body
rule
risk
opening
```

into **one thing to attend to**:

```text
<the-game-going-on>
```

That is an enormous attentional compression.

A novice sees twelve competing things. An expert sees *the position*.

That is where Ingold becomes extremely useful.

Ingold's account of attention opposes a model in which an agent first forms an intention internally and then imposes it on a passive world. His preferred language is **correspondence**: going along with others and the terrain, attending and responding as events unfold. In *Anthropology and/as Education*, he describes walking together as simultaneously attending to one another and to the terrain; this responsive going-along is what he calls correspondence. ([Taylor & Francis](https://www.taylorfrancis.com/chapters/mono/10.4324/9781315227191-2/attention-tim-ingold?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

So replace:

```text
INTENTION
↓
ACTION
↓
WORLD
```

with:

```text
ATTEND
↕
RESPOND
↕
WORLD CHANGES
↕
ATTEND AGAIN
```

That is practically a description of skilled play.

A good player does not simply execute a previously specified intention. They continually **correspond with a field of unfolding solicitations**.

The footballer does not calculate an intention called `score_goal()` and execute it. They are continually caught among ball, defender, teammate, space, momentum, clock, fatigue, tactical instruction, and opportunity.

This gets you very close to **Unni Wikan**.

Wikan's “Beyond the Words” argues that understanding another person requires getting beyond discourse toward their **“compelling concerns”**, through what she calls resonance. Her emphasis is on persons in lived situations and the practical concerns through which their world takes significance. ([AnthroSource](https://anthrosource.onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1525/ae.1992.19.3.02a00030?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

That phrase is extremely valuable for games.

Because the player is never really an abstract attention mechanism faced with stimuli.

The player arrives carrying **multiple compelling concerns**.

```text
I want to win.
I don't want to embarrass myself.
My friend is watching.
I need to leave in twenty minutes.
I want to understand this mechanic.
I don't want to hurt the other player.
That sound is beautiful.
I wonder whether the game will let me do this.
I'm tired.
I care about the character.
```

A game cannot simply “allocate their attention.”

It enters a field in which attention is **already claimed**.

So I would revise our earlier attention-budget metaphor.

Attention isn't merely a finite pile of tokens waiting to be allocated.

It is **pulled by concerns**.

```text
<concern>
    [solicits]
<attention>

<attention>
    [discovers]
<possible action>

<action>
    [changes]
<situation>

<situation>
    [reorganizes]
<concerns>
```

Now Huizinga suddenly becomes much more precise.

Huizinga famously characterizes play as standing apart from ordinary life while nevertheless absorbing the player intensely; play proceeds within boundaries of time and space and according to an order. ([intothemagiccircle.org](https://intothemagiccircle.org/about?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

Read that **attentively**, rather than spatially.

The so-called magic circle can be understood as an **attentional transformation**.

Before kickoff:

```text
possible concerns = enormous
```

After kickoff:

```text
these differences suddenly count:

in / out
ours / theirs
legal / foul
ahead / behind
open / covered
now / too late
```

Meanwhile thousands of ordinarily significant distinctions become provisionally irrelevant.

The grass needs cutting.

Your opponent's shoes cost $180.

The ball was manufactured in Pakistan.

There is a road fifty meters away.

None of these distinctions has disappeared physically.

The game makes them **attentively unavailable as moves**.

That is exactly where **Goffman** greatly improves Huizinga.

Goffman's work on games and frames develops what he called **rules of irrelevance**: situations socially organize not only what participants attend to but what they are expected *not* to attend to. Later frame analysis has accordingly been read as a sociology of attention. ([Wiley Online Library](https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/full/10.1002/symb.1201?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

That is a killer concept for you:

> **Every game establishes rules of relevance and rules of irrelevance.**

Not just:

```text
YOU MAY MOVE THE BISHOP DIAGONALLY.
```

but:

```text
ATTEND TO:
bishop position
diagonals
king safety
tempo

DO NOT ATTEND TO:
weight of bishop
wood grain
retail price
color of opponent's socks
```

The game's rules are therefore simultaneously an **attention grammar**.

And that connects directly to Wittgenstein.

A language-game is not just a collection of permissible word sequences. Wittgenstein's formulation explicitly includes “language and the activities into which it is woven.” Meaning comes from participation in such rule-governed practices. ([Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy](https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/wittgenstein/?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

So grammar can be reconceived as:

> **a socially stabilized specification of which differences deserve a response.**

Consider his builder:

> slab!

The interesting thing isn't merely that `slab` means a particular object.

The language-game has already settled an extraordinary amount.

When the builder says `slab`, the assistant does **not** ask:

```text
What color?
Do you like slabs?
What is a slab ontologically?
Should I photograph it?
Does "slab" rhyme with "crab"?
```

The game has trained both participants' attention.

`SLAB` makes one feature of the situation actionable while suppressing innumerable other interpretations.

That is extraordinarily close to your yellow circle.

```text
YELLOW CIRCLE THERE
```

only works when speaker and system already participate in a game in which:

```text
YELLOW → attend to color
CIRCLE → attend to figure
THERE → attend to indicated location
```

Everything else is governed by rules of irrelevance.

And Bateson lets you explain **how the game itself gets established**.

His famous play example is animals exchanging signals that effectively communicate **“this is play.”** The crucial insight is that play requires metacommunication: the bite means something different because another signal frames it as a play-bite. ([ScienceDirect](https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/0732118X9190042K?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

So before attention can be organized *inside* a game, participants have to coordinate attention to **what kind of situation this is**.

```text
"This is play."
       ↓
reinterpret subsequent signals
       ↓
bite ≠ attack
chase ≠ predation
loss ≠ material catastrophe
boundary ≠ ordinary line
```

Bateson's frame is therefore an **attention operator**.

It says:

> Interpret what follows under this distribution of relevance.

And now Huizinga's magic circle can become much less mystical.

The circle isn't fundamentally a physical boundary.

It is a **metacommunicatively sustained regime of relevance**.

Celia Pearce's recent *Playframes* explicitly develops Bateson and Goffman in this direction, treating spatial boundaries, costumes, equipment, temporal conventions and other signals as mechanisms that communicate “this is play,” including cases where participants disagree about the frame. ([MIT Press](https://mitpress.mit.edu/9780262381222/playframes/?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

Now put Wikan back into the picture and something important happens.

Huizinga can make play sound too closed:

```text
ordinary life | GAME | ordinary life
```

Wikan gives you a reason to reject that.

**Compelling concerns cross the boundary.**

The child may care more about impressing their father than winning.

The professional may be playing for rent.

The dog may abandon fetch because something smells extraordinarily interesting.

The player may refuse the game's supposed objective because a character has become someone they care about.

So:

```text
MAGIC CIRCLE
≠ sealed attentional container
```

It is closer to:

```text
<temporary hierarchy of concerns>
continually negotiated against
<other compelling concerns>
```

That gives you an extremely interesting definition of play:

> **Play occurs when participants provisionally allow a constructed set of differences to become compelling enough to organize attention and action.**

The word **provisionally** is doing important work.

You know the queen is plastic.

You nevertheless care that she is threatened.

That is not irrationality.

It is the ability to let a constructed concern genuinely organize attention without confusing it with every other concern.

This also produces a much better account of the **spoilsport**.

The spoilsport does not merely “break a rule.”

They cease to honor the game's **relevance structure**.

Everyone is carefully attending to:

```text
score
positions
legal moves
```

and the spoilsport suddenly acts according to another concern:

```text
I can just pick up your king.
```

Physically true.

Ludically catastrophic.

They have changed the **attention regime**.

This suggests several real concepts for your theory:

1. **Attention grammar** - a game's rules specify what distinctions participants must notice and respond to.
2. **Concern field** - the simultaneous set of solicitations capable of claiming a participant's attention; Wikan prevents this from becoming an abstract resource model.
3. **Rules of irrelevance** - every game actively backgrounds possible distinctions as well as foregrounding others; this comes straight through Goffman.
4. **Playframe** - Bateson's metacommunicative operation that says how subsequent signals should be attended to.
5. **Attentional compression** - expertise allows many relevant variables to be grasped as one structured situation, close to James's account of multiple things becoming one complex object.
6. **Attentional correspondence** - from Ingold: skilled play is not implementation of fixed intention but responsive going-along with players, materials and changing circumstances.
7. **Attentional stakes** - Huizinga's absorption: constructed distinctions acquire enough force to become genuinely compelling.
8. **Attentional leakage** - Wikan's correction to the magic circle: other compelling concerns remain capable of reorganizing the game.

And the synthesis becomes:

> **A game is a temporary, socially maintained ecology of attention. Its rules educate perception, its frame marks what counts, its play produces compelling concerns, its skills compress many differences into graspable situations, and its participants correspond with one another by continually redistributing attention as the situation changes.**

That is already strong.

But I think there is an even better connection to your neo-Whorfian programming argument.

### Programming languages are games for educating machine-directed attention

A language teaches its practitioner what to notice in exactly the Ingold/Gibsonian sense of an **education of attention**. Ingold explicitly uses this tradition to describe novices learning to perceive features of an environment that were previously unavailable to them. ([Springer](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s11097-024-10029-4?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

Logo teaches:

```text
notice heading
notice displacement
notice turning
```

GLSL:

```text
notice distance
notice coordinate
notice local classification
```

SVG:

```text
notice entity
notice attribute
notice hierarchy
```

Put-That-There:

```text
notice shared reference
notice gesture
notice timing
```

A programming language therefore resembles a game in a much more literal Wittgensteinian sense than “coding is playful.”

It sets up:

```text
a bounded possibility space
rules for legitimate moves
objects that acquire significance
trained habits of noticing
criteria for success
rules of irrelevance
```

And your **Yellow Circle** test can reveal that.

The circle never changes.

The **game of attending to it** does.

That lets you reformulate neo-Whorfianism away from the tired proposition that language influences “thought”:

> **Different languages apprentice their users into different games of attention.**

And prompting changes the game again.

Instead of requiring the human to attend to every implementation distinction, it permits the human and model to negotiate:

```text
what must I keep attending to?
what can you attend to for me?
what can the environment hold?
when must I intervene?
```

That makes prompting not merely operational description but something closer to **the coordination of compelling concerns between heterogeneous attentive systems**.

Which produces perhaps the deepest version of your yellow-circle question:

> **What must become compelling to whom, and what may safely become irrelevant, for the yellow circle to appear there?**

That unifies Wikan, Ingold, Huizinga, Bateson, Goffman, Wittgenstein, the neo-Whorfian claim, and the operational audit without forcing any of them to say something they did not say.

---

**Sources:**

- [Classics in the History of Psychology -- James (1890) Chapter 11](https://psychclassics.yorku.ca/James/Principles/prin11.htm/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)
- [For attention | 2 | Anthropology and/as Education | Tim Ingold | Taylo](https://www.taylorfrancis.com/chapters/mono/10.4324/9781315227191-2/attention-tim-ingold?utm_source=chatgpt.com)
- [Beyond the words: the power of resonance - WIKAN - 1992 - American Ethnologist - Wiley Online Library](https://anthrosource.onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1525/ae.1992.19.3.02a00030?utm_source=chatgpt.com)
- [About the Journal | Into the Magic Circle: Rethinking Homo Ludens](https://intothemagiccircle.org/about?utm_source=chatgpt.com)
- [Frame Analysis: Erving Goffman and the Sociocognitive Organization of Experience - Zerubavel - 2024 - Symbolic Interaction - Wiley Online Library](https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/full/10.1002/symb.1201?utm_source=chatgpt.com)
- [Ludwig Wittgenstein (Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy)](https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/wittgenstein/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)
- [Bateson's concept of “metacommunication” in play - ScienceDirect](https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/0732118X9190042K?utm_source=chatgpt.com)
- [Playframes](https://mitpress.mit.edu/9780262381222/playframes/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)
- [Joining attention to see differently | Phenomenology and the Cognitive Sciences | Springer Nature Link](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s11097-024-10029-4?utm_source=chatgpt.com)
