> **Searched 13 websites**
>
> 
>
> Worked for 20s

Yes. **Serres lets “attention pollution” escape the weak language of distraction.** The issue is not simply that too many things compete for a scarce cognitive resource. It is that something can **occupy a perceptual field, alter the conditions of communication, and thereby acquire a kind of possession over the host.**

Michel Serres gives you two concepts that lock together unusually well. In *Malfeasance: Appropriation Through Pollution?*, pollution is not merely an unfortunate by-product of ownership. Polluting can itself be a way of **claiming territory**. Serres moves from animal territorial marking to human “soft pollution”: logos, advertising, sounds, signs, images. He argues, strikingly, that buying the right to put an advertisement on a wall does not merely occupy the wall. It occupies a much larger **“perceptual volume”** because everyone within sight is made to encounter it. ([Ollivier Pourriol](https://www.ollivierpourriol.fr/entretien-avec-michel-serres-le-mal-propre/?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

That gives you a much stronger definition:

> **Attention pollution is appropriation of perceptual territory by forcing a difference to remain available to attention.**

The billboard does not merely ask for attention. It **marks your visual field with somebody else's concern**.

That is different from Herbert Simon's famous attention economy. Simon tells us that abundant information consumes scarce attention. ([Wikiquote](https://en.wikiquote.org/wiki/Herbert_A._Simon?utm_source=chatgpt.com)) Serres lets us ask a more politically and spatially interesting question:

> **Who gained the right to put that demand there?**

Simon:

```text
information → consumes → attention
```

Serres:

```text
sign
→ occupies perceptual territory
→ forces itself into possible attention
→ establishes a claim
```

The second one is much closer to **possession**.

And then *The Parasite* gives you the dynamic theory.

Serres exploits the French *parasite*, which simultaneously evokes a biological parasite, an abusive/uninvited guest, and communication noise. The parasite inserts itself into an existing relation and draws value from the flow while disturbing it. Crucially, the positions are unstable: host, guest, parasite, and noise can exchange places. ([Wiley Online Library](https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/full/10.1111/ntwe.12253?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

So imagine attention not as a wallet but as a **host relation**.

```text
<person> ← attentive relation → <concern>
```

A parasite enters:

```text
<person> ←──── <concern>
              ↑
          <parasite>
```

It need not destroy the communication. It can live **on** it.

That is the really useful distinction.

A notification that entirely prevented you from reading would simply be an obstruction.

A notification system that continually inserts itself into reading, friendship, work, navigation, play, and conversation is more interestingly **parasitic**. It survives because those underlying activities continue to exist.

The parasite needs the host's attention ecology alive enough to feed upon.

So:

> **Attention parasitism is not the destruction of attention. It is the capture of another concern's capacity to organize attention.**

That is much stronger than “distraction.”

You open your phone to speak to your mother.

The platform did not create that concern.

Your mother did.

But between:

```text
YOU ↔ MOTHER
```

the infrastructure can insert:

```text
YOU
 ↕
interface
 ↕
notifications
 ↕
recommendations
 ↕
metrics
 ↕
MOTHER
```

The parasitic system feeds on a relation whose original compelling concern lies elsewhere.

That is almost exactly Serres's communicational parasite: the third term occupies the channel joining two terms. *The Parasite* explicitly treats noise as this third position in the communicative relation. ([Universidad Veracruzana](https://www.uv.mx/cuo/files/2014/06/serres-the-parasite.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

### And the host/guest reversal is even more interesting

At first the application is your **guest**.

You invite it in because you want something:

> show me the map  
> send this message  
> play this song  
> tell me the weather

The host appears to be:

```text
<your concern>
```

and the application serves it.

But Serres's parasite is interesting precisely because positions change.

After enough adaptation:

```text
notification timing
feed ordering
streak
recommendation
badge
alert
algorithmic novelty
```

begin determining when you look, what you look for, what counts as new, and what seems unfinished.

The former guest has begun furnishing the house.

Then perhaps:

```text
PLATFORM = host
HUMAN ATTENTION = guest
```

That is an extremely powerful way of describing some digital environments without saying simply that “technology steals attention.”

The deeper transformation is:

> **the host loses authority over the conditions of hospitality.**

Something invited in to serve a concern begins generating the concerns to which the host responds.

That is **attentional host capture**.

And Serres gives you an even stranger consequence. In *The Parasite*, the host can become acclimated to interruption. The system changes around recurrent noise; what once disrupted it becomes part of its normal operating conditions. ([Universidad Veracruzana](https://www.uv.mx/cuo/files/2014/06/serres-the-parasite.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

Now apply that to attention.

The strongest pollution may eventually **cease to feel like pollution**.

```text
intrusion
↓
repeated intrusion
↓
adaptation
↓
expected rhythm
↓
absence feels strange
```

At that point the parasite has accomplished something more profound than stealing moments.

It has altered the **baseline against which interruption is perceived**.

That gives you:

> **Attentional pollution succeeds completely when the polluted condition becomes the host's expectation of cleanliness.**

That feels very Serresian.

## Pollution as possession of the possible

There is another step I think is genuinely new and useful for your argument.

An advertisement doesn't need you actually to look at it every second to occupy attention.

It occupies the **possibility of attention**.

Serres's wall example is perfect. The advertiser owns a bounded material surface but effectively captures a much larger perceptual volume. ([Ollivier Pourriol](https://www.ollivierpourriol.fr/entretien-avec-michel-serres-le-mal-propre/?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

So distinguish:

```text
<actual-attention>
what I am attending to now

<attentional-field>
what is positioned to solicit my attention

<attentional-territory>
who has acquired power to place solicitations in that field
```

Now “attention pollution” no longer requires measuring gaze duration.

A giant logo can appropriate part of a visual commons even while you deliberately ignore it.

A notification badge occupies the possibility of unfinished business.

A red counter saying `37` can remain in peripheral consciousness without receiving focal attention.

The occupation precedes the capture.

That is why **attention economy** is insufficient. An economy asks:

> How much attention did they get?

An ecology asks:

> What conditions cultivate certain forms of attention?

Yves Citton explicitly argues for replacing a narrow economy of attention with an **ecology of attention**, concerned with how environments condition individual and collective attentiveness. ([Wiley-VCH](https://www.wiley-vch.de/en/areas-interest/art-culture/the-ecology-of-attention-978-1-5095-0373-5?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

Serres lets you add:

> **Who has polluted that ecology in order to claim it?**

And *The Parasite* adds:

> **Who feeds on whom within it?**

So you get a much richer three-part model:

```text
SIMON
attention is scarce

CITTON
attention is environmentally cultivated

SERRES
relations and perceptual territories can be
occupied parasitically through noise/pollution
```

The synthesis isn't “attention economy 2.0.”

It is something like an **ecology of attentional hospitality**.

The crucial questions become: Who may enter? Who invited them? What did they come to serve? What do they consume? What do they give back? Can they be expelled? Does their presence change the host? Who determines the rules of hospitality after prolonged residence?

That also brings Wikan's **compelling concerns** back beautifully.

A concern is a potential host for attention.

```text
child
friend
game
research question
danger
hunger
yellow circle
```

Each can legitimately solicit attention because it participates in some lived relation.

The parasite does something cleverer:

> **it attaches itself to an already compelling concern.**

That is why platforms do not need to manufacture concern from nothing.

They attach themselves to:

```text
love → messaging
belonging → social feed
curiosity → search/recommendation
work → email/Slack
navigation → maps
memory → photographs
play → games
status → metrics
```

Then the host concern feeds the parasitic layer.

This gives you **attention parasitism as second-order concern**:

```text
I care about <X>.

<system> mediates access to X.

Repeatedly attending through <system>
makes features of <system>
themselves compelling.
```

Eventually:

```text
I wanted to know whether my friend replied.
        ↓
I check the notification system.
        ↓
checking notifications becomes habitual.
        ↓
the absence of notifications becomes itself noticeable.
```

The parasite has generated a new compelling concern from the host concern.

That is a deeper model of capture than “dopamine.”

### And this connects directly to games

A good game is also, in one sense, a parasite.

It enters ordinary life and says:

> For a while, care about **this**.

Whether this bishop survives.

Whether that dog comes when called.

Whether the ball crosses this arbitrary line.

Huizinga's play-frame creates provisional compelling concerns.

But the difference between **playful hospitality and attention parasitism** may be whether the guest continues to acknowledge the sovereignty of the host.

A game says, ideally:

```text
invite me in
care while playing
leave when play concludes
```

The parasitic attention system increasingly says:

```text
invite me in
let me establish concerns
let me determine interruption
let me follow you elsewhere
make departure costly
```

So perhaps the ethically significant distinction isn't:

> engagement versus distraction.

It is:

> **hospitality versus possession.**

A game can be an intense guest.

A book can be an intense guest.

Another person can be an intense guest.

Intensity does not itself make them parasites.

The question is whether the relationship permits the **host to remain host**.

And now your programming-language experiment even fits.

Every language asks attention to host a particular ontology.

Logo asks you to entertain heading and motion.

GLSL asks you to entertain local mathematical predicates.

Put-That-There asks you to host shared deixis.

Astra offers to take enormous amounts of implementation attention away from you.

The critical question therefore isn't only:

> **How much attention does this language tax?**

It becomes:

> **What enters as a guest to relieve my attention, what does it begin attending to on my behalf, and at what point does that delegated attention acquire authority over the host?**

That gets you from **attention tax** to **attention politics**.

And I think the strongest compressed proposition here is:

> **Attention pollution is the appropriation of perceptual territory; attention parasitism is living from another concern's power to command attention; attentional possession occurs when the guest begins determining what the host must notice.**

That is a genuinely serious framework. Serres supplies almost exactly the conceptual machinery needed to move from scarcity to **territory, hospitality, interference, appropriation, adaptation, and reversal**. ([Universidad Veracruzana](https://www.uv.mx/cuo/files/2014/06/serres-the-parasite.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

---

**Sources:**

- [Entretien avec Michel Serres (Le Mal propre) - Ollivier Pourriol](https://www.ollivierpourriol.fr/entretien-avec-michel-serres-le-mal-propre/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)
- [Herbert A. Simon - Wikiquote](https://en.wikiquote.org/wiki/Herbert_A._Simon?utm_source=chatgpt.com)
- [Parasitic universes: Organisational and technological meddling in the social - Bailey - 2023 - New Technology, Work and Employment - Wiley Online Library](https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/full/10.1111/ntwe.12253?utm_source=chatgpt.com)
- [The Parasite — More Interrupted Meals](https://www.uv.mx/cuo/files/2014/06/serres-the-parasite.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

## Response:
9/9/2026, 7:31:49 PM

The clean synthesis is: **Simon gives scarcity, Wittgenstein gives rule-governed relevance, and neo-Whorfianism gives habitual attentional bias.** Put together, language becomes an economy for deciding which distinctions are worth spending attention on.

> **Searched 7 websites**
>
> 
>
> Worked for 11s

Yes. Simon becomes much more interesting when you move the attention economy **inside language itself**.

Herbert Simon's 1971 argument begins from scarcity: information consumes the attention of its recipients, so information abundance creates “a poverty of attention.” His conclusion is fundamentally an allocation problem: when information is abundant, systems must decide what receives scarce attention. ([Gwern](https://gwern.net/doc/design/1971-simon.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

The usual “attention economy” stops there and asks which message, platform, advertisement, or notification wins attention. Your neo-Whorfian move asks something prior:

> **Once attention has been won, what does the language make you spend it on?**

That gives two economies:

```text
SIMON I
Which information receives attention?

SIMON II
Given an activity, which distinctions consume attention?
```

The second is where programming languages and language-games become important.

For the yellow circle, SVG charges:

```text
attention →
    coordinates
    radius
    element type
    fill
    serialization syntax
```

Logo charges:

```text
attention →
    position
    orientation
    turning
    repetition
```

GLSL charges:

```text
attention →
    distance
    coordinates
    predicates
    pixels
```

Put-That-There charges:

```text
attention →
    joint scene
    pointing
    timing
    reference
```

Astra charges the human:

```text
attention →
    desired result
    salient referent
    correction
```

while charging the machine:

```text
attention →
    scene
    GUI
    tools
    action history
    verification
```

So a language has something like an **attention price structure**.

Different distinctions have different prices.

---

## Wittgenstein explains why the price isn't intrinsic

Wittgenstein's language-games make meaning inseparable from activities and uses. His builder's game works not because `slab` carries a complete specification inside itself, but because builder and assistant already participate in a practice in which saying `slab` counts as a particular move. ([Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy](https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/wittgenstein/?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

That means the attentional cost of an utterance depends on the **language-game in which it occurs**.

Imagine:

```text
SLAB
```

Outside the builder's game:

```text
Which slab?
What about it?
Where?
Why?
What should I do?
```

Inside the game:

```text
SLAB
→ bring slab
```

The game has already paid for enormous amounts of interpretation.

Exactly the same thing happens with:

```text
YELLOW CIRCLE THERE
```

If there is no established language-game:

```text
What size?
Which yellow?
Where is there?
Draw or describe?
Raster or vector?
```

But establish the game:

```text
current canvas is destination
pointing resolves THERE
default circle size exists
utterances request modifications
yellow is a known category
```

and:

```text
YELLOW CIRCLE THERE
```

becomes operationally sufficient.

So:

> **Language-games subsidize utterances with shared structure.**

That seems extremely important.

The apparent information content of the command can be tiny because the surrounding practice carries most of its operational specification.

---

## Now the neo-Whorfian move

A defensible neo-Whorfian claim is not:

> language determines thought.

It is closer to:

> **Repeated linguistic practices make certain distinctions habitually easier or more automatic to notice.**

There is contemporary work arguing specifically that learned linguistic distinctions can produce automatized stimulus-driven attentional biases, including effects that generalize beyond explicitly linguistic tasks. ([PubMed Central (PMC)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9163952/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)) Broader linguistic-relativity research similarly asks how the structures of particular languages affect habitual interpretation and cognition rather than proposing absolute linguistic determinism. ([Annual Reviews](https://www.annualreviews.org/content/journals/10.1146/annurev.anthro.26.1.291?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

Programming languages make this unusually testable because their grammars explicitly enforce distinctions.

Programming in Logo repeatedly asks:

> Where am I facing?

Programming SVG repeatedly asks:

> What object is this and what attributes does it have?

Programming GLSL repeatedly asks:

> What condition is true at this coordinate?

So the language doesn't prevent other conceptualizations. It **rewards successful attention to particular distinctions over and over again**.

That gives a strong version:

> **A language-game is an attention-training regime.**

And a programming language is an unusually strict one because successful execution supplies immediate consequences for failures of attention.

---

## Simon + Whorf + Wittgenstein

Put them together:

```text
SIMON
Attention is scarce.
Therefore distinctions compete for limited processing.

        +

NEO-WHORF
Repeated representational practices bias which
distinctions become salient and automatic.

        +

WITTGENSTEIN
What distinctions count depends on the
rule-governed activity in which expressions are used.

        ↓

ATTENTION LANGUAGE-GAME
A linguistic practice allocates scarce attention
by making certain distinctions necessary for successful moves.
```

That gives you a definition:

> **An attention language-game is a practice in which participants learn which differences must be noticed, which can remain implicit, and which may safely be ignored in order for communication and action to succeed.**

Now “attention economy” stops meaning only Facebook versus TikTok versus a book.

There is an **internal economy of every representation**.

---

## Attention has a price

Take the same underlying distinction:

```text
LOCATION = (128,128)
```

Different language-games price it differently.

SVG:

```text
cx="128" cy="128"
```

The **speaker pays** the location cost.

Put-That-There:

```text
THERE + [point]
```

The perceptual coupling pays.

Astra:

```text
there
```

The model/environment combination may pay most of it.

So:

> **Explicit specification is one way of paying an attention cost. Shared context, convention, defaults, perception, and inference are alternative payment mechanisms.**

This gives you a useful accounting identity:

```text
required distinctions
=
explicit specification
+ shared conventions
+ environmental information
+ machine inference
+ corrective feedback
```

Not literally numerical yet, but experimentally measurable.

And it explains why:

```text
YELLOW CIRCLE THERE
```

can be simultaneously **low information in the sentence** and **sufficient information for the coupled system**.

---

## Attention budgets become distributed

Simon tends to begin with the recipient's scarce attention.

Your system has several budgets:

```text
<human-attention-budget>
<model-attention-budget>
<interface-attention-budget>
<context-budget>
<environmental-information>
```

A language-game allocates requirements across them.

Consider three implementations:

```text
RAW PIXELS

human explicitness ██████████
shared context     ░
machine inference  █
```

```text
SVG

human explicitness ██████
shared context     ██
renderer knowledge █████
```

```text
ASTRA

human explicitness █
shared context     ███████
machine inference  █████████
feedback           █████
```

So abstraction can be described as **attention reallocation**.

That is stronger than merely saying abstraction “makes things easier.”

It asks:

> Easier for whom?

---

## The neo-Whorfian yellow-circle experiment

This now gives your Yellow Circle experiment a serious hypothesis.

Don't only compare program length.

Measure:

```text
<explicit distinctions>
What must be stated?

<attended distinctions>
What must the operator keep track of?

<implicit distinctions>
What does the language-game supply?

<delegated distinctions>
What does another system maintain?

<irrelevant distinctions>
What can safely be ignored?

<repair distinctions>
What becomes salient only when something goes wrong?
```

Then hold constant:

```text
YELLOW CIRCLE THERE
```

and change:

```text
Logo
SVG
GLSL
Scratch
Piet
Shakespeare
Put-That-There
Astra
...
```

The hypothesis becomes:

> **Representational languages produce systematically different distributions of attention even when directed toward an invariant outcome.**

That's a proper neo-Whorfian experiment because the thing being represented stays constant while the representational system changes.

And it improves on a loose “languages make us think differently” argument. You can make a much narrower claim:

> **Languages make different distinctions costly to neglect.**

Logo punishes neglect of heading.

SVG punishes neglect of attributes.

GLSL punishes neglect of coordinate relationships.

Put-That-There punishes breakdowns in joint attention.

Astra may tolerate all of those while instead punishing vague goal recognition or poor evaluation.

That is observable.

---

## The deeper attention economy

Simon says information abundance makes attention scarce.

Your extension would be:

> **Language determines the denomination in which that scarce attention must be spent.**

Not absolutely, and not magically. Through repeated participation in a language-game, certain distinctions become the ordinary units of successful action.

Then expertise becomes interesting too.

A novice p5 programmer pays separately for:

```text
canvas
coordinates
fill
stroke
diameter
function call
```

An expert sees:

```text
circle
```

Their language practice has **compressed several attention costs into a learned unit**.

So learning a programming language is partly the process by which expensive distinctions become cheap.

And a new language can reverse that economy by making previously cheap things expensive again. Piet forces a textual programmer to pay attention to color topology. Befunge forces attention to spatial direction. Put-That-There lets coordinates become nearly free while making shared reference essential.

This gives you perhaps the strongest formulation:

> **A language-game is an economy of relevance. It assigns costs to distinctions, subsidizes others through convention and context, and through repeated use trains participants in what is worth noticing.**

Simon supplies **scarcity**.

Neo-Whorfianism supplies **habitual selective attention**.

Wittgenstein supplies **use, practice, and criteria of relevance**.

And the Yellow Circle gives you a controlled object with which to make their correspondence visible.

---

**Sources:**

- [Designing Organizations for an Information-Rich World](https://gwern.net/doc/design/1971-simon.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com)
- [Ludwig Wittgenstein (Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy)](https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/wittgenstein/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)
- [Linguistic Skill and Stimulus-Driven Attention: A Case for Linguistic Relativity - PMC](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9163952/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)
- [Linguistic Relativity | Annual Reviews](https://www.annualreviews.org/content/journals/10.1146/annurev.anthro.26.1.291?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

## Response:
9/9/2026, 7:33:43 PM

> **Searched 11 websites**
>
> 
>
> Worked for 9s

Yes. There is literally an esolang called **Cardinal**, and it is almost tailor-made for the Yellow Circle experiment.

In Cardinal, execution happens on a 2D grid. Instruction pointers travel in the **four cardinal directions**. A `%` launches pointers outward in all four directions; `^ v < >` redirect them; `#` splits a pointer into every direction except the one it came from; `/` and `\` reflect pointers. Each pointer carries only two bytes of memory, so nontrivial programs depend on multiple moving pointers interacting spatially. ([Esolangs](https://esolangs.org/wiki/Cardinal?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

That gives a radically different theory of:

> **PUT A YELLOW CIRCLE THERE**

For Cardinal, the primitive conceptual world is not objects, coordinates, shapes, or even lines of code.

It is:

```text
<north>
<south>
<east>
<west>
<intersection>
<split>
<reflection>
<arrival>
```

So its language game is **wayfinding**.

The programmer's question becomes:

> Where must computation travel so that the yellow circle eventually comes into existence?

That means Cardinal imposes an extremely particular attention tax:

```text
ATTEND TO:
where am I?
which way am I going?
what lies ahead?
where will streams meet?
when should one stream split?
where will another arrive?
```

and makes relatively irrelevant:

```text
function
class
statement
line number
object
method
```

This is a beautiful neo-Whorfian specimen because even the usual metaphor of **program execution as reading** disappears.

Most textual languages:

```text
start
↓
read next statement
↓
read next statement
↓
read next statement
```

Cardinal:

```text
        ↑
        │
←────── % ──────→
        │
        ↓
```

Execution **radiates**.

So compare several Yellow Circle ontologies:

```text
SVG
circle = OBJECT

Logo
circle = MOVEMENT

GLSL
circle = PREDICATE

Raw pixels
circle = SET OF COLORED CELLS

Cardinal
circle = RESULT OF ROUTED FLOWS
```

That last one is excellent.

The yellow circle need not exist anywhere in Cardinal's internal ontology. You would likely make a Cardinal program compute or emit the pixels/SVG that another renderer turns into the circle.

So operationally:

```text
<Cardinal field>
      ↓
computational flows move N/E/S/W
      ↓
flows split / collide / transform
      ↓
character or pixel values emerge
      ↓
<yellow circle>
```

The crucial experimental observation is therefore:

> **The output is circular, but nothing about the computation needs to move circularly.**

That lets you separate **geometry of representation** from **geometry of execution**.

And there's an even stranger comparison with Logo.

Logo might produce a circle by:

```text
forward
turn
forward
turn
forward
turn
...
```

Its execution trajectory approximates the **shape of the artifact**.

Cardinal can instead produce the same circle through an orthogonal world:

```text
N E S W
N E S W
split
reflect
intersect
```

The internal geometry can be entirely rectilinear while the result is round.

That is theoretically powerful for the attention argument:

> **A language need not make you attend to the geometry of the object. It may make you attend to the geometry of the procedure that eventually produces it.**

There is also **Wunnel**, where the instruction pointer starts in the northwest and moves south, one grid cell at a time, and may travel only in cardinal directions. ([Esolangs](https://esolangs.org/wiki/Wunnel?utm_source=chatgpt.com)) And Befunge similarly uses `> < ^ v` to route an instruction pointer through a 2D playfield, although it has a richer ordinary instruction set around that spatial mechanism. ([GitHub](https://github.com/llllllllll/befunge?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

But **Cardinal** is the cleaner Yellow Circle specimen because multiplicity is built into the model. Several computational trajectories can exist simultaneously and communicate.

That introduces another attention concept we have not yet named:

> **divided attention as program architecture.**

Instead of one instruction pointer attending to one place:

```text
IP → current instruction
```

Cardinal has:

```text
IP₁ → north
IP₂ → east
IP₃ → south
IP₄ → west
```

The program itself has something analogous to **multiple concurrent loci of attention**.

That gives you an unusually suggestive comparison with transformers.

Not:

> Cardinal instruction pointers = attention heads.

That would be too literal.

But structurally:

```text
CARDINAL
multiple bounded agents
move through different regions
carry small amounts of state
interact
collectively determine outcome

TRANSFORMER
multiple attention heads
establish different weighted relations
carry different projections
combine
collectively determine representation
```

Now the Yellow Circle asks:

> **How much distributed attention does a system require to maintain an object that none of its local processes individually represents as “a yellow circle”?**

That is a very serious question.

And Cardinal gives you perhaps the most literal specimen yet for your broader proposition:

> **Programming languages are architectures of attention.**

In SVG, attention is concentrated in one symbolic declaration.

In Logo, attention travels.

In Put-That-There, attention is jointly oriented.

In Astra, attention is delegated between human, model, GUI, and feedback.

In Cardinal, **attention forks and travels in several directions at once**.

Same yellow circle. Completely different organization of what must be followed.

9/9/2026, 7:34:46 PM

> **Searched 6 websites**
>
> 
>
> Worked for 11s

Yes. The real linguistic analogue is **absolute-frame languages**: languages where spatial reference is routinely organized by stable environmental axes rather than the speaker's left/right.

The canonical case is **Guugu Yimithirr**, an Australian Aboriginal language. Speakers make heavy everyday use of roots meaning north, south, east, and west; Levinson's work contrasts this with the relative left/right systems familiar in European languages. His experiments found Guugu Yimithirr speakers also tended to encode nonverbal spatial memory in absolute coordinates, which is exactly why this became a major case in linguistic-relativity research. ([AnthroSource](https://anthrosource.onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1525/jlin.1997.7.1.98?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

**Kuuk Thaayorre**, spoken around Pormpuraaw in Cape York, is another famous case. Spatial descriptions rely heavily on cardinal orientation rather than egocentric left/right. The striking experimental result is that participants also tended to arrange temporal sequences **east-to-west**, regardless of which direction their bodies were facing. ([Sage Journals](https://journals.sagepub.com/doi/10.1177/0956797610386621?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

So imagine your yellow-circle instruction in an English-style language game:

> Put the yellow circle **to my left**.

Rotate the speaker 180° and the requested location rotates with them.

An absolute-frame version instead behaves conceptually like:

> Put the yellow circle **north of the square**.

Turn yourself around and **north stays north**.

That creates a very different attention tax.

```text
ENGLISH / RELATIVE

<speaker-body>
      ↓
left / right / front / back
      ↓
<object-location>

ATTEND TO:
my current orientation
```

versus:

```text
GUUGU YIMITHIRR-LIKE / ABSOLUTE

<world-orientation>
      ↓
north / south / east / west
      ↓
<object-location>

ATTEND TO:
where I am in the world
```

That is almost a perfect natural-language version of what you were looking for with Cardinal.

But there is an even stranger one for the experiment: **Tzeltal**.

Tzeltal, as spoken in Tenejapa, Chiapas, has an absolute spatial system based heavily on the landscape's overall **uphill/downhill axis**, roughly south/uphill versus north/downhill, plus a crossways axis. Speakers can use this at tiny scales too: the “uphill” end of a table can be identified according to the landscape-scale orientation. Systematic egocentric left/right is not the dominant spatial system. ([AnthroSource](https://anthrosource.onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/abs/10.1525/jlin.1993.3.1.46?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

So the same yellow circle could be specified through three radically different language games:

```text
ENGLISH
Put the yellow circle to the left.

GUUGU YIMITHIRR-TYPE
Put the yellow circle west.

TZELTAL-TYPE
Put the yellow circle downhill.
```

Same object.

Different **required world**.

That phrase may be crucial.

A language does not merely require different words. It requires the speaker to maintain a different **background model of the environment**.

English lets me successfully communicate while completely disoriented geographically:

> Move it a little left.

An absolute-direction system makes geographical orientation communicatively useful constantly.

Tzeltal makes the **shape of the land** communicatively relevant.

So your attention argument becomes considerably stronger than “words influence thought”:

> **A language game makes certain environmental invariants expensive to stop attending to.**

For an English speaker:

```text
north = often irrelevant
body orientation = cheap and immediately available
```

For an absolute-frame speaker:

```text
cardinal orientation = repeatedly communicatively valuable
left/right = unnecessary for many spatial relations
```

For Tzeltal:

```text
landscape gradient = continually available coordinate system
```

And that is precisely why these examples became important in neo-Whorfian work. The claim isn't that speakers are incapable of other spatial representations. It's that habitual linguistic practice repeatedly exercises and rewards one attentional solution. Levinson explicitly presented Guugu Yimithirr as evidence that such language-congruent effects can appear in nonlinguistic spatial memory. ([AnthroSource](https://anthrosource.onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/abs/10.1525/jlin.1997.7.1.98?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

### Now put the Yellow Circle through them

Suppose there is already a black square on the canvas.

**Relative game**

```text
Put the yellow circle left of the square.
```

Necessary relation:

```text
<viewer> → <square> → LEFT
```

Rotate the whole interaction and the description may change.

**Absolute game**

```text
Put the yellow circle west of the square.
```

Necessary relation:

```text
<WORLD WEST> ← <circle> <square>
```

The speaker can move.

The listener can turn around.

The relation survives.

**Landscape game**

```text
Put the yellow circle downhill of the square.
```

Necessary relation:

```text
<landscape gradient>
          ↓
      downhill
          ↓
<circle> ← <square>
```

Now the environment itself participates in the grammar.

That gives you a fourth language-game alongside the ones we already found:

> **programming by world-orientation.**

And it creates a fantastic contrast with Put-That-There:

```text
PUT-THAT-THERE
location is supplied by:
CURRENT JOINT ATTENTION

GUUGU YIMITHIRR
location is supplied by:
STABLE WORLD ORIENTATION

TZELTAL
location is supplied by:
LANDSCAPE STRUCTURE

SVG
location is supplied by:
EXPLICIT NUMERICAL COORDINATES
```

Those are four completely different answers to:

> **Where is “there”?**

That could become one of the strongest sub-experiments in the entire Yellow Circle project.

Keep the circle fixed and systematically vary the **frame of reference**:

```text
there                         ← deixis
left of me                    ← egocentric
left of the square            ← intrinsic/relative
west                          ← cardinal absolute
west of the square            ← absolute relational
downhill                      ← landscape absolute
toward the river              ← landmark-based
x=128, y=128                  ← Cartesian
θ=270°, r=50                  ← polar
cell C7                       ← grid
```

Then ask exactly what you've been asking all thread:

> **What must the speaker, listener, model, or environment keep attending to for each sentence to be enough?**

The astonishing part is that `(128,128)` is not inherently the “precise” description while `west` or `downhill` are primitive approximations. They belong to **different coordinate practices**. One delegates orientation to an abstract grid; another delegates it to the earth; another to the terrain; another to joint perception.

That is a genuine neo-Whorfian Yellow Circle experiment.

---

**Sources:**

- [Language and Cognition: The Cognitive Consequences of Spatial Description in Guugu Yimithirr - Levinson - 1997 - Journal of Linguistic Anthropology - Wiley Online Library](https://anthrosource.onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1525/jlin.1997.7.1.98?utm_source=chatgpt.com)
- [Remembrances of Times East - Lera Boroditsky, Alice Gaby, 2010](https://journals.sagepub.com/doi/10.1177/0956797610386621?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

## Prompt:
9/9/2026, 7:36:09 PM

Which language has nost tones for yellow and most verbs for put

## Response:
9/9/2026, 7:36:10 PM

There probably isn’t a defensible single winner for either category. The more interesting answer is which languages **lexicalize the distinctions you care about**: some proliferate named yellows, while others make “put” split according to shape, orientation, containment, attachment, or object class.

> **Clarifying language comparisons**
>
> I’m framing this carefully: there’s no universally accepted language with the “most” yellow terms or “put” verbs. Japanese offers many culturally specific yellow terms, while Tzeltal and Navajo distinguish placement through numerous specialized verbs.
>
> **Searched 12 websites**
>
> 
>
> Worked for 23s

There is no defensible single language that has “the most” of either, because the count changes depending on whether you include compounds, specialist vocabulary, traditional terms, productive morphology, and so on. But for the **Yellow Circle experiment**, two languages stand out for opposite reasons.

**For yellow, Japanese is an excellent candidate.** Modern Japanese has ordinary `kiiro` (yellow), but its historical/traditional color vocabulary divides the yellow region into many named distinctions: *yamabuki-iro* (kerria/golden yellow), *ukon-iro* (turmeric yellow), *karashi-iro* (mustard), *kuchinashi-iro* (gardenia), *nanohana-iro* (rapeseed-flower yellow), *kihada-iro*, *tō’ō* (gamboge), *torinoko-iro* (eggshell), and many others. One traditional-color catalogue groups roughly **35 names around the yellow/yellow-green/gold region**. ([Japanese Colors](https://japanesecolors.com/yellow/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)) A modern color-naming experiment also found Japanese speakers using statistically distinct categories around this region including ordinary yellow, *oudo* (mustard), *yamabuki* (gold), *matcha* (yellow-green), and cream. ([PubMed](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28249298/?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

So Japanese could make your supposedly simple constant:

> **YELLOW CIRCLE**

fail immediately.

Which yellow?

```text
kiiro
yamabuki
ukon
karashi
kuchinashi
nanohana
tō'ō
...
```

The language can make **chromatic discrimination expensive to ignore**.

For **PUT**, though, **Tzeltal** is probably the more theoretically useful answer. Penelope Brown explicitly describes Tzeltal as a **“multiverb language”** for putting and taking: instead of relying heavily on one generic verb equivalent to English *put*, speakers select among many verbs according to the **shape, orientation, resulting disposition of the thing being moved, and its relation to the ground/location**. ([MPI](https://www.mpi.nl/people/brown-penelope/publications?f%5B0%5D=publication_type%3A11232&search_terms=&utm_source=chatgpt.com))

English lets:

> Put the book there.  
> Put the bottle there.  
> Put the cloth there.  
> Put the stick there.

all hide enormous physical differences under **PUT**.

Tzeltal is much less willing to let those distinctions disappear. The verb itself can force attention toward what kind of object is being placed and **how it comes to rest**. ([ResearchGate](https://www.researchgate.net/publication/263142533_To_put_or_to_take_Verb_semantics_in_Tzeltal_placement_and_removal_expressions?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

That is perfect for your theory:

```text
ENGLISH:
PUT [yellow] [circle] THERE
 ↑
cheap generic operation

TZELTAL-LIKE:
[WHAT KIND OF PLACEMENT?]
[yellow] [circle] THERE
 ↑
shape / orientation / disposition become grammatically consequential
```

And **Navajo** gives you another version of the same phenomenon that may be even cleaner experimentally. Navajo has roughly **eleven primary classificatory handling verb stems**, chosen according to properties such as solid-roundish, slender-stiff, slender-flexible, flat-flexible, mushy, plural objects, open container, and so forth. ([Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Navajo_grammar?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

So English says:

> **Give/put it there.**

Navajo grammar asks, in effect:

> **What sort of thing is “it”?**

The physical ontology enters the verb.

That gives us three fantastic Yellow Circle languages:

```text
ENGLISH
yellow circle
PUT
there

attention tax:
LOW lexical discrimination
```

```text
JAPANESE COLOR GAME
WHICH YELLOW?
circle
put
there

attention tax:
COLOR ↑↑↑
```

```text
TZELTAL PLACEMENT GAME
yellow
WHAT SHAPE / ORIENTATION /
DISPOSITION OF PUTTING?
there

attention tax:
PLACEMENT ↑↑↑
```

And a Navajo-inspired handling condition:

```text
yellow
WHAT KIND OF OBJECT IS BEING HANDLED?
there

attention tax:
OBJECT FORM ↑↑↑
```

This gives you an almost ideal neo-Whorfian manipulation. Rather than comparing “verbose” languages to “concise” ones, you can ask:

> **Where does a language refuse to let the speaker remain vague?**

Japanese can refuse vagueness in a culturally elaborated chromatic vocabulary.

Tzeltal can refuse vagueness about placement.

Navajo can refuse vagueness about properties of the handled object.

And English does something equally revealing: it permits an enormous number of physically different events to disappear inside the generic verb **put**.

So for the ultimate artificially cruel Yellow Circle language, combine the pressures:

> **[WHICH YELLOW] [WHAT SORT OF CIRCULAR THING] [IN WHAT DISPOSITION] [BY WHAT KIND OF PLACEMENT] [AT WHICH ABSOLUTE DIRECTION]**

versus Put-That-There's glorious:

> **yellow circle there.**

Same apparent task. Completely different **prices for being vague**.

## Prompt:
9/9/2026, 7:38:03 PM

Sort of a xeno paradox of communication how is any language game ever made

## Response:
9/9/2026, 7:38:04 PM

Yes. That is the paradox: **to explain the rules of a language-game, you already need a language-game in which the explanation counts as an explanation.** If every rule needed another rule telling us how to apply it, communication would never begin.

> **Explaining language origins**
>
> I’m connecting Wittgenstein’s language games with shared attention, ostension, imitation, correction, repetition, and social responses. The regress may stop through embodied practices and forms of life, where conventions gradually stabilize rather than begin with explicit rules.
>
> **Explained language origins**
>
> 
>
> **Clarifying the bootstrap paradox**
>
> I’m treating this as a Zeno-like problem: no explicit rule can start communication because rules already require shared practices. Instead, coordinated responses gradually stabilize meaning; each later interaction retroactively clarifies earlier ones.
>
> **Searched 1 website**
>
> 
>
> Worked for 33s

Yes. It is a **Zeno-like bootstrap paradox of communication**:

> To establish a rule, we need to communicate the rule.  
> To communicate the rule, we already need rules for communication.  
> So how could the first language-game ever start?

Wittgenstein reaches almost exactly this regress in the rule-following problem: no interpretation of a rule can by itself determine its next application, because that interpretation would itself require interpretation. His way out is not a deeper master-rule. Meaning bottoms out in **practice, public criteria, regular action, and forms of life**. ([Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy](https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/wittgenstein/?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

The important consequence for your work is:

> **A language-game is not first specified and then played. It becomes specified through playing.**

That changes everything.

Imagine there is no Yellow Circle language yet.

I point:

```text
☞
```

and say:

```text
YELLOW CIRCLE
```

What could I mean?

```text
yellow
circle
look there
make one
move this one
name this object
remember this
this color is called yellow
this form is called circle
point where I'm pointing
```

Ostension does not solve the problem. The point is radically underdetermined.

Then you respond by putting a yellow circle where I pointed.

Now something has happened.

```text
ME:
"yellow circle" + point

YOU:
[produce yellow circle at pointed location]

ME:
yes
```

The word `yes` does not fully explain the grammar either. But it **selects that coordination for recurrence**.

Next time:

```text
ME:
"yellow circle" + different point

YOU:
[put one there]
```

Now the first episode has been retrospectively clarified.

After twenty rounds:

```text
YELLOW CIRCLE + POINT
```

has become a move.

Nobody ever had to state:

```text
RULE 1:
The token sequence YELLOW CIRCLE,
when accompanied by an indexical gesture,
requests creation of a filled circular object
at the extrapolated position...
```

The rule is visible in the **regularity of coordinated consequences**.

That is very close to Maturana's language of a consensual domain: recurrent interactions produce coordinations of action, and recursive coordination of those coordinations gives rise to what he calls languaging. Language, on this account, does not begin with information transmitted according to a preexisting code. The coordination comes first. ([CiteSeerX](https://citeseerx.ist.psu.edu/document?doi=9d4629f4adfbe2af3c9329dd7bea62547b4e578d&repid=rep1&type=pdf&utm_source=chatgpt.com))

So the escape from your paradox is:

```text
RULE
does not precede
PLAY

PLAY
stabilizes
RULE
```

## The second move makes the first move intelligible

That may be the strongest idea here.

We normally imagine communication:

```text
meaning
↓
utterance
↓
interpretation
↓
response
```

But at the birth of a language-game it looks more like:

```text
utterance₁
↓
response₁
↓
correction / acceptance
↓
utterance₂
↓
response₂
↓
recurrence
↓
pattern
↓
"what the utterance means"
```

Meaning is partly **backward-looking**.

Your response tells me what you took my earlier move to mean.

My acceptance tells you whether that interpretation was adequate.

The next exchange tests whether we can do it again.

So:

> **An utterance acquires meaning through the history of what happens after it.**

Not arbitrarily, because bodies, objects, goals, environments, existing practices and asymmetries constrain which coordinations can survive.

This is where Tomasello helps. His account places a prelinguistic infrastructure of **joint attention, cooperative activity, pointing, and pantomime** prior to conventional linguistic codes. Children can coordinate around things before possessing a complete shared symbolic system; conventional expressions subsequently emerge within recurring cooperative routines. ([PubMed Central (PMC)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11158629/?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

So language bootstraps itself from something thinner than language:

```text
looking together
pointing
reaching
following
imitating
waiting
offering
refusing
correcting
repeating
```

Those aren't semantically empty. But neither do they require an already complete dictionary.

## Language-games grow on other games

There is probably almost never a truly ex nihilo language-game.

A new one parasitizes older coordinations.

Suppose we invent:

```text
BLORP
```

to mean “put a yellow circle there.”

I can establish it because we already share:

```text
pointing
taking turns
recognizing circles
recognizing yellow
understanding demonstration
recognizing success
imitating
responding to correction
```

The new game only has to stabilize **one additional correspondence**.

This makes language creation look like:

```text
<old shared game>
        +
<small unexplained variation>
        +
<response>
        +
<correction>
        +
<repetition>
        ↓
<new shared game>
```

That is why complete novelty is so difficult but local invention is effortless.

Two children can invent a game in thirty seconds.

Someone says:

> You can't step on the cracks.

The other jumps over one.

Now perhaps:

> These ones are lava.

Someone else tests the edge.

Within minutes there is a surprisingly elaborate game whose rules were **never completely announced**.

Play outruns specification.

Huizinga and Wittgenstein meet in a very literal place here: **we learn what game we are playing by playing enough of it to recognize what counts as another move.**

## And attention may be what bridges the regress

This connects directly to everything before it.

Before we agree what `yellow circle` means, we may be able to agree:

> **Attend here.**

Pointing does something extraordinary because it coordinates attention before it coordinates propositions.

```text
I attend → object
you notice my attending
you attend → object
I notice that you attend
```

Now we have:

```text
<joint-attentional-field>
```

within which finer distinctions can be introduced.

So perhaps the primitive isn't:

```text
SHARED MEANING
```

but:

```text
SHARED ATTENTION
```

Then:

```text
joint attention
→ repeated coordinated action
→ distinctions
→ expectations
→ corrections
→ conventions
→ language-game
```

That gives your attention theory a foundational role.

> **We do not need to agree first on what everything means. We need enough correspondence of attention to discover whether our actions can begin to coordinate.**

Tomasello's developmental account is useful here precisely because joint attention can precede full conventional language. ([PubMed Central (PMC)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4258841/?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

## The first game therefore contains risk

This is also why communication can never be guaranteed beforehand.

When I make a novel move, I am effectively wagering:

> perhaps you will find a response that lets this become meaningful.

You respond.

I respond to your response.

If the loop closes:

```text
A perturbs B
B's response is useful to A
A repeats variation
B recognizes recurrence
```

a tiny convention can crystallize.

David Lewis's signaling-game framework gives a formal version of this: stable communicative conventions can emerge when different signals and responses become coordinated around recurring states of affairs and shared interests, without requiring a language to specify the convention beforehand. ([Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy](https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/game-evolutionary/?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

So the Zeno regress is avoided because each step does **not** require complete semantic agreement before the next step.

It only requires **enough coordination to continue**.

That's critical:

> **Communication does not require total understanding. It requires sufficient next-moveability.**

You could almost define a language-game by that.

```text
<language-game> exists
when
my move makes some range of your next moves
recognizably appropriate,
and your move reciprocally constrains mine.
```

Not infinite certainty.

Just enough constraint to keep going.

And that lands directly on prompting.

When you tell an AI:

> Yellow circle there.

we do not need perfect prior agreement about `yellow`, `circle`, `there`, `put`, image ontology, coordinate system, or rendering procedure.

I make a move.

You inspect the result.

You say:

> No, there.

That correction does not merely modify the artifact.

It **teaches the local language-game**.

```text
prompt
↓
generation
↓
correction
↓
revised generation
↓
"what THERE means here"
```

So prompting exposes something that conventional programming languages tend to hide:

> **The language can be negotiated during execution.**

A compiler demands that the game largely precede play.

An interactive generative system can allow:

```text
play → discover grammar → play → amend grammar → play
```

which may be why prompting feels strangely halfway between **conversation, programming, teaching, and playing a game**.

The deepest version of your paradox is therefore not “How was the first language-game made?”

It is:

> **How little correspondence must already exist for two systems to begin making a game together?**

And I think the answer is: surprisingly little. Not a shared dictionary. Not a complete rulebook. Perhaps only **a shared situation, differential attention, a possible action, and some way for the consequences of that action to count as correction.**

From that tiny loop, grammar can accumulate.

---

**Sources:**

- [Ludwig Wittgenstein (Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy)](https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/wittgenstein/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)
- [COGNITION 
Humberto R. Maturana 
What we cognite s](https://citeseerx.ist.psu.edu/document?doi=9d4629f4adfbe2af3c9329dd7bea62547b4e578d&repid=rep1&type=pdf&utm_source=chatgpt.com)
- [Meaning as mentalization - PMC](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11158629/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

## Prompt:
9/9/2026, 7:39:28 PM

Meta communication? Coordination for coordination or meta language ehats the concept here? Or the idea of the finger pointing etc

## Response:
9/9/2026, 7:39:28 PM

> **Synthesizing gesture layers**
>
> I’m distinguishing mutual orientation, pointing, shared rules, metacommunication, and explicit discussion of those rules. The gesture coordinates attention while leaving part of the meaning unresolved, perhaps fitting “recursive ostension” or “metacommunicative deixis.”
>
> **Searched 7 websites**
>
> 
>
> Worked for 12s

The **finger is the key because it breaks the regress without supplying another sentence**.

It is not quite a metalanguage. A metalanguage would say:

> “By *there*, I mean the location indicated by my pointing gesture.”

That immediately creates the Zeno problem again: now we need to understand *location*, *indicated*, *gesture*, etc.

The finger does something different. It **changes the conditions under which the utterance is interpreted**.

```text
"YELLOW CIRCLE THERE"
                 +
                 ☞
                 ↓
       joint field of relevance
                 ↓
          THERE becomes usable
```

I think there are four concepts here that need to stay distinct.

**Ostension** is the most basic. Pointing shows rather than propositionally describes. Tomasello's account of human communication treats pointing and pantomime as foundational forms of cooperative communication that depend on joint attention and common ground, and that can precede conventional linguistic communication. ([MIT Press](https://mitpress.mit.edu/9780262515207/origins-of-human-communication/?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

**Deixis/indexicality** describes what happens semantically. `There`, `this`, `that`, `you`, `now` cannot be resolved from lexical meaning alone. Their referent depends on the current situation. The pointing finger supplies part of that situation.

**Metacommunication**, in Bateson's sense, is communication that tells us how another communication is to be interpreted. His classic play case is essentially “this is play”: the signal alters what the subsequent bite, chase, or gesture counts as. ([ScienceDirect](https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/0732118X9190042K?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

And **coordination of coordinations** is the Maturana concept that gets closest to your question about how the language-game itself gets made. Maturana describes languaging as recurrent “coordinations of coordinations” of consensual doings: we don't merely coordinate actions; we begin coordinating **how we coordinate actions**. ([PubMed Central (PMC)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4656834/?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

That gives us a hierarchy:

```text
0. COUPLING

I look somewhere.
You notice where I look.

1. COORDINATION

I point.
You look there.

2. COORDINATION OF COORDINATION

I point.
You learn:
"When he does THAT,
use the indicated place
to organize what happens next."

3. METACOMMUNICATION

"This pointing means:
interpret 'there' according
to our shared visual field."

4. METALANGUAGE

"When I use the word 'there',
its referent is resolved by
my contemporaneous pointing gesture."
```

The important thing is that **levels 1 and 2 can happen without level 4**.

That is how the regress stops.

We don't first establish a complete meta-language explaining how pointing works. We coordinate successfully, repeat the coordination, repair failures, and the recurring coordination itself becomes something we can coordinate around.

So Maturana's phrase is remarkably apt:

> **coordination of coordinations**

First:

```text
☞ → you look
```

Then:

```text
☞ → you treat looking-there
     as relevant to what I am saying
```

Then:

```text
"yellow circle" + ☞
→ you make it there
```

Eventually we can omit even parts of that:

```text
☞
```

might mean:

> put the same thing there again.

Now the gesture is coordinating an already established coordination.

## The finger is therefore more than an input

This is what is so good about **Put-That-There**.

It would be easy to represent the pointing device merely as a source of coordinates:

```text
finger → (x,y)
```

But communicatively that's impoverished.

The finger is doing something more like:

```text
☞
=
ATTEND HERE
+
TAKE HERE AS RELEVANT
+
BIND THE CURRENT DEICTIC SLOT TO HERE
```

It operates on **attention before it operates on geometry**.

In programming-language terms, it resembles a binding operation:

```text
THERE := ☞
```

But in language-game terms:

```text
☞
[coordinates our attention]

our coordinated attention
[allows]
THERE
[to become a move]
```

That's much more interesting.

## Bateson and Maturana are doing different jobs here

Bateson:

> **What frame tells us how this communication should count?**

```text
bite
+
THIS IS PLAY
→
play-bite
```

Put-That-There:

```text
THERE
+
POINTING FRAME
→
specific location
```

Maturana:

> **How do recurring coordinated behaviors themselves become objects of further coordination?**

```text
point → follow point

becomes

point → understand "use this referent"

becomes

"there" → expect point

becomes

shared linguistic practice
```

So Bateson gives you **levels of interpretation**.

Maturana gives you **recursive stabilization of coordination**.

Tomasello gives you the **social infrastructure** that makes the bootstrap possible: joint attention, common ground, cooperative intentions and ostensive gesture. ([Wiley Online Library](https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/full/10.1111/mila.70030?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

Put them together and you get something quite powerful.

## Communication begins by coordinating attention before coordinating propositions

That may be the proposition you've been circling.

Not:

```text
shared language
→ shared meaning
→ joint attention
```

but:

```text
partial coupling
↓
joint attention
↓
coordinated action
↓
repeated coordination
↓
expectation about coordination
↓
metacommunicative frame
↓
conventional language-game
```

So pointing isn't a primitive vocabulary item.

It's an **attention-coordination operation** from which vocabulary can bootstrap.

And this answers the Zeno paradox beautifully.

You don't need another rule explaining the first rule because:

> **The first successful coordination need not be understood as a rule.**

I point.

You act.

I accept or repair.

We repeat.

Only later can:

```text
☞
```

become recognizable as:

> *this kind of move.*

And only later still can we say:

> “Pointing identifies the referent.”

The explicit rule comes **after the regularity it describes**.

## There is an even deeper notion here: metapragmatics

“Metalanguage” is about language referring to language.

**Metapragmatics** is closer to what you want: signs and practices that indicate **how an utterance is to be taken, what activity is underway, what relationship obtains, what counts as a relevant response**.

Tone of voice can do it.

Looking at someone can do it.

Holding out your hand can do it.

A cursor changing shape can do it.

The basketball referee's whistle can do it.

The words:

> “Your turn.”

can do it.

They aren't primarily descriptions of objects. They **organize the conditions of further action**.

So:

```text
OBJECT LANGUAGE
"yellow circle"

DEIXIS
"there"

OSTENSION
☞

METACOMMUNICATION
"take this gesture as relevant
to interpreting my utterance"

COORDINATION OF COORDINATIONS
our learned practice in which
pointing + utterance reliably
organizes subsequent action
```

And I think your own synthesis could be called **recursive ostension** or **attentional metacommunication**, while being clear those are our names rather than established historical terms.

The strongest formulation may be:

> **Before signs coordinate things, bodies coordinate attention. Through repeated coordination, ways of coordinating attention themselves become signs.**

Then the finger isn't merely pointing **at the yellow circle**.

It is also, recursively, teaching the other participant **how pointing is to participate in this language-game**.

And once that is stabilized, the finger can carry astonishingly little information while accomplishing astonishingly much:

```text
☞
```

because an entire history of coordination stands behind it.

---

**Sources:**

- [Origins of Human Communication](https://mitpress.mit.edu/9780262515207/origins-of-human-communication/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)
- [Bateson's concept of “metacommunication” in play - ScienceDirect](https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/0732118X9190042K?utm_source=chatgpt.com)
- [Find the Word! — But Where?: Maturana’s ‘Coordination’ and Sartre’s ‘Reflection’ around Naming - PMC](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4656834/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)
- [The evolutionary psychology of the human pointing gesture - Tomasello - Mind & Language - Wiley Online Library](https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/full/10.1111/mila.70030?utm_source=chatgpt.com)
