Languaging maturana analysis of all the yellow circle code

## Response:
9/9/2026, 7:41:05 PM

Yes. The Maturana move is to stop asking what each program *means* and ask what recurrent **coordination of action** each language stabilizes. Then every Yellow Circle implementation becomes a different way of coordinating coordinations around the same invariant.

> **Searched 7 websites**
>
> 
>
> Worked for 12s

Maturana changes the whole Yellow Circle project because he makes the **program text secondary**. The primary object is the recurrent coordination of doings that the text stabilizes.

For him, language is not fundamentally symbols carrying meanings from one head to another. It is a way of living together in recurrent, consensual **“coordinations of coordinations of doings.”** Objects then arise inside languaging as tokens that let participants coordinate without reopening all the prior coordinations that made the object available in the first place. ([Inteco](https://www.inteco.cl/articulos-9?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

That gives us a radical reading of the yellow circle:

> **“Circle” is not first an object that different languages describe. “Circle” is a stabilized coordination that allows an enormous history of previous coordinations to disappear from immediate attention.**

Maturana explicitly says that objects can operate as tokens for the consensual coordinations they coordinate, thereby **obscuring those prior coordinations**. ([Scribd](https://www.scribd.com/document/1072889906/1988-Maturana-Ontology-of-Observing-The-Biological-Foundations-of-Self-Consciousness-and-the-Physical-Domain-of-Existence?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

That word, **obscure**, is gold for the operational audit.

## The Yellow Circle as sedimented coordination

Before anyone can say:

```text
CIRCLE
```

there has already been a history something like:

```text
look
↕
follow contour
↕
distinguish inside/outside
↕
recognize recurrence
↕
coordinate similar cases
↕
coordinate the coordination
↕
"CIRCLE"
```

Once `CIRCLE` stabilizes, all of that can disappear.

We can simply say:

```text
circle
```

The token has become an **attentional compression of previous coordinations**.

So the Yellow Circle audit becomes:

> **How much prior coordination does each programming language allow the word or construct “circle” to obscure, and how much does it force back into active coordination?**

That is much deeper than syntax comparison.

---

# A Maturanian scale of the Yellow Circle

At one extreme:

```text
RAW PIXELS
```

almost none of the coordination is sedimented into the program's ontology.

At the other:

```text
YELLOW CIRCLE THERE
```

nearly everything has been sedimented into consensual distinctions and shared circumstances.

Between them lie different degrees of recursive coordination.

We can write:

```text
<doing>
↓
<coordination-of-doing>
↓
<coordination-of-coordination>
↓
<object-token>
↓
<coordination-using-object-token>
↓
<coordination-about-that-coordination>
```

Programming languages locate their primitives at different places in this stack.

---

## 1. Raw PPM: before the object

```text
pixel 0,0 white
pixel 1,0 white
...
pixel 128,128 yellow
```

Maturanian reading:

```text
<circle>
IS NOT PRESENT
```

The program coordinates:

```text
<position> ↔ <color value>
```

again and again.

The human observer performs the higher-order distinction:

```text
{many coordinated yellow pixels}
        ↓
"CIRCLE"
```

So raw pixels expose coordinations that the object-token normally obscures.

**Maturanian character:** `<pre-object coordination>`.

This is why raw pixels have such high attention tax. The higher-level consensual distinction `circle` is no longer doing the compressive work.

---

## 2. GLSL: coordination as distinction

```text
distance(point, center) ≤ radius
```

Now there is a more powerful recurrent coordination:

```text
<point>
[distinguish-as]
inside / outside
```

Repeated over the field.

The circle is the consequence of a **rule for making distinctions**.

Still, no persistent `<circle>` object is necessary.

So:

```text
CIRCLE
↓ obscured into
recurrent INSIDE/OUTSIDE distinction
```

**Maturanian character:** `<recursive coordination of distinctions>`.

This one is very close to Maturana's vocabulary because the system literally coordinates distinctions over a domain.

---

## 3. Logo: coordination as doing

Logo is almost perfectly Maturanian.

```text
forward
turn
forward
turn
...
```

The circle emerges from:

```text
<movement>
↕
<orientation>
↕
<movement>
↕
<orientation>
```

There need initially be no object called `circle`.

The form arises through **coordinated doing**.

So Logo says:

```text
CIRCLE =
stabilized consequence of recurrent coordinated movement
```

This is very close to Maturana's insistence that objects arise in coordinated praxis rather than existing first as representations.

**Maturanian character:** `<coordination of doings>`.

---

## 4. Python Turtle

Python Turtle performs an important recursion over Logo.

Logo can expose:

```text
repeat movement + turning
```

Python Turtle can say:

```text
circle(48)
```

The earlier coordination is now hidden inside a new coordination.

```text
movement/turning
      ↓ stabilized
circle-operation
```

`circle()` therefore behaves exactly like one of Maturana's object-tokens:

> it enables further coordination while obscuring the coordinations that constitute it.

**Maturanian character:** `<coordination-of-coordination packaged as operation>`.

---

# 5. SVG: the object has fully arrived

SVG says:

```text
<circle ...>
```

Now `circle` operates as an explicit consensual token.

The programmer no longer coordinates:

```text
turn
move
classify pixel
construct curvature
```

Those have been sedimented into the SVG/browser ecology.

Instead:

```text
<CIRCLE>
+
<center>
+
<radius>
+
<fill>
```

become enough for further coordination.

Maturana's formulation is almost eerily suitable:

> objects arise in languaging as coordinations that can become tokens for further coordinations. ([PubMed Central (PMC)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4656834/?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

SVG's `circle` is precisely such a token.

**Maturanian character:** `<object-token>`.

---

# 6. Wolfram: even more coordination disappears

```wolfram
Graphics[{Yellow, Disk[]}]
```

Now defaults absorb still more prior coordination:

```text
where?
→ default

how large?
→ default

how curved?
→ Disk already knows

what color values?
→ Yellow already knows
```

`Disk[]` is a highly sedimented coordination.

So:

```text
many distinctions
↓
one object-token
```

This is **languaging as operational compression**.

The price is that a massive historical ecology of conventions must already exist.

---

# 7. p5.js / Processing

```text
fill(yellow)
circle(x,y,d)
```

Here several consensual coordinations have become independent manipulable tokens:

```text
<CIRCLE>
<FILL>
<POSITION>
<SIZE>
```

Programming consists of **coordinating already stabilized coordinations**.

That is extremely Maturanian.

We could literally read:

```text
circle()
```

as one prior coordination, and:

```text
fill()
```

as another.

The p5 program coordinates them:

```text
YELLOW
    ↓
CIRCLE
    ↓
THERE
```

So the source program is a **coordination of coordinations** in a fairly literal operational sense.

---

# 8. Canvas / PostScript

These sit one layer lower.

They require:

```text
begin path
construct arc
close
fill
```

The object `circle` is partially decomposed back into **procedure**.

So compared with SVG:

```text
SVG:
object-token dominates

Canvas/PostScript:
coordination sequence partially reappears
```

They reveal more of the doing that declarative `circle` obscures.

---

# 9. Pillow

Pillow says approximately:

```text
ellipse(bounding_box)
```

Interesting Maturanian case because our human distinction:

```text
CIRCLE
```

gets coordinated through another object distinction:

```text
BOUNDING RECTANGLE
```

So:

```text
circle
↓
ellipse-with-equal-axes
↓
bounding box
↓
raster
```

The operational coordination does not have to preserve the human object's ontology.

Different consensual domains can nevertheless couple successfully around the resulting artifact.

---

# 10. CSS

CSS is even stranger:

```text
box
+
border-radius: 50%
=
circle appearance
```

Maturanian lesson:

> Objects need not be operationally constituted through the same coordinations by which an observer distinguishes them.

The observer says:

```text
CIRCLE
```

The CSS system coordinates:

```text
BOX
STYLE
LAYOUT
CURVATURE
```

Yet the coupled interaction succeeds.

So this is an important example of **cross-domain coordination**.

```text
human distinction: <circle>

CSS distinction: <styled rectangle>

perceptual consequence:
coordination succeeds
```

---

# 11. OpenSCAD / TikZ

These establish a consensual domain of **geometry**.

The relevant doings have become:

```text
construct
transform
position
combine
```

A circle is a mathematical-geometric token available for further recursion:

```text
circle
→ translate circle
→ combine circle
→ extrude circle
```

Every operation is a coordination performed **on a stabilized prior coordination**.

---

# 12. Scratch

Scratch makes the Maturanian structure physically visible.

A block is already a coordination such as:

```text
MOVE
TURN
REPEAT
```

You then literally coordinate these coordinations by joining blocks.

```text
[MOVE]
   ↕
[TURN]
   ↕
[REPEAT]
```

Scratch may therefore be one of the cleanest visualizations of:

> **coordination of coordinations of doing.**

The syntax is not primarily inscription.

It is assembly of stabilized actions.

---

# 13. Shakespeare

Here something different happens.

The machine coordination:

```text
variable manipulation
arithmetic
output
```

is recursively coordinated through another consensual domain:

```text
CHARACTER
SPEAKING
PRAISE
INSULT
ENTER
EXIT
```

Thus:

```text
computational coordination
↕
dramatic coordination
```

The language deliberately forces two histories of consensual coordination to overlap.

The statement can participate simultaneously in:

```text
<theater language-game>
AND
<computation language-game>
```

So Shakespeare becomes **coordination across consensual domains**.

---

# 14. Chef

Same structure:

```text
computational action
↕
cooking action
```

`mix`, `stir`, `ingredient`, `bowl`, `serve` already belong to a human consensual domain.

Chef recruits that domain to coordinate machine operations.

So the language is parasitic in the productive sense:

```text
<cooking coordination>
[becomes token for]
<computation coordination>
```

The prior language-game supplies the new one with ready-made distinctions.

---

# 15. Rockstar / LOLCODE / ArnoldC / Emojicode

These perform increasingly shallow versions of the same recursion.

Existing coordination systems:

```text
rock lyrics
internet vernacular
movie quotations
emoji practice
```

become tokens for computational coordination.

The interesting Maturanian question is not whether `VISIBLE` or 🍇 “means” a programming operation.

It is:

> **What recurrent coordinated response has stabilized around this token?**

Meaning is not stored in the symbol. Maturana explicitly rejects that picture: meanings belong to the flows of coordinated doings in which signs participate. ([PubMed Central (PMC)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4656834/?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

So:

```text
🍇
```

doesn't contain “begin block.”

Its role exists in the recurrent programming practice that treats it that way.

---

# 16. Beatnik / Poetic

These are more interesting because ordinary linguistic behavior remains operationally consequential.

Beatnik:

```text
WORD
↓
Scrabble-score
↓
machine operation
```

Poetic:

```text
WORD LENGTH
↓
machine distinction
```

The same linguistic token participates in two coordination domains at once:

```text
human semantic coordination
machine numerical coordination
```

They can diverge.

A beautiful word substitution that preserves human meaning can destroy the computation.

That gives a precise case of **non-isomorphic structural coupling**.

---

# 17. Whitespace

Whitespace reverses what ordinary textual languaging stabilizes as relevant.

Usually:

```text
letters = distinctions
spaces = separators/background
```

Whitespace establishes another consensual domain:

```text
spaces/tabs/newlines = operational distinctions
letters = irrelevant
```

So the language is essentially a **re-coordination of relevance**.

A previously unnoticed distinction becomes the token through which further coordination occurs.

This belongs directly in the attention project.

---

# 18. Piet

Piet makes color differences into computational distinctions.

```text
hue/lightness transition
→ operation
```

Ordinary vision already participates in consensual color distinctions.

Piet recursively coordinates those distinctions:

```text
COLOR
↓
RELATION BETWEEN COLORS
↓
COMPUTATIONAL ACTION
```

So it is almost textbook:

> **coordination of distinctions of distinctions**.

And the yellow-circle experiment becomes especially elegant because:

```text
yellow in program
may coordinate an operation

yellow in output
coordinates a perceived color
```

Same perceptual distinction, different consensual domains.

---

# 19. Befunge / Cardinal / Hexagony / Cubix

These transform **location and movement** into coordinated computational behavior.

Befunge:

```text
instruction location
+
direction
→ next action
```

Cardinal:

```text
N / E / S / W
+
split
+
intersection
→ computation
```

Hexagony and Cubix alter the topology but preserve the principle.

Maturanian reading:

```text
execution =
recurrent coordination of movement through a domain
```

They are fascinating alongside Logo.

Logo:

```text
movement produces artifact
```

Befunge/Cardinal:

```text
movement produces computation
which produces artifact
```

A coordination has moved up one recursive level.

---

# 20. AsciiDots / Marbelous

These turn computation into observable **flows of entities**.

```text
dot travels
marble falls
junction changes trajectory
```

Again:

```text
coordination of movement
→ coordination of values
→ coordination of output
```

The computational process is externalized as a causal choreography.

---

# 21. Folders

Folders might be one of the purest Maturanian provocations.

There is no privileged source text.

Instead:

```text
containment
nesting
hierarchy
```

become operational distinctions.

The ordinary consensual action:

> put this folder inside that folder

is recursively recruited as:

> construct this computational relation.

So:

```text
organization-of-things
→ token for
organization-of-computation
```

---

# 22. Velato

Here recurrent musical relations become computational coordinations.

```text
pitch interval
→ operation
```

Not absolute note identity but **relation** carries the program.

Very Maturanian in spirit:

> what becomes operational is not an isolated object but a recurrent relation between doings.

---

# 23. Olympus

Olympus changes the coordination between programmer and machine.

Conventional imperative programming:

```text
programmer [commands] machine
```

Olympus:

```text
programmer [petitions] deity
```

The operation may ultimately be computationally similar, but the **relational coordination** changes.

That is important because Maturana insists languaging occurs within ways of living together, not merely abstract symbol manipulation. ([Inteco](https://www.inteco.cl/articulos-9?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

Olympus makes the social relation encoded by the language-game visible.

---

# 24. Entropy

Entropy destabilizes the recurrence required for consensual coordination.

If:

```text
value
```

does not remain stable under access, then repeated coordination cannot simply assume identity.

The programmer must continually coordinate with drift.

So:

```text
coordination₁ ≠ guaranteed coordination₂
```

This resembles structural drift more than static symbolic representation.

The yellow circle becomes:

> Can an invariant arise through coordinations whose internal states do not remain invariant?

---

# 25. Valence

Valence refuses the ordinary programming requirement that:

```text
one token
→ one operational interpretation
```

Instead multiple coordinations remain possible.

So the system holds a **plural consensual domain** open longer than ordinary compilers permit.

The yellow circle asks:

> Can different recursive coordinations converge on one artifact without requiring prior syntactic uniqueness?

---

# 26. Put-That-There

Now Maturana becomes almost unavoidable.

```text
"CREATE A YELLOW CIRCLE THERE"
+
☞
```

The operation does not reside in the sentence.

Nor in the gesture.

It exists in their **coordination**.

First order:

```text
point
→ follow point
```

Second order:

```text
point
→ use pointed place
as relevant to current utterance
```

Third order:

```text
"there"
+
point
→ coordinate creation at that location
```

`THERE` becomes a token that obscures an entire recurrent history:

```text
seeing pointing
following pointing
sharing field
binding gesture to utterance
understanding requested modification
```

That is almost exactly Maturana's claim about objects/signs operating as nodes within recurrent coordination.

Put-That-There is not merely multimodal input.

It is **recursive coordination across modalities**.

---

# 27. Paint operated by a human

The human does not say:

```text
ellipse rasterization...
```

They coordinate with the GUI:

```text
see ellipse icon
→ click
see yellow swatch
→ click
see canvas
→ drag
see result
→ release/correct
```

The GUI is a **consensual domain** built between designer and user through learned recurrent coordinations.

A button is not inherently a button in Maturanian terms.

It becomes one insofar as:

```text
appearance
↕
clicking
↕
expected consequence
```

stabilize.

The GUI is therefore a **languaging surface of coordinated doings**.

---

# 28. Python controlling Paint

This introduces recursion:

```text
Python operation
→ coordinates OS action

OS action
→ coordinates GUI action

GUI action
→ coordinates Paint operation

Paint operation
→ coordinates graphics machinery
```

So the yellow circle is produced through:

```text
coordination
of coordination
of coordination
of coordination
```

That is exactly why operational audits become difficult.

Each layer allows the next layer to treat prior coordinations as settled tokens.

```text
click(x,y)
```

obscures OS event machinery.

```text
ellipse tool
```

obscures geometric machinery.

```text
Paint
```

obscures thousands of further coordinations.

---

# 29. LLM → SVG

Now the user says:

```text
yellow circle there
```

and the model generates:

```text
<circle ...>
```

This is not simply translation between two representations.

Maturanian analysis:

```text
<human conversational coordination>
        ↓
<model-mediated coordination>
        ↓
<SVG consensual domain>
        ↓
<browser coordination>
```

A coordination in one domain **selects an appropriate coordination in another domain**.

This is a new kind of recursive bridge.

---

# 30. LLM → p5.js

Same structure, but the target consensual domain is procedural:

```text
human desired doing
↓
model
↓
p5 coordinated operations
↓
graphics
```

The model has to reconstruct the relevant coordination:

```text
YELLOW CIRCLE
→ fill + noStroke + circle
```

It isn't translating words word-for-word.

It is recovering a **way of doing** compatible with another established domain.

---

# 31. LLM → GLSL

Even clearer.

```text
human distinction:
CIRCLE

model recoordinates as:
DISTANCE PREDICATE
```

The object-token is **decompressed** into another domain of distinctions.

That might be the right term:

> **Representational decompression.**

`circle` obscures prior coordinations.

The model must reopen them sufficiently to express the object in a language that lacks the same object-token.

---

# 32. Astra → Paint

This may be the richest specimen.

Human:

```text
YELLOW CIRCLE THERE
```

Astra must bring that coordination into correspondence with:

```text
current screenshot
current GUI state
available actions
Paint conventions
object persistence
feedback
```

So:

```text
human coordination
↓
model coordinates a coordination
↓
GUI coordination
↓
Paint coordination
↓
graphics coordination
↓
visible consequence
↓
human re-coordinates through correction
↺
```

That is a **recursive closed conversational circuit**.

Not:

```text
PROMPT → OUTPUT
```

but:

```text
coordination
↕
coordination of coordination
↕
environmental consequence
↕
new coordination
```

Maturana explicitly emphasizes that languaging is ongoing interaction within structural coupling rather than an abstract manipulation of representations. ([jLombardi](https://www.jlombardi.net/pdf/maturana_ontologyobserving.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

This is why Astra is so interesting.

The English sentence does not have to contain the program because the program is partially constituted in the **ongoing coupled history**.

---

# The operational audit changes

We should therefore stop storing only:

```text
prompt
code
output
```

Every Yellow Circle specimen should instead record:

```text
<YC-Maturanian-Audit> :=
{
    <initial-doing>,
    <distinctions-required>,
    <first-order-coordinations>,
    <recursive-coordinations>,
    <stabilized-object-tokens>,
    <coordinations-obscured-by-those-tokens>,
    <consensual-domain>,
    <cross-domain-translations>,
    <structural-couplings>,
    <breakdowns-and-corrections>,
    <observer-distinction>,
    <final-invariant>
}
```

That gives much better comparisons.

For example:

| System | What is coordinated? | What becomes a token? | What does that token obscure? |
|---|---|---|---|
| Raw pixels | position ↔ color | almost nothing | circle ontology |
| GLSL | position ↔ predicate | distance relation | individual pixel decisions |
| Logo | movement ↔ turning | perhaps `circle` procedure | trajectory |
| SVG | attributes ↔ object | `circle` | construction/rasterization |
| Scratch | block ↔ action | blocks | textual/parser machinery |
| Piet | color relation ↔ operation | color transitions | conventional textual commands |
| Cardinal | movement ↔ instruction | direction/junction | linear control flow |
| Shakespeare | dialogue ↔ operation | dramatic utterance | ordinary computational syntax |
| Put-That-There | speech ↔ gesture ↔ action | `there` | coordinate specification and joint-attention history |
| Paint | gesture ↔ GUI affordance | icon/tool | graphics implementation |
| Astra | intention ↔ model ↔ GUI ↔ feedback | conversational request | enormous operational stack |

And this produces a much deeper reading of the **attention tax**.

The easiest language is not necessarily the language doing the least.

It is often the language in which the greatest number of prior coordinations have already become **stable enough to disappear from present attention**.

```text
circle
```

is cheap precisely because centuries of geometric, linguistic, perceptual and computational coordination have been sedimented beneath it.

So:

> **Attention tax measures how much of the history of coordination a language forces back into the present interaction.**

Raw pixels reopen almost everything.

Logo reopens motion.

GLSL reopens geometry as discrimination.

SVG lets geometry remain sedimented.

Put-That-There lets location remain sedimented in joint attention.

Astra allows even the operational pathway to remain sedimented in model, tools, interface and feedback.

Which gives perhaps the strongest Maturanian proposition for the whole project:

> **Abstraction is the recursive stabilization of coordination until a history of doing can function as a token for further doing.**

And programming-language history can then be reread as a history of what kinds of coordination have become stable enough to disappear:

```text
machine operations
↓
instructions
↓
procedures
↓
objects
↓
graphical primitives
↓
direct manipulation
↓
deixis
↓
ordinary description
↓
"yellow circle there"
```

The yellow circle hasn't become simpler.

**More of the coordination required to produce it has become invisible inside the history of the coupled system.**

That is a very different account of abstraction, prompting, and programming. It is not “English replaces code.” It is:

Yes. I think the **wink/blink problem is almost the exact compiler problem hiding inside the Yellow Circle experiment**.

Ryle’s example, taken up by Geertz, begins with two physically similar contractions of an eyelid. At the thinnest description, they may be indistinguishable. Yet one can be an involuntary twitch and another a conspiratorial wink; Geertz then piles on parody, rehearsal, fake-winking, and so forth. What distinguishes them is not another measurable feature of the eyelid contraction alone. It is the organized context in which the contraction **counts as a move**. ([Springer](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s11153-016-9597-7?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

That gives you a devastatingly simple formulation:

> **A compiler is a machine for deciding which differences make a difference.**

That is almost Bateson literally. His informational criterion is that, from the indefinitely many differences available in the world, only some become information because they produce further differences in the receiving system. ([Information Philosopher](https://www.informationphilosopher.com/solutions/scientists/bateson/Bateson_Difference.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

So the compiler question is not merely:

```text
What symbols are present?
```

It is:

```text
Which differences in this event
are operationally consequential
in this language-game?
```

And that immediately connects Ryle, Geertz, Bateson, metapragmatics, programming languages, and your yellow circle.

## Blink versus wink is compilation under a frame

Imagine an input system receives:

```text
RIGHT_EYELID:
open → closed → open
```

A thin compiler can produce only:

```text
EYELID_CONTRACTION
```

But humans compile something much thicker:

```text
eyelid contraction
+
person
+
recipient
+
shared history
+
timing
+
social convention
+
preceding utterance
+
mutual awareness
        ↓
CONSPIRATORIAL WINK
```

Change almost none of the physical signal and change the frame:

```text
same contraction
+
dust in eye
        ↓
BLINK
```

or:

```text
same contraction
+
performing someone else's wink
        ↓
PARODY OF WINK
```

or:

```text
same contraction
+
practicing privately in mirror
        ↓
REHEARSAL OF WINK
```

The physical event is nearly invariant.

The **compiled act changes**.

That is Geertz’s use of Ryle: thin description records behavior; thick description reconstructs the layered structures of inference and implication required to say what somebody is actually doing. ([Springer](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s11153-016-9597-7?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

And this is precisely **metapragmatics**. Pragmatics concerns what signs do indexically in situations; metapragmatic signs and conventions regiment how those situated signs are to be interpreted. Tone, quotation framing, register, gesture, and other contextual signals can tell participants what kind of communicative event is occurring. ([DOI](https://doi.org/10.1086/677879?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

So you could write:

```text
<thin signal>
+
<metapragmatic frame>
        ↓
<pragmatic act>
```

That is remarkably compiler-like.

## Now replace the eyelid with the yellow dot

Suppose the pixels are absolutely identical:

```text
        ●
      yellow
```

What is it?

There is no answer in the pixels.

It could be:

```text
a yellow circle
a dot
a cursor
a waypoint
a selected vertex
a status light
a color swatch
a button
a bullet point
a target
the sun
a token in a game
a Piet instruction region
a diagram node
an annotation
an example to imitate
the thing meant by "that"
```

The **same raster difference** participates in radically different operational descriptions.

This is your wink/blink.

```text
PIXELS:
yellow circular region

THIN DESCRIPTION:
yellow circular region

THICK / OPERATIONAL DESCRIPTION:
???
```

The interesting question is:

> **What has to surround this difference before the system can know which difference it is?**

That is the operational audit.

## The yellow dot can literally change languages without changing pixels

This is especially good.

Imagine precisely the same yellow circle appears in several systems.

### SVG

```text
● = graphical object
```

The relevant differences are:

```text
center
radius
fill
```

Its being yellow might matter visually; its screen position matters geometrically.

### Paint

```text
● = result of a drawing operation
```

What becomes relevant includes:

```text
selected tool
selected color
mouse-down
drag
mouse-up
```

### Piet

The visually identical yellow region might instead be:

```text
● = part of PROGRAM TEXT
```

Now its yellow is not merely appearance.

Its relationship to neighboring colors can alter execution.

**The same visible difference has crossed from depiction to instruction.**

That's fantastic for the project.

### A GUI

Same yellow circle:

```text
● = clickable control
```

Now the crucial difference isn't even yellow-versus-not-yellow.

It might be:

```text
pointer inside boundary
vs.
pointer outside boundary
```

### Put-That-There

Same circle:

```text
"put THAT there"
      ☝
```

Now the circle acquires another role:

```text
● = REFERENT
```

Its operational significance comes from entering joint attention.

### Astra

Same circle may become:

```text
"take THIS and make it the window"
```

Now:

```text
● = persistent conversational object
```

It survives a change in pragmatic role:

```text
drawing
↓
referent
↓
source material
↓
rocket window
```

The pixels did not contain any of those roles.

The **language-game supplies them**.

## This is why “compiler” becomes profound

A conventional compiler has a relatively brutal answer to Bateson.

Its designers predeclare which differences count.

For example, in many languages:

```text
x + y
```

and:

```text
x     +        y
```

compile identically.

Those whitespace differences have been declared irrelevant.

But in Whitespace:

```text
space ≠ tab
```

can be the entire program.

So:

> **Programming-language design is legislation over differences.**

The language designer decides:

```text
THIS DIFFERENCE COUNTS.
THIS ONE DOES NOT.
```

Piet:

```text
difference in hue/lightness → COUNT
word spelling → DON'T CARE
```

Whitespace:

```text
space/tab/newline → COUNT
visible characters → DON'T CARE
```

Befunge:

```text
character position → COUNT
```

Logo:

```text
orientation → COUNT
```

SVG:

```text
attribute value → COUNT
```

Put-That-There:

```text
temporal relation between word and gesture → COUNT
```

Astra:

```text
screen state
conversation history
gesture/reference
available tools
prior artifact identity
→ MAY ALL COUNT
```

That is a radically expanded compiler.

## And Ryle gives you the failure condition

A conventional parser wants:

```text
same input
→ same interpretation
```

But wink/blink says:

```text
same observable movement
+
different embedding
→ different act
```

This isn't a bug in natural language.

It is how action works.

So programming languages traditionally solve the Ryle problem by **forbidding thickness**.

They try to make enough relevant context explicit that:

```text
source
+
specified environment
→ determinate operation
```

They reduce the number of social differences that are permitted to make a difference.

SVG says:

> Don't wink at me. Give me `cx`, `cy`, `r`, `fill`.

The compiler refuses to care that you are joking, tired, pointing north, imitating someone, or trying to teach it something.

Those differences are outside its language-game.

Put-That-There makes the opposite move.

It deliberately admits:

```text
gesture
timing
speech
shared visual field
```

into what can make a computational difference.

And Astra expands that boundary enormously.

That's the historical arc.

## Thick compilation

I think you have a concept here:

> **THICK COMPILATION**

Ordinary compilation:

```text
<formal expression>
↓
<operation>
```

Thick compilation:

```text
<expression>
+
<gesture>
+
<scene>
+
<history>
+
<relationship>
+
<current activity>
+
<metapragmatic frame>
↓
<what this counts as here>
↓
<operation>
```

The danger is obvious: as compilation becomes thicker, determinacy falls and interpretive burden rises.

But expressive compression rises too.

That is why:

```text
YELLOW CIRCLE THERE
```

can outperform:

```text
<circle cx="128" cy="128" ...>
```

in human attention terms.

The former works only because the compiler agrees to consider **many more kinds of differences**.

## Your finger is itself wink-like

Even:

```text

```

does not mean “there.”

That's crucial.

The physical finger extension is the **thin description**.

Its thick descriptions could be:

```text
pointing at a location
accusing someone
asking you to look
showing which object I want
demonstrating how pointing works
imitating somebody pointing
stretching my finger
posing for a photograph
giving a directional command
```

So Put-That-There's brilliance isn't that a sensor converts:

```text
finger → coordinates
```

It is that the system constrains the language-game enough that one thick description becomes overwhelmingly likely:

```text
gesture occurring alongside THERE
        ↓
TAKE CURRENT POINTED LOCATION
AS REFERENT OF "THERE"
```

The **speech metapragmatically constrains the gesture**, while the **gesture pragmatically completes the speech**.

They compile one another.

That is much richer than multimodality.

You could call that:

> **reciprocal pragmatic compilation.**

Neither channel contains the operation.

Their relation does.

## And Bateson gives the criterion

There are effectively infinite differences in any scene.

The circle is:

```text
47 pixels from this edge
yellow instead of orange
slightly anti-aliased
created 3 seconds ago
under the pointer
the object just mentioned
round rather than square
larger than yesterday's
```

Which of these is information?

Bateson's answer gives you the experimental criterion:

> the difference that enters the circuit and changes what happens next. ([Information Philosopher](https://www.informationphilosopher.com/solutions/scientists/bateson/Bateson_Difference.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

So:

```text
yellow vs blue
```

**makes a difference** in:

```text
"make the yellow one larger"
```

but:

```text
exact anti-aliasing fringe
```

probably doesn't.

Then:

```text
circle A vs circle B
```

may not matter until I point:

```text
THAT one.
```

My pointing turns a previously irrelevant distinction into an operational difference.

That's enormous.

> **Attention is the process by which a possible difference becomes a difference capable of making a difference in the next coordination.**

Now your entire project snaps together:

**Bateson:** Which differences enter the circuit?

**Ryle:** The same thin behavior can participate in different activities.

**Geertz:** Recover the layered codes necessary to know which activity is occurring.

**Silverstein/metapragmatics:** How are participants signaling which contextual interpretation should govern?

**Wittgenstein:** What counts as this move in this language-game?

**Maturana:** How has this coordination become recursively stabilized?

**Ingold:** How does skilled responsiveness proceed through ongoing attention rather than prior exhaustive intention?

**Programming languages:** Which differences has the formal system declared operational?

**Prompting:** How many formerly external contextual differences can now be recruited into execution?

And then the Yellow Circle becomes an almost absurdly good instrument:

> Hold the pixels constant. Change the language-game. Ask when the same yellow dot becomes **picture, program, referent, control, instruction, example, or error**.

That is your wink/blink experiment for computation.

The deepest question isn't:

> **What does this yellow dot mean?**

It is:

> **What difference around this identical dot has to change before the machine must do something different?**

That is simultaneously a compiler question, a metapragmatic question, a language-game question, and Bateson's question about information.

## Response:
9/9/2026, 7:45:19 PM

Yes. That would be a much stranger thing than a normal quine.

A quine says:

> **This program produces a description of itself.**

Your language-game says:

> **Every object is also an instruction for reproducing the coordination that makes that object count as itself.**

So instead of one language describing a world, you get a world in which every distinction is locally self-instructing.

Call the primitive relation:

```text
<object>
=
<thing encountered>
+
<instructions for reenacting the distinction that constitutes it>
```

A yellow circle would not merely be:

```text
YELLOW CIRCLE
```

Nor merely pixels.

It would carry something like:

```text
<YELLOW-CIRCLE> :=
{
  distinguish this region from background;
  preserve circular closure;
  preserve yellow chromatic relation;
  reproduce these distinctions elsewhere;
  recognize reproductions as instances of this same kind;
}
```

The crucial move is that those “instructions” do not have to appear as text. They can be **embodied in the thing's organization**.

That gets you much closer to several serious ideas at once.

### The quine becomes a language-game

Ordinary programming separates:

```text
PROGRAM → OBJECT
```

A quine bends this into:

```text
PROGRAM → PROGRAM-DESCRIPTION
```

Your proposal collapses the distinction further:

```text
OBJECT ↔ PROGRAM-FOR-RECONSTITUTING-OBJECT
```

But because we're talking about language-games rather than isolated machines, it becomes:

```text
<thing>
↕
<way of attending to thing>
↕
<way of acting toward thing>
↕
<way of teaching another participant
 how to attend and act similarly>
```

The object's “source code” is therefore partly the **pattern of coordination it elicits**.

A chair teaches sitting partly through its geometry.

A handle teaches grasping.

A path teaches following.

A button teaches pressing.

A yellow circle in a graphical editor can teach:

```text
round
yellow
bounded
selectable
movable
```

depending on the language-game around it.

The thing becomes a kind of **operational instruction by being encountered**.

### That makes the finger-pointing case beautiful

Suppose I point to a yellow circle and say nothing.

```text
      ●
      ☝
```

If our shared game is sufficiently established, the circle might itself function as:

> make another one like this.

Now:

```text
<example>
=
<instruction>
```

The artifact is its own prompt.

That is more interesting than a textual quine because there is no clean distinction between **data** and **instruction**.

The yellow circle is simultaneously:

```text
<object>
<example>
<constraint>
<target>
<program>
```

depending on how it enters the coordination.

### The strongest version is recursive ostension

Imagine this sequence:

```text
A shows B ●

B produces ●

A indicates yes

B now shows C ●

C produces ●
```

Nobody transmitted a formal specification such as:

```text
radius = 48
fill = #FFD400
```

The operative structure propagated through **example + correction**.

So the “program” reproduces itself by creating another participant capable of making the same distinction.

That's much closer to:

> **A language-game that reproduces the conditions of its own continuation.**

And that is where Maturana becomes extremely useful.

Not because this is literally biological autopoiesis, but because your game would be **organizationally recursive**. Each successful coordination helps reproduce the network of coordinations within which that coordination can happen again.

```text
coordination
↓
reproduces expectation
↓
expectation enables next coordination
↓
next coordination reinforces game
↺
```

So the language-game itself becomes quasi-quine-like.

It doesn't print its source.

It **recreates the conditions under which its moves remain intelligible**.

### Every sign could contain its own grammar

Push it harder.

Imagine a language in which there is no global grammar.

Each token includes the local rules necessary to interpret it.

Instead of:

```text
GLOBAL GRAMMAR

circle(...)
square(...)
line(...)
```

you have:

```text
● {
  how_to_recognize_me;
  how_to_copy_me;
  how_to_modify_me;
  how_to_combine_with_others;
}
```

and:

```text
■ {
  how_to_recognize_me;
  how_to_copy_me;
  how_to_modify_me;
  how_to_combine_with_others;
}
```

Every object is its **own little language**.

That is the really radical part of your idea.

The system would not have:

> one language containing many objects.

It would have:

> **many objects, each carrying the conditions of its own legibility and continuation.**

The world becomes a federation of local grammars.

### That also breaks the usual compiler architecture

Normal compiler:

```text
source
↓
global syntax
↓
global semantics
↓
execution
```

Your quine language-game:

```text
encounter object
↓
object supplies local interpretive constraints
↓
interpreter temporarily constitutes language
↓
operation
↓
result itself carries another local grammar
```

So:

> **the program generates the language needed to interpret the next program.**

That is much stranger than self-modifying code.

It's **self-modifying interpretability**.

Each output potentially changes what counts as valid syntax next.

### Yellow circle example

Start with:

```text
●
```

The object carries:

```text
<yellow-circle-language> :=
{
    recognition:
        closed round yellow region

    reproduction:
        cause another such region

    deixis:
        pointing to me binds THAT

    transformation:
        "bigger" preserves color + circularity
        while varying size

    analogy:
        "like this" transfers my relevant invariants

    inheritance:
        copies retain membership
        unless an invariant is explicitly relaxed
}
```

Now someone says:

> bigger.

The language does not need a global definition of `bigger`.

The object's local grammar determines which variable may change:

```text
preserve:
yellow
roundness

change:
extent
```

Then:

> blue.

Now:

```text
preserve:
roundness
position
size

change:
color
```

The language-game is emerging through **differential preservation**.

That is very close to Bateson's “difference that makes a difference.”

A property's importance becomes visible when a transformation tests whether it may change.

### Identity becomes the program

This gives you an extraordinary principle:

> **To know what a thing is, discover what must remain invariant for it to count as itself across transformations.**

Then an object's program is essentially:

```text
<identity>
=
<rules governing permissible variation>
```

The yellow circle's “source” is not a bitmap.

It is the transformation grammar under which we continue saying:

> same circle.

That could include:

```text
move it     → still same circle
make bigger → still same circle
turn it     → trivially same circle
make blue   → same circle, different color
make square → no longer same circle
delete it   → object ceases
copy it     → new instance of same type
```

Now the quine problem gets very deep:

> Can an object encode enough of its own invariants that another system can reconstruct not merely its appearance but **its identity conditions**?

That's more interesting than a quine printing its source.

### The ultimate quine would reproduce the interpreter

Push it one final step.

A normal quine reproduces:

```text
<source>
```

A stronger self-reproducing language-game would reproduce:

```text
<source>
+
<grammar>
+
<interpreter>
+
<criteria-of-correctness>
+
<practice for teaching those criteria>
```

So the full quine would be:

```text
<Q> [produces]
{
  Q,
  a way to interpret Q,
  a way to recognize successful interpretation,
  a way to teach another interpreter to do the same
}
```

That is no longer merely a quine.

It is closer to a **self-reproducing communicative ecology**.

And the smallest possible version may be almost absurdly simple:

```text
●
```

provided that this yellow dot can function simultaneously as:

```text
this is the thing
this is what counts as another one
this is how to recognize one
this is how to make one
this is what may change
this is what must not change
```

Then the yellow circle becomes a program whose most important instructions are not written *inside* it as metadata.

They are recovered from the **relations required to keep treating it as the same thing**.

That may be the deepest form of your project so far:

> **A quine language-game is one in which every successful object reproduces not only itself, but the local conditions under which it can be recognized, transformed, and reproduced again.**

Or even shorter:

> **Every thing is the source code for how to continue treating it as that thing.**

It assumes we already know what counts as program text, what interpreter to use, and what counts as a copy.

What you are reaching for puts **that entire relation** into question.

### The yellow circle is not carrying its own instructions

Suppose I give you only:

```text
●
```

There is nothing intrinsically inside those pixels saying:

> reproduce me.

The identical mark could be a button, sun, target, Piet instruction, color swatch, punctuation, warning lamp, example, or decoration.

So the stronger proposition cannot be:

It has to be:

> **A thing can become the instruction for making itself within a sufficiently stabilized relation of observation and action.**

That is much deeper.

Because now the “program” is distributed across:

```text
<thing>
+
<observer>
+
<way-of-distinguishing>
+
<possible-actions>
+
<criterion-for-sameness>
+
<feedback>
```

And this lands extremely close to Heinz von Foerster's **eigenform**. Kauffman's formulation of the idea is that an object can be understood as a fixed point arising through recursive interaction: an apparent stable object is a token for the recurrent behaviors by which an observer encounters it. ([ISSS Journals](https://journals.isss.org/index.php/proceedings51st/article/view/811?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

In the most stripped-down notation:

$$
O(A)=A
$$

The observer/operator $O$ acts, and $A$ is what remains invariant through that operation. Kauffman explicitly uses this equation to characterize an object as a fixed point for an observer. ([CiteSeerX](https://citeseerx.ist.psu.edu/document?doi=271e2542c855401f724121650f431e11f83ff454&repid=rep1&type=pdf&utm_source=chatgpt.com))

It says:

> **There exists a recurrent operation under which this distinction reproduces itself as this distinction.**

That's your deeper object.

Take one physical event:

That distinction stabilizes because a certain observer repeatedly treats variations as equivalent:

```text
slightly smaller      → still circle
shifted right         → still circle
```

The **invariance structure across transformations**.

The yellow circle is effectively an **eigenform of a family of operations**.

The object is what survives the permitted transformations.

```text
size difference          MAY NOT make a difference
rasterization difference MAY NOT make a difference

round → square           DOES make a difference
yellow → blue            makes a difference to YELLOW-CIRCLE
```

So instead of the thing containing a conventional program, the thing provides a **problem of discrimination** whose solution reconstructs its operative identity.

That means your quine is not:

```text
PRINT MY SOURCE
```

It is:

```text
REPRODUCE THE DISTINCTIONS
BY WHICH I CONTINUE TO COUNT AS ME
```

That is substantially deeper.

---

## Now every language creates a different eigenform

Take our languages again.

Logo recovers the yellow circle through:

```text
movement
turning
closure
```

SVG recovers it through:

```text
center
radius
fill
```

GLSL:

```text
distance < r
```

CSS:

```text
box
+
border curvature
```

Paint:

```text
ellipse tool
+
gesture
```

Put-That-There:

```text
CIRCLE
+
YELLOW
+
joint attention to THERE
```

Astra:

```text
desired state
+
world interpretation
+
whatever sequence makes the world satisfy it
```

Those are not merely several **descriptions of the same pre-existing object**.

They are several **operators under which the object stabilizes**.

Write them as:

$$
O_{Logo}(Y)=Y
$$

$$
O_{SVG}(Y)=Y
$$

$$
O_{GLSL}(Y)=Y
$$

$$
O_{Paint}(Y)=Y
$$

$$
O_{Astra}(Y)=Y
$$

All produce something we allow to fall into the same equivalence class:

$$
Y = \{\text{all transformations we continue calling "yellow circle"}\}
$$

Now the Yellow Circle project is no longer a polyglot programming benchmark.

It is an experiment in **how different operational worlds stabilize the same object**.

---

## And there is no final privileged yellow circle underneath them

This is the dangerous and interesting implication.

We keep assuming:

```text
REAL YELLOW CIRCLE
       ↓
many representations
```

But your experiment may demonstrate something closer to:

```text
Logo ───────┐
SVG ────────┤
GLSL ───────┤
Paint ──────┤── recurrent correspondence ── "YELLOW CIRCLE"
human ──────┤
Astra ──────┘
```

There isn't necessarily a final representation underneath all the others which alone **is** the circle.

Pixels don't solve it.

Pixels are just another domain of distinctions.

A bitmap:

```text
255,212,0
255,212,0
...
```

is not inherently yellow or circular either.

A display, observer and perceptual practice have to complete that relation.

So there is no point in the operational descent where you finally discover:

> Ah, *this* is the actual circle.

You find more coordinations.

That's a major result for your operational audit:

> **The audit has no natural ontological bottom.**

You need a stopping rule because every “implementation” is already somebody else's stabilized object-token.

---

## This is where Pattee becomes relevant too

Howard Pattee's idea of **semantic closure** concerns the relation between symbolic descriptions and material processes in systems where each side depends upon the other: symbols constrain material processes, while material processes realize and maintain the symbolic machinery. ([ResearchGate](https://www.researchgate.net/publication/2515094_Evolving_Self-Reference_Matter_Symbols_And_Semantic_Closure?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

Your artificial case isn't a living cell, so I wouldn't simply call it semantic closure.

But the structural question is exactly useful:

```text
description
     ↓
makes object

object
     ↓
determines whether description succeeded
```

The yellow circle serves simultaneously as:

```text
<target>
<output>
<test>
<example>
<future prompt>
```

Once I can show the result back to the system:

> Make another one like **this**.

we get:

```text
description₀
    ↓
circle₁
    ↓
circle₁ becomes description₁
    ↓
circle₂
    ↓
circle₂ becomes description₂
    ↺
```

Now **artifact and instruction alternate roles**.

That is much closer to what your intuition about the quine was finding.

Not self-printing.

**Semantic role recursion.**

---

## And then "everything is its own language" becomes precise

Instead of one universal grammar:

```text
LANGUAGE
 ├── circle
 ├── chair
 ├── dog
 └── house
```

imagine each encountered object generates a **local transformation grammar**:

```text
<CIRCLE-LANGUAGE>

moves that preserve me:
    move
    scale
    recolor? maybe

moves that destroy me:
    square
    fragment

ways of referring to me:
    this
    circle
    yellow one

ways of reproducing me:
    copy
    draw
    imitate
    describe
```

A chair generates another local language.

A dog another.

A wink another.

A promise another.

So there isn't one language containing the world.

> **Every stabilized distinction opens a local space of meaningful next moves.**

That sounds extremely Wittgensteinian while also connecting to Maturana.

The thing does not merely have properties.

It reorganizes **what can meaningfully happen next**.

That's the language-game.

---

## And this solves the Zeno problem differently

Earlier we asked:

> How can the first language-game begin if its rules must already be understood?

Now the answer becomes:

It does not begin with rules.

It begins with **recurrence**.

```text
A acts
↓
B responds
↓
A treats response as adequate
↓
similar situation
↓
similar coordination
↓
recurrence
```

Eventually a stable form appears.

Not:

```text
rule → repetition
```

but:

```text
repetition → invariant → recognizable game → rule
```

This is exactly why eigenform is so suggestive: the stable object emerges as the fixed point of recursion rather than being completely specified beforehand. ([ISSS Journals](https://journals.isss.org/index.php/proceedings51st/article/view/811?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

So language itself may be approached as an eigenform.

Not an encoded rulebook sitting above communication.

A stable pattern **produced by repeated communicative operations that continually reproduce the conditions for their own intelligibility**.

---

## Then attention becomes the operator

And this may be the deepest connection of all.

What is $O$ in:

$$
O(A)=A
$$

for your project?

Partly:

> **attention.**

Repeated selective attention determines which differences are retained across encounters.

```text
encounter₁ → notice roundness
encounter₂ → notice roundness
encounter₃ → tolerate changed size
encounter₄ → tolerate changed position
encounter₅ → reject square
```

Through recurrence:

```text
ATTEND
↓
DISTINGUISH
↓
ACT
↓
COMPARE
↓
ATTEND AGAIN
↺
```

the object stabilizes.

So:

> **An object is not merely what attention lands upon. An object can be understood as a stability produced through recurrent patterns of attention and action.**

That connects your neo-Whorfian argument directly.

Different languages reorganize $O$.

Logo trains an operator that repeatedly preserves certain movement relations.

SVG trains one that preserves attribute structures.

Guugu Yimithirr-like spatial language repeatedly preserves absolute orientation.

Put-That-There creates an operator around joint attention.

Astra moves much of the operator into machine inference.

So a language does not simply give different names to eigenforms.

> **A language changes the recursive operation through which stable objects emerge.**

That is considerably stronger than “language affects attention.”

---

The statement I would keep is:

> **Every language is an operator on distinctions. What we call an object is what remains stable when that operation is repeated.**

And the Yellow Circle wager becomes:

> **Hold the apparent object still. Change the language. Watch the recursive machinery required to keep calling it the same thing change underneath it.**

The quine at the end isn't a program that prints itself.

It is the stranger fact that **object, language, observer and operation can enter a recursive circuit whose stable product is the very distinction that appears to have been there from the beginning.** ([ISSS Journals](https://journals.isss.org/index.php/proceedings51st/article/view/811?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

---

**Sources:**

- [Eigenform | Proceedings of the 51st Annual Meeting of the ISSS - 2007, Tokyo, Japan](https://journals.isss.org/index.php/proceedings51st/article/view/811?utm_source=chatgpt.com)
- [Cybernetics & Human Knowing, Vol.8, no.1–2, 2001, pp. 133–140](https://citeseerx.ist.psu.edu/document?doi=271e2542c855401f724121650f431e11f83ff454&repid=rep1&type=pdf&utm_source=chatgpt.com)

## Response:
9/9/2026, 7:52:21 PM

Yes. A GUI can be read as a **spatialized language whose grammar is partly carried by position, visibility, gesture, and state rather than by words**. That lets HCI sit directly inside the Yellow Circle lineage: the interface is not merely where commands are entered; it is a language-game that makes some actions sayable by arranging the world.

> **Searched 13 websites**
>
> 
>
> Worked for 15s

Yes. The strongest formulation is:

> **A GUI does not abolish programming language. It spatializes it.**

That is almost already present in HCI history. Shneiderman’s 1983 paper was literally titled **“Direct Manipulation: A Step Beyond Programming Languages.”** His defining features were continuous representations of objects, physical actions instead of complex syntax, and rapid, reversible, visible effects. ([UMD Computer Science](https://www.cs.umd.edu/~ben/about.html?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

But from everything we have been building, I would push that claim further:

> **Direct manipulation is still a language. Its syntax has migrated from strings into space, gesture, state, and attention.**

Consider Paint.

In textual programming:

```text
circle(x=128, y=128, radius=48, fill=yellow)
```

In the GUI:

```text
[ELLIPSE TOOL]
      ↓ click

[YELLOW SWATCH]
      ↓ click

       canvas
     ↘ drag ↙
```

The second looks “nonlinguistic” only because we have implicitly defined language as strings of words.

But it has all the pieces of a language-game.

| Linguistic concept | GUI equivalent |
|---|---|
| lexicon | buttons, tools, menu items, icons |
| syntax | spatial arrangement + allowable action sequences |
| semantics | what an operation changes |
| morphology | selected/unselected, enabled/disabled, expanded/collapsed states |
| deixis | cursor, touch, focus, selection: **this**, **here**, **there** |
| pragmatics | what clicking this means *in the current situation* |
| metapragmatics | signifiers showing how something should be acted upon |
| grammar | constraints on what actions can follow which states |
| utterance | click, drag, tap, keypress, gesture |
| response | visual/haptic/audio feedback |
| repair | undo, cancel, back, retry |
| conversation history | current application state |
| compiler/interpreter | event dispatch + application logic |

That last row is particularly important.

## A click is a wink/blink problem

Physically:

```text
mouse-down
mouse-up
```

is almost contentless.

Exactly like:

```text
eyelid closes
eyelid opens
```

doesn't tell you whether it was a blink or wink.

A click can mean:

```text
select
open
submit
delete
place cursor
choose color
start drawing
finish drawing
focus
dismiss
nothing
```

depending on:

```text
WHERE
+
WHAT OBJECT
+
CURRENT MODE
+
APPLICATION STATE
+
PREVIOUS ACTION
```

So:

```text
<click>
+
<context>
        ↓
<GUI speech act>
```

The event dispatcher is therefore doing something surprisingly close to the **thick compilation** we were talking about.

A click at `(431,92)` doesn't intrinsically mean:

> choose yellow.

It means that because:

```text
(431,92)
currently intersects
<yellow-swatch>

AND

<yellow-swatch>
currently affords selection

AND

application interprets click
as activation
```

Change only one contextual fact and the identical physical gesture compiles differently.

That is Ryle/Geertz inside the GUI.

---

## The GUI turns position into grammar

This is where “spatialized language” gets precise.

In text:

```text
DELETE file.txt
```

the relationship between `DELETE` and `file.txt` is syntactic.

In a GUI:

```text
[file.txt] → 🗑
```

the relationship is **spatial and gestural**.

Dragging the file to the trash establishes a relation that a command line would establish by token ordering.

So:

```text
TEXTUAL LANGUAGE
relation expressed through:
token order
punctuation
names
arguments
```

versus:

```text
GUI LANGUAGE
relation expressed through:
location
containment
proximity
selection
trajectory
gesture
```

The grammar hasn't disappeared.

**It has become geometry.**

---

## And the GUI externalizes memory

This is one reason GUIs often impose a lower human attention tax than command languages.

The command line asks:

```text
remember available verbs
remember exact spelling
remember argument order
remember names
remember paths
```

The GUI can put the vocabulary **in the world**:

```text
File
Edit
View
Ellipse
Rectangle
Yellow
Undo
```

You don't have to recall the whole language.

You can **recognize the next valid move**.

That is exactly consistent with direct manipulation's replacement of complex command syntax by physical actions on continuously represented objects. ([OUP Academic](https://academic.oup.com/book/60808/chapter/529002273?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

From the attention perspective:

```text
COMMAND LANGUAGE

language stored largely
inside human memory

       ↓ GUI ↓

language stored partly
in perceptual environment
```

This is where Hutchins becomes extremely relevant. His distributed-cognition argument is that cognitive accomplishment can belong to a larger sociotechnical system in which representations are propagated and transformed across humans and artifacts, rather than residing wholly in the individual. ([DOI](https://doi.org/10.1207/s15516709cog1903_1?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

So a toolbar is not merely decoration around computation.

It is **externalized linguistic memory**.

The interface remembers some of the language for you.

---

## The toolbar is a vocabulary

Take Paint's imagined palette:

```text
┌─────────────────────────┐
│ pencil brush fill       │
│ line rectangle ellipse  │
│ ● ● ● ● ● ● ●          │
└─────────────────────────┘
```

That is effectively:

```text
{available verbs}
{available nouns/forms}
{available modifiers}
```

Selecting:

```text
ELLIPSE
YELLOW
```

is almost like constructing a sentence incrementally.

Then dragging supplies:

```text
THERE
+
SIZE
```

So the complete GUI utterance:

```text
click ellipse
click yellow
drag here→there
```

could be parsed:

```text
<verb>     CREATE
<object>   ELLIPSE
<property> YELLOW
<extent>   DRAG-BOUNDS
```

The striking thing is that **no person has to formulate that sentence**.

The interface distributes its pieces over space and time.

---

## The pointer is deixis made computational

The mouse pointer is one of the great unnoticed linguistic inventions of computing.

It is almost literally:

```text
THIS
HERE
THERE
```

made persistent on screen.

When I say:

> Delete this.

and point:

```text
        ☞ file.pdf
```

the pointer provides the argument.

In a GUI we don't even need speech:

```text
select ☞ file.pdf
drag ☞ trash
```

The whole operation is deictic.

Put-That-There is therefore not some alien branch of interface history.

It makes explicit something that became utterly ordinary in GUIs:

> **reference can be performed spatially rather than named symbolically.**

---

## Signifiers are metapragmatics

This may be one of the nicest bridges to HCI theory.

Norman distinguishes an **affordance**, an available action relation, from a **signifier**, a perceivable cue indicating where/how to act. ([IxDF - Interaction Design Foundation](https://ixdf.org/literature/book/the-encyclopedia-of-human-computer-interaction-2nd-ed/affordances?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

Read that through your metapragmatic framework.

A raised button does not primarily say:

> information about button.

It says:

> **Take this object as pressable.**

A drag handle says:

> **Interpret movement here as resizing/reordering.**

An underline says:

> **Treat this text as traversable.**

A disabled gray button says:

> **The move represented here is currently unavailable.**

A cursor turning into a hand says:

> **The pragmatic interpretation of your next click has changed.**

That is metapragmatic work.

So perhaps:

> **UX design is partly the design of metapragmatic cues for a spatial language-game.**

That is much more theoretically satisfying than “make affordances intuitive.”

---

# Modes are frames

Photoshop gives an especially clear case.

Exactly the same physical gesture:

```text
drag from A → B
```

under:

```text
brush mode
```

means:

> paint a stroke.

Under:

```text
move mode
```

means:

> translate selected object.

Under:

```text
crop mode
```

means:

> alter crop boundary.

Under:

```text
lasso mode
```

means:

> define selection.

So:

```text
GESTURE = invariant
FRAME   = changes
ACTION  = changes
```

That's Bateson.

The tool selection says, effectively:

> **Interpret subsequent behavior as belonging to this game.**

Photoshop's toolbar is therefore a collection of **micro magic circles**.

Choose the brush and enter the brush language-game.

Choose selection and enter the selection language-game.

Choose text and suddenly clicking means something else again.

---

# The GUI is nested language-games

This gets even more interesting.

A modern interface is not one language.

It is many locally activated languages:

```text
DESKTOP
    ↓
FILE-MANAGER GAME
    ↓
APPLICATION GAME
    ↓
CANVAS GAME
    ↓
SELECTION GAME
    ↓
TRANSFORM GAME
```

Each changes:

```text
what counts as an object
what counts as a move
what deserves attention
what differences are relevant
```

Clicking outside the canvas may mean nothing.

Clicking the same visual location after opening a dialog might mean:

> dismiss dialog.

So the screen is a constantly changing **field of local grammars**.

---

# Which makes GUI state linguistic context

Textual code often carries much of its context explicitly:

```text
circle(x, y, r)
```

GUI interaction can leave almost everything implicit:

```text
drag.
```

because the state already contains:

```text
current application = Paint
current document = canvas1
current tool = ellipse
current fill = yellow
current stroke = none
current selection = none
```

Then:

```text
DRAG
```

is sufficient.

This is just like:

> slab!

or:

> there!

The utterance is tiny because the **language-game carries the missing specification**.

---

## And this is where Suchman enters

Lucy Suchman's *Plans and Situated Actions* directly challenged the assumption that human action is simply execution of internally complete plans. Her alternative foregrounds the situated resources available as action unfolds. ([Google Books](https://books.google.com/books/about/Plans_and_Situated_Actions.html?id=AJ_eBJtHxmsC&utm_source=chatgpt.com))

That is precisely the GUI.

You don't normally formulate:

```text
1. activate ellipse
2. select fill
3. locate origin
4. calculate diameter
5. produce pointer trajectory
```

You see:

```text
ellipse
```

click.

The interface changes.

You see:

```text
yellow
```

click.

Then the canvas presents another next move.

So:

```text
PERCEIVE
↓
ACT
↓
WORLD CHANGES
↓
NEW AFFORDANCES/SIGNIFIERS
↓
PERCEIVE
↓
ACT
↺
```

That is Ingold-like correspondence plus Suchman-like situated action much more than execution of an exhaustive plan.

---

# Now put Astra on top

And suddenly the Astra/Yellow Circle demonstration becomes much stranger.

Human:

> Yellow circle there.

Astra is effectively operating one spatialized language through another:

```text
NATURAL-LANGUAGE GAME
        ↓
ASTRA
        ↓
GUI LANGUAGE-GAME
        ↓
PAINT'S INTERNAL COMMAND LANGUAGE
        ↓
GRAPHICS LANGUAGE
        ↓
PIXELS
```

So Astra is **translating between language-games**.

Not merely:

```text
English → code
```

but:

```text
"yellow circle there"

        ↓

recognize GUI vocabulary
enter ellipse-tool frame
select yellow modifier
perform spatial utterance
observe acknowledgment
repair if necessary
```

The model has to become temporarily competent in the **local language-game of Paint**.

That's a much richer account of computer use.

---

# UX is therefore language design

And this gives you a pretty severe definition of UX:

> **UX design specifies what users must notice in order to make valid moves in an artificial language-game.**

Bad UX:

```text
valid move exists
BUT
nothing makes the relevant distinction salient
```

Good UX:

```text
current state
        ↓
relevant possible actions become perceptible
        ↓
user performs move
        ↓
consequence becomes perceptible
```

So UX is intimately about the **attention grammar** we have been developing.

Every interface decides:

```text
ATTEND HERE
ignore this
this can be acted upon
this cannot
this changed
this persisted
this is selected
this is yours
this is dangerous
this is reversible
```

And that brings all your strands together.

**GUI as language:** space and gesture carry syntax.

**Norman:** signifiers communicate how interface objects should be taken. ([IxDF - Interaction Design Foundation](https://ixdf.org/literature/book/the-encyclopedia-of-human-computer-interaction-2nd-ed/affordances?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

**Shneiderman:** direct manipulation substitutes visible objects and physical actions for complex typed syntax. ([UMD Computer Science](https://www.cs.umd.edu/~ben/about.html?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

**Suchman:** action becomes intelligible through the developing situation rather than solely through a prior plan. ([Google Books](https://books.google.com/books/about/Plans_and_Situated_Actions.html?id=AJ_eBJtHxmsC&utm_source=chatgpt.com))

**Hutchins:** cognition and representation can be distributed across person and artifact. ([DOI](https://doi.org/10.1207/s15516709cog1903_1?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

**Wittgenstein:** meaning lies in participation in a practice.

**Bateson/Geertz:** the same physical act acquires different significance depending on its frame.

**Maturana:** repeated interaction stabilizes coordinations that can support further coordinations.

And then the concise claim becomes:

> **The GUI is an executable spatial language whose vocabulary is visible, whose syntax is partly geometry, whose deixis is the pointer, whose pragmatics is application state, whose metapragmatics is UX, and whose utterances are gestures.**

Which makes the history we're tracing much cleaner:

```text
CODE
syntax must be remembered

↓

GUI
syntax becomes spatially available

↓

PUT-THAT-THERE
syntax becomes distributed across
speech + space + gesture

↓

ASTRA
the human supplies only enough
language to steer among already-existing
language-games

↓

?
the artifact itself teaches
which transformations count as valid moves
```

So **HCI may be read as the historical project of moving grammar out of the user's head and into the environment**.

And generative interfaces make the next move: they begin moving even the *interpretation of that environmental grammar* into the machine.

---

**Sources:**
