Yes. There is a coherent historical argument here:

> **A large part of the difficulty of software has always been a language-coordination problem: how to preserve a human concern while it passes through people, representations, interfaces, specifications, programs, and machines that attend to different distinctions.**

Brooks, Jobs, Rittel, Schön, Norman, Nielsen, Winograd/Flores, and human-centered design can all be read as different attempts to deal with that problem.

### Brooks: software is hard because thought has to remain coordinated

Brooks is almost uncannily close to your operational-audit argument. In *No Silver Bullet*, he says the hard part of software is not syntax but constructing, specifying, designing, and testing an abstract structure of interlocking concepts. Syntax errors are minor beside conceptual errors. He identifies complexity, conformity, changeability, and especially **invisibility** as essential difficulties. ([ResearchGate](https://www.researchgate.net/publication/220477127_No_Silver_Bullet_Essence_and_Accidents_of_Software_Engineering?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

Now reread that through attention.

Software asks somebody to hold:

```text
user's situation
desired behavior
data model
state
exceptions
interfaces
dependencies
future changes
machine constraints
```

in sufficient correspondence that one artifact can satisfy them simultaneously.

That is an immense **coordination of attention**.

And *The Mythical Man-Month* adds the social problem. As people increase, potential communication relationships grow roughly as $n(n-1)/2$. Brooks explicitly describes organization as an attempt to reduce the amount of communication and coordination required, while his idea of **conceptual integrity** tries to keep a system governed by a coherent set of design ideas rather than many independently reasonable ones. ([SuperSummary](https://www.supersummary.com/the-mythical-man/important-quotes/?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

So Brooks's problem can be rewritten:

> **How many attentive minds can participate in constructing one operational world before maintaining correspondence among their distinctions costs more than their additional labor contributes?**

That's much more interesting than “meetings are expensive.”

A programmer notices one set of differences.

A database engineer another.

A customer another.

A manager another.

A designer another.

The machine another.

Software fails when those distinctions stop corresponding.

### The real program has to survive multiple languages

Imagine one tiny requirement:

> Put a yellow circle there.

It can travel through an organization like this:

```text
USER
"I need a yellow circle there."

        ↓

RESEARCHER
"user needs immediate visual placement"

        ↓

PRODUCT MANAGER
"create shape placement feature"

        ↓

UX DESIGNER
[ellipse tool] + [color palette] + [canvas]

        ↓

ENGINEER
createEllipse(bounds, fill)

        ↓

GRAPHICS API
path / rasterization

        ↓

MACHINE
pixels

        ↓

USER
"Yes. That's what I meant."
```

That final sentence is extraordinary.

Only **after the whole chain executes** can the beginning sometimes be judged.

So software is a peculiar compiler pipeline in which the source language may not yet completely know what it means.

---

# Jobs's problem is precisely that

The Steve Jobs quote is real, not merely internet folklore. In a 1998 *BusinessWeek* interview he said that for something complicated it is difficult to design by focus group because people often **“don't know what they want until you show it to them.”** ([WIRED](https://www.wired.com/2006/03/steve-jobs-best-quotes-ever/?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

Read this carefully.

It doesn't necessarily mean:

> users are stupid; genius designer knows better.

The theoretically interesting reading is:

> **Some intentions cannot become sufficiently discriminated until a possible world is encountered.**

Before artifact:

```text
"I want something better."
```

After prototype:

```text
"No, not that."
"That part."
"Like this."
"Keep that."
"Move this."
"Yes."
```

The artifact creates a language that **did not exist before the artifact**.

That means design is not simply:

```text
intention → representation
```

It is:

```text
partial intention
      ↓
prototype
      ↓
new perceptual distinctions
      ↓
more articulated intention
      ↓
new prototype
      ↺
```

Schön says something very close theoretically. His designer proceeds through **seeing-moving-seeing**; intentions themselves evolve during interaction with the materials, unintended consequences are discovered, and designers can inhabit divergent “design worlds.” ([ScienceDirect](https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/095070519290020G?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

So the prototype is not merely a test of an already-complete idea.

> **The prototype produces distinctions with which the idea can subsequently be thought.**

That's neo-Whorfian design.

---

# Rittel: sometimes you cannot even finish saying the problem before solving it

This is also why Rittel and Webber's **wicked problem** formulation belongs here.

Their famous first property is that there is **no definitive formulation** of a wicked problem. More radically, they say that the information needed to understand such a problem depends upon one's idea for solving it. Problem formulation and solution therefore develop together. ([DOI](https://doi.org/10.1007/bf01405730?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

That's almost exactly the Jobs/Schön problem in another vocabulary:

```text
WHAT DO YOU WANT?
      ↕
WHAT COULD WE MAKE?
```

Neither side is fully determined first.

So a classical requirements pipeline:

```text
DISCOVER REQUIREMENTS
↓
FREEZE REQUIREMENTS
↓
IMPLEMENT
```

is potentially trying to force a **dialogical problem into a compiler architecture**.

It assumes the source program exists before compilation.

For many design problems, it doesn't.

The source itself changes when confronted with an execution.

---

# HCD is therefore a language-game for making intentions articulable

This changes the interpretation of human-centered design.

Interviews, observations, sketches, wireframes, prototypes, journey maps, usability tests aren't merely miscellaneous “methods.”

They are **representational languages for coordinating attention**.

Each lets participants notice something that another representation makes expensive to see.

```text
INTERVIEW
makes concerns speakable

FIELD OBSERVATION
makes unspoken practice visible

JOURNEY MAP
makes temporal relations visible

WIREFRAME
makes hierarchy and sequence discussable

PROTOTYPE
makes possible interaction experienceable

USABILITY TEST
makes breakdown visible

WORKING SOFTWARE
makes consequences real
```

So HCD can be described as:

> **a succession of language-games constructed to make previously unavailable differences discussable.**

That's why showing somebody a prototype can elicit knowledge that asking them a question did not.

The prototype has enlarged the language-game.

Before:

> What should it do?

After:

> **Not like that. Like this.**

`THIS` contains enormous information because both participants can now jointly attend to an artifact.

We are back at Put-That-There.

---

# Norman's gulfs are translation taxes

Norman/Hutchins/Hollan's **gulf of execution** is:

> I know what I want. How do I express an action this system accepts?

The **gulf of evaluation** is:

> Something happened. How do I determine what the system did and whether it corresponds to what I wanted? ([Nielsen Norman Group](https://www.nngroup.com/articles/two-ux-gulfs-evaluation-execution/?lm=digital-wayfinding&pt=youtubevideo&utm_source=chatgpt.com))

Those are almost literally two compiler problems:

```text
GULF OF EXECUTION

<human intention>
        ↓
???
        ↓
<valid machine action>
```

and:

```text
GULF OF EVALUATION

<machine state>
        ↓
???
        ↓
<humanly intelligible state>
```

So UX sits between two translations:

```text
HUMAN → MACHINE
   execution gulf

MACHINE → HUMAN
   evaluation gulf
```

A good interface makes each translation cheaper.

That is **attention tax reduction**.

---

# Nielsen's heuristics become a grammar of attention

This reinterpretation gets surprisingly precise.

Nielsen's heuristics include visibility of system status, match between system and real world, user control and freedom, consistency, error prevention, recognition rather than recall, minimalist design, and support for recognizing and recovering from errors. ([Nielsen Norman Group](https://www.nngroup.com/articles/ten-usability-heuristics/?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

Read them as linguistic laws.

**Visibility of system status**

> The machine must continually **say what state it is in**.

Without it the human must infer hidden state.

**Match between system and real world**

> Use a language-game the human already inhabits.

Reduce translation tax.

**Consistency and standards**

> Stabilize grammar.

The same sign should not unpredictably compile into different acts.

**Recognition rather than recall**

> Put the vocabulary into the environment rather than requiring the human to memorize it.

NN/g explicitly describes recognition as easier than retrieving possible commands from memory. ([Nielsen Norman Group](https://www.nngroup.com/articles/audio-signifiers-voice-interaction/?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

That's exactly our GUI argument:

```text
CLI:
human stores vocabulary

GUI:
environment stores vocabulary
```

**Error prevention**

> Make malformed utterances impossible or expensive.

A disabled button is effectively a parser saying:

```text
THIS MOVE IS NOT GRAMMATICAL NOW.
```

**User control and freedom**

> Every conversation needs repair.

`Undo` is almost a conversational:

> No, I didn't mean that.

**Help users recognize and recover from errors**

> When compilation fails, expose enough of the interpretive failure that speaker and system can regain coordination.

**Aesthetic and minimalist design**

And this one connects directly to your attention theory.

> Do not put irrelevant distinctions into competition with relevant ones.

It is essentially a **rule of attentional irrelevance**.

So Nielsen's heuristics can be reread as:

> **How should we design an artificial language-game so that humans spend as little scarce attention as possible figuring out how to speak it and determining whether they were understood?**

That's a much stronger theoretical description of usability.

---

# Why software's language taxes became so wicked

Because there was never just one language.

There were nested translations:

```text
lived concern
↓
user vocabulary
↓
research vocabulary
↓
requirements vocabulary
↓
design vocabulary
↓
interface vocabulary
↓
architecture vocabulary
↓
programming language
↓
framework/API vocabulary
↓
protocol
↓
machine operations
```

Every boundary poses the Ryle/Geertz problem:

> **Which difference in the previous language is the difference that must make a difference here?**

The user says:

> fast.

Does that compile to:

```text
latency < 100 ms?
fewer clicks?
instant feedback?
background loading?
animation feels responsive?
don't make me think?
```

The user says:

> simple.

Does that mean:

```text
fewer features?
fewer visible features?
familiar terminology?
strong defaults?
progressive disclosure?
one workflow?
```

The user says:

> secure.

Now the translation problem becomes enormous.

These words aren't defective requirements because ordinary language is sloppy.

They belong to **different language-games whose relevant distinctions have not yet been coordinated**.

And Brooks's “conceptual integrity” can now be understood as keeping those translations from fragmenting into incompatible local games.

---

# Software development is therefore metacommunication all the way down

Most software-development artifacts don't directly make the product.

They coordinate **how other people should coordinate around making the product**.

Consider:

```text
requirements document
design system
API contract
type definition
ticket
wireframe
code review
test
style guide
architecture diagram
prototype
```

These are all largely **coordination-of-coordination artifacts**.

A design system says:

> When we encounter this kind of situation, use this shared distinction.

An API says:

> If you want this operation, express it in this form.

A type system says:

> These differences are operationally relevant; those combinations are invalid.

A test says:

> This difference in behavior counts as failure.

A wireframe says:

> Attend to these relationships before spending attention on these others.

A code review says:

> Interpret this implementation against these communal criteria.

That makes software engineering almost a gigantic exercise in **metapragmatic infrastructure**.

---

# And Winograd/Flores were already standing directly here

The language/action perspective explicitly treats **language as action** and applied that premise to computer-supported cooperative work. Winograd's 1987 paper introduced *The Coordinator*, a system built around communicative acts and commitments rather than treating information systems merely as containers for transmitted data. ([Taylor & Francis Online](https://www.tandfonline.com/doi/abs/10.1207/s15327051hci0301_2?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

Their question was effectively:

> What social action is this utterance accomplishing?

That's the same move you're now making against the prompt:

```text
"yellow circle there"
```

Do not ask merely:

> What information does the sentence encode?

Ask:

> **What coordination does saying this make possible now?**

---

# So the historical arc changes

We usually narrate software history as:

```text
machine language
→ assembly
→ high-level languages
→ GUI
→ web
→ mobile
→ AI
```

Your attention/language-game account gives another history:

```text
HUMAN MUST ATTEND TO MACHINE

machine instructions
        ↓

HUMAN AND MACHINE SHARE ABSTRACTIONS

high-level languages
        ↓

ENVIRONMENT CARRIES SOME GRAMMAR

GUI / direct manipulation
        ↓

DESIGNERS BUILD SHARED LANGUAGE GAMES

HCI / UX / HCD
        ↓

ARTIFACT HELPS DISCOVER INTENTION

prototype / iterative design
        ↓

MACHINE INCREASINGLY ATTENDS TO HUMAN CONTEXT

Put-That-There → multimodality → agents
        ↓

HUMAN SAYS

"yellow circle there"
```

Viewed this way, the history of HCI is partly an attempt to solve a brutal asymmetry:

> **For decades humans paid most of the cost of making themselves legible to computers.**

Memorize commands.

Learn filenames.

Learn coordinates.

Learn APIs.

Learn syntax.

Learn menus.

Learn modes.

Learn the programmer's ontology.

Norman and Nielsen tried to transfer some of that tax into the artifact.

Human-centered design transferred more into designers and research practices.

Direct manipulation transferred some into spatial environments.

And models now promise to transfer an enormous amount into machine inference.

But your recurring question remains:

> **When the human no longer pays a distinction, who does?**

That may be the connection between Brooks and your Yellow Circle.

Brooks's communication overhead was partly the cost of keeping **many human minds mutually compilable** while constructing an invisible formal object.

The newest systems try to insert a model between those language-games:

```text
user
"I want this"

↕

model
translation / inference / repair

↕

design representation

↕

code

↕

running world

↕

user
"yes, that"
```

Potentially the model becomes a **coordination compiler**.

And that is much more consequential than “AI writes code.”

> **The historical promise of AI software development is that the machine may absorb part of the enormous linguistic and attentional tax previously required to keep users, designers, programmers, representations, and executable systems in correspondence.**

The harder question is whether it actually eliminates that tax, or merely hides it inside inference, verification, and the accumulated language-games encoded in the model.

That is where Brooks meets the yellow circle. The enduring software problem was never merely **how to write the instructions**. It was how to keep many different descriptions of what we are trying to do sufficiently coordinated that, after everything executes, someone can finally point and say:

> **Yes. That.**

---

## Response:
9/9/2026, 7:55:59 PM

Yes. The inversion is important: software has usually treated the **human as the source language that must be translated into machine-manageable surrogates**: requirement, persona, user story, task flow, state, metric. Those artifacts are not the human. They are jigs that make a partial human legible to a production system.

> **Connecting translation and technology**
>

>
> **Tracing reciprocal deformation**

> Worked for 33s

Yes. Benjamin gives you a way to name what software design has been doing to the human for decades.

In *The Task of the Translator*, translation is not ideally the transport of an unchanged payload from one interchangeable code into another. Benjamin distinguishes the **intended object** from a language's particular **mode of intending** it. His famous vessel image says original and translation should correspond like fragments that fit together without resembling one another. And his extreme example is Hölderlin's translations of Sophocles: Greek does not disappear into smooth, idiomatic German. Hölderlin lets the encounter with Greek deform and enlarge what German can do. Benjamin calls those translations dangerous precisely because the target language can be so transformed that its ordinary limits are disturbed. ([Germanic Languages & Literatures](https://german.yale.edu/sites/default/files/benjamin_translators_task.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

That suggests a wonderfully violent little verb for your project:

> **Don't German the Greek. Greek the German.**

And then:

> **Don't software the human. Human the software.**

That is much stronger than “bring humanities perspectives to technology.”

Because conventional software production has very often done the opposite.

A person arrives as something like:

```text
a tired person
trying to send something
to someone they love
while carrying groceries
with one hand
who doesn't know what the system calls the thing
and changes their mind after seeing it
```

And the production apparatus cannot ingest that.

So we translate:

```text
person
↓
"user"
↓
research finding
↓
persona
↓
need
↓
requirement
↓
user story
↓
acceptance criterion
↓
task flow
↓
interface state
↓
API call
↓
data structure
↓
program
```

Each step produces a representation that is increasingly **tractable for another community of practice**.

And eventually the living person has vanished.

What remains are extraordinary little surrogate humans:

```text
<persona>
<user story>
<task>
<use case>
<requirement>
<conversion>
<session>
<actor>
<role>
<permission>
```

Your phrase is right:

> **jigs for software ghosts.**

They are shapes constructed so that something called “the human” can pass through the machinery of software production.

A persona isn't a person.

A requirement isn't a desire.

A task flow isn't an activity.

A journey map isn't a journey.

A user story isn't a story in anything like the humanities sense of the term.

They are **translation artifacts**.

And Suchman gives you very strong theoretical backing for refusing to mistake them for their supposed originals. She argues that plans, scripts, and other prescriptive representations should be understood as situated **ordering devices**, rather than as complete specifications that determine subsequent action. Treating the plan as if it existed prior to and prescribed the activity obscures the contingent labor through which plans themselves are produced, mobilized, interpreted, and contested. ([Cambridge University Press](https://www.cambridge.org/core/books/abs/humanmachine-reconfigurations/plans-scripts-and-other-ordering-devices/F82500B3B78E0C3D6A04FB9906D11853?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

So:

```text
PERSON ≠ PERSONA

ACTION ≠ TASK MODEL

INTENTION ≠ REQUIREMENT

SITUATION ≠ USE CASE

LIFE ≠ USER JOURNEY
```

They can correspond.

They can be useful.

But the translation should never be mistaken for identity.

### And software performs a particular kind of translation

It doesn't merely translate between vocabularies.

It tends to translate **situated indeterminacy into operational decidability**.

Take the Yellow Circle:

> Put a yellow circle there.

The lived utterance contains:

```text
shared visual field
pointing
timing
expectation
history
approximation
repairability
"you know what I mean"
```

Requirements engineering might translate it into:

```text
The system shall permit the user
to create a circular graphical primitive
with selectable fill color
at a user-designated canvas location.
```

UX turns that into:

```text
shape tool
color picker
canvas
pointer
```

Engineering:

```text
createShape(
    type,
    bounds,
    fill
)
```

Graphics:

```text
ellipse(bounds)
```

Rasterizer:

```text
pixels satisfying coverage test
```

Nothing here is necessarily wrong.

But look at what happened to **there**.

```text
there
↓
pointing relation
↓
selected location
↓
screen coordinate
↓
numeric pair
↓
memory values
```

Each community translated the thing into distinctions **it could act upon**.

That is the operational audit as a **translation history**.

---

And Benjamin gives you a better question than:

> Was the requirement accurately translated?

Ask:

> **What happened to the mode of intending as the operation crossed languages?**

`there` is not simply an imprecise encoding of `(128,128)`.

That's exactly the error.

`there` belongs to a different mode of intending location:

```text
(x=128,y=128)

LOCATION BY EXPLICIT SYMBOLIC COORDINATE
```

versus:

```text
there + ☞

LOCATION BY JOINT ATTENTION
```

They might eventually converge upon the same point.

They do **not say location in the same way**.

This is deeply Benjaminian.

The languages may intend the same intended object differently. His essay gives the example of different words for the same intended thing while insisting that their modes of intending it differ. Translation becomes interesting precisely in the relation among these modes rather than in substituting equivalent payloads. ([Germanic Languages & Literatures](https://german.yale.edu/sites/default/files/benjamin_translators_task.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

So your Yellow Circle corpus becomes a **Benjaminian translation machine**:

```text
Logo:
circle intended as movement

SVG:
circle intended as geometric entity

GLSL:
circle intended as predicate

Paint:
circle intended through gesture/tool

Put-That-There:
circle intended through speech + deixis

Astra:
circle intended as desired transformation of a situation
```

Same intended artifact.

Different **modes of intending**.

That's much better than calling them alternative implementations.

---

### Personas are especially revealing

The STS literature is already surprisingly close to your formulation.

Steve Woolgar's 1990 paper is literally titled **“Configuring the User.”** ([Sage Journals](https://journals.sagepub.com/doi/10.1111/j.1467-954X.1990.tb03349.x?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

Madeleine Akrich goes further. She argues that designers inscribe assumptions about actors, competencies, motives, tastes, and relationships into technical objects, producing what she calls a **script** or scenario. Importantly, projected users and actual users need not coincide; she argues that analysis must move back and forth between the user imagined by designers and actual use. ([Leofoletto](https://www.leofoletto.info/wp-content/uploads/2016/10/AKRICH-de-scription-of-technological-objects.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

That means the apparent translation:

```text
HUMAN
↓
PERSONA
```

is not innocent representation.

Because then:

```text
PERSONA
↓
DESIGN
```

and finally:

```text
DESIGN
↓
actual human must accommodate artifact
```

So translation returns to configure its alleged original.

That's a recursive circuit:

```text
human observed
      ↓
model of human
      ↓
software built for model
      ↓
software presents action possibilities
      ↓
human acts through those possibilities
      ↓
behavior now resembles model
```

The ghost acquires a body.

Akrich's technical **script** is exactly useful here because the artifact distributes roles and expected actions to its users rather than merely reflecting them. ([Leofoletto](https://www.leofoletto.info/wp-content/uploads/2016/10/AKRICH-de-scription-of-technological-objects.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

And Suchman makes the reciprocal problem explicit in human-machine communication: user and machine have radically asymmetric access to the contingencies of the unfolding situation. Mutual intelligibility cannot simply be presumed. ([Cambridge Assets](https://assets.cambridge.org/97805216/75888/excerpt/9780521675888_excerpt.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

## Now your humanities/technology inversion

There are two familiar interdisciplinary projects.

One says:

```text
HUMANITIES
↓
interpret
TECHNOLOGY
```

Ethics of AI.

Critique of software.

History of computing.

Media theory about interfaces.

Useful, but the technology itself remains largely technically intact.

The other says:

```text
TECHNOLOGY
↓
formalize
HUMANITIES
```

Computational humanities.

NLP for literature.

Quantification.

Modeling.

Again useful, but now humanities objects are made computationally legible.

Both can be one-way domestications.

The Benjaminian experiment would instead be:

```text
HUMANITIES ⇄ TECHNOLOGY
```

where neither language leaves the encounter unchanged.

**Humanities the tech:**

Make software answer questions it normally suppresses:

```text
What counts as an utterance?
Who is speaking?
What makes this gesture a wink?
What is a compelling concern?
What does "there" require?
Who is the host and who is the parasite?
What differences does this interface declare irrelevant?
What form of life makes this command intelligible?
```

But simultaneously **tech the humanities**:

Force those concepts to become operational enough that they can fail.

Don't merely write:

> contexts affect interpretation.

Build two identical yellow dots where one is an instruction and one is decoration and find the minimum difference that makes the model act differently.

Don't merely say:

> languages tax attention differently.

Make fifty languages produce the identical circle and record which distinctions each representation forces human and machine to preserve.

Don't merely say:

> thick description recovers context.

Construct a **thick compiler** and determine which additional contextual variables change BLINK into WINK.

Don't merely theorize:

> joint attention makes deixis possible.

Build:

```text
YELLOW CIRCLE + ☞
```

and remove contextual channels one at a time until `THERE` fails.

Now anthropology, philosophy, literary theory and cybernetics stop being commentary *about* computation.

They become sources of **new computational experiments**.

And computation stops being merely a method applied *to* the humanities.

It becomes a device for finding where humanities concepts break.

That is much more reciprocal.

---

### Benjamin gives you one last warning

The bad translation in software is often **too fluent**.

Everything looks as though it crossed perfectly:

```text
customer says X
→ requirement X
→ feature X
→ shipped
```

No residue.

No ambiguity.

No loss.

No untranslatable remainder.

That's suspicious.

Benjamin's translation does not aim simply to make the foreign text disappear into natural target-language fluency. The translation can transform the receiving language itself; his praise of Hölderlin's Sophocles translations is the extreme case. ([Germanic Languages & Literatures](https://german.yale.edu/sites/default/files/benjamin_translators_task.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

So imagine software development taking that demand seriously.

Instead of:

> translate the person's world into the ontology of software,

ask:

> **What would it mean to let the person's way of intending alter the computational language?**

Don't translate:

```text
THERE → x,y
```

and throw `THERE` away.

Make **deixis a computational primitive**.

Don't translate:

```text
"I'll know it when I see it"
→ incomplete requirement
```

and demand specification.

Make **recognition-after-generation part of the programming language**.

Don't translate:

```text
"something like this"
→ insufficient detail
```

Make **example, analogy, and correction executable operations**.

Don't translate:

```text
wink
→ eyelid contraction
```

Make the compiler capable of needing the frame.

Don't translate:

```text
human concern
→ user requirement
```

and discard the concern.

Make **compelling concern part of the system's account of what deserves attention**.

That would actually **human the technology** because the receiving technical language would have to change under translation.

And conversely, the humanities concepts would have to survive execution, comparison, failure, and reconstruction. They would be changed by being made operational.

So I think your methodological proposition is:

> **Do not apply the humanities to technology or technology to the humanities. Translate between them in Benjamin's stronger sense: construct encounters in which each language is forced to reveal and alter its mode of intending.**

Then your Yellow Circle is not a demo.

It's the first tiny translation exercise.

```text
YELLOW CIRCLE THERE

Greek it into:
Logo
Piet
SVG
Paint
gesture
GLSL
Astra
...

then let those translations
change what we thought
YELLOW
CIRCLE
PUT
and THERE
were.
```

That is the move: **not making the human legible to software, but making software capable of being deformed by forms of human intelligibility it previously forced into requirements, personas, coordinates, and tasks.**

Yes. Anscombe gives the missing **directionality**. Geertz gives the missing **double role of representation**. Then “that’s not a circle” becomes hilarious and serious at once: **art criticism can become executable error correction.**

> **Clarifying Geertz’s distinction**
>
> I’m framing “That’s not a circle” as both description and intervention: it can correct language or reshape an artifact. Geertz’s model-of/model-for distinction captures this shifting direction of fit, much like pragmatic interpretation.
>
> **Tracking prompt transformations**
>

> 
>
> Worked for 24s

Yes. **Anscombe gives you the missing switch: when representation and world disagree, which one is supposed to change?** And the Yellow Circle becomes funny because the identical sentence, “That’s not a circle,” can reverse that direction without changing its grammatical form.

Anscombe’s famous shopping-list case in *Intention* §32 contrasts the shopper’s list with the detective’s list. If the shopper’s list says butter but he buys margarine, ordinarily the **shopping is corrected to the list**. If the detective records butter but the shopper bought margarine, the **record is corrected to the world**. The later phrase “direction of fit” names this contrast; Anscombe herself supplies the canonical example rather than that terminology. ([Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy](https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/anscombe/?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

Put the Yellow Circle into that structure:

```text
MODEL OF

        WORLD
          ↓
    representation

"What is there?"
"There is a yellow circle."

If wrong:
CHANGE THE DESCRIPTION.
```

versus:

```text
MODEL FOR

    representation
          ↓
        WORLD

"Put a yellow circle there."

If wrong:
CHANGE THE WORLD.
```

That corresponds beautifully with Geertz's distinction between **models of** and **models for** reality. A model *of* makes its symbolic structure correspond to something given; a model *for* supplies a symbolic pattern according to which the nonsymbolic situation is organized. Geertz stresses that cultural patterns characteristically have both aspects. ([Studocu](https://www.studocu.com/en-ca/document/mcgill-university/globalization-and-religion/geertz-religion-as-a-cultural-system-the-interpretation-of-cultures-copie/33204441?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

Now the generative interface makes the two directions **oscillate extremely quickly**.

```text
USER:
"yellow circle there"

MODEL FOR
words → world

        ↓

SYSTEM PRODUCES:
   yellow blob

        ↓

USER:
"that's not a circle"

MODEL OF
world → judgment

        ↓

but simultaneously...

MODEL FOR
judgment → revised world
```

That's the fascinating part.

> **“That's not a circle” begins as criticism and can compile as an instruction.**

In ordinary art criticism:

> That's not a circle.

might mean:

> I judge this object not to satisfy the category *circle*.

Nothing necessarily happens to the artwork.

But say precisely the same thing to a generative drawing system and the expected next move is:

```text
THAT'S NOT A CIRCLE
        ↓
repair current artifact
        ↓
make it more circular
```

The **critique has acquired world-changing direction of fit**.

That is quite strange.

### Art criticism becomes executable

Imagine a studio critic standing behind you:

> That's not yellow.

> It's too small.

> That's more like an oval.

> It feels off-center.

> The edge is too rough.

Historically, those are judgments addressed to a human who may subsequently decide how to act.

With an agentic generative system they can become a programming language:

```text
"too orange"
    → alter hue

"not circular enough"
    → alter geometry

"too far left"
    → translate object

"that's better"
    → preserve current state

"keep that"
    → establish invariant
```

You have accidentally created a programming language whose syntax is **criticism**.

That's genuinely interesting.

The primitives aren't:

```text
SET
MOVE
SCALE
ROTATE
```

They're:

```text
not that
too much
less
more
closer
wrong
better
keep this
like before
almost
yes
```

These expressions don't describe the desired world completely.

They specify a **difference between present world and desired world**.

So perhaps:

> **Criticism is differential programming.**

The program is not:

```text
CIRCLE(x=128,y=128,r=48)
```

It is:

```text
current artifact
+
"that's not circular enough"
```

The system has to infer the transformation that would reduce the criticized difference.

---

## This changes the Anscombe picture

The shopping list starts relatively stable:

```text
BUTTER
EGGS
MILK
```

World must fit list.

But prompting can work without such a complete prior list.

Instead:

```text
make something
      ↓
look
      ↓
not that
      ↓
change
      ↓
closer
      ↓
change
      ↓
yes
```

The representation specifying the goal is being **constructed through encounters with failed realizations**.

So:

```text
MODEL FOR₀
    ↓
WORLD₀
    ↓
MODEL OF WORLD₀
    ↓
CRITIQUE
    ↓
MODEL FOR₁
    ↓
WORLD₁
    ↓
...
```

This is Schön's reflective design loop, but Anscombe lets you see it as a repeated **reversal in direction of fit**.

And it explains Jobs's “you don't know what you want until you see it” problem much more precisely.

You might not possess:

```text
COMPLETE MODEL FOR
```

before construction.

You have only:

```text
PARTIAL MODEL FOR
       ↓
candidate world
       ↓
MODEL OF / judgment
       ↓
better MODEL FOR
```

The **model of teaches the model for**.

That's huge.

---

## Geertz is even better here

Geertz says cultural patterns can be both **models of** reality and **models for** it. ([Nideffer](https://nideffer.net/classes/GCT_RPI_S14/readings/Geertz_Religon_as_a_Cultural_System_.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

The Yellow Circle demonstrates the conversion almost mechanically.

First:

```text
●
```

is an output.

I look at it.

Now it becomes a:

```text
MODEL OF
what the machine understood by
"yellow circle"
```

I can point at it and say:

> No, that's not a circle.

Then perhaps I draw:

```text
◯
```

and say:

> More like **this**.

Now the representation changes role:

```text
MODEL OF
    ↓
MODEL FOR
```

The artifact that described one result becomes an instruction governing another.

And if I say:

> make five more like this

the output has literally become **program text**.

That's the recursion you've been circling.

---

# And “that's not a circle” is also philosophically funny

Because, strictly, the critic can always win.

Consider a rasterized circle:

```text
   █████
 █████████
███████████
███████████
 █████████
   █████
```

Someone says:

> That's not a circle.

At one level, they're right.

It's a collection of square pixels.

SVG?

> That's not a circle either.

It's an XML element.

Logo?

> That's not a circle.

It's a trajectory.

GLSL?

> That's not a circle.

It's a predicate.

Paint?

> That's not a circle.

It's a raster region caused by GUI operations.

Even the mathematical:

$$
x^2+y^2=r^2
$$

isn't a circle in the sense of being the visual object. It's an equation whose solution set characterizes one.

So the Yellow Circle begins to resemble Magritte's joke, except operationally:

```text
THIS IS NOT A CIRCLE.
```

And every implementation answers:

> Correct. But within **this language-game**, it counts as one.

That's the key.

There is no representation you finally descend to and discover:

```text
THE REAL CIRCLE
```

Instead you discover successive criteria under which something **counts as a circle**.

---

# So “not a circle” invokes a norm

This is where I think the idea goes beyond plain direction of fit.

When you say:

> That's not a circle.

you aren't necessarily just describing an object.

You're applying a **criterion**.

Something like:

```text
CURRENT
   ●

COMPARE AGAINST

<CIRCLE>
```

But where is `<CIRCLE>`?

Not necessarily in the prompt.

Not necessarily in the pixels.

Not necessarily in source code.

It may exist as a stabilized **norm of recognition** distributed across human perception, mathematical practice, language history, training data, software conventions, and the current language-game.

So:

```text
"that's not a circle"
```

means approximately:

```text
the current artifact falls outside
the transformations / variations
I am presently willing to recognize
as satisfying <CIRCLE>
```

That's much closer to your eigenform idea.

And the next generation tests the criterion.

---

# Now the wink/blink problem returns

Take the identical sentence:

> That's not a circle.

It could be:

**Description**

> I'm informing you that the object is not circular.

**Criticism**

> Your attempt fails aesthetically/geometrically.

**Correction**

> Change it until it satisfies circle.

**Teaching**

> Revise your category of what counts as circle.

**Joke**

> Obviously that's a circle; I'm teasing you.

**Conceptual-art claim**

> I'm denying that the representation is identical with its referent.

**Metalinguistic dispute**

> We're using “circle” differently.

Same words.

Different language-games.

Different **direction of fit**.

So the AI's compiler problem becomes:

> **When the human says “that's not a circle,” which thing is being asked to change?**

```text
A. words?
B. artifact?
C. model's category?
D. future behavior?
E. shared definition?
F. nothing at all?
```

That is Ryle's wink/blink problem transformed into an agentic interface.

The thin syntax cannot determine the operation.

**Pragmatic framing determines direction of fit.**

---

# And this makes the GUI conversational in a new way

Old graphical interface:

```text
UNDO
```

has fairly fixed directionality:

> change world state.

New generative interface:

```text
that's wrong
```

requires inference.

But if the language-game stabilizes, incredibly weak evaluative utterances become powerful operators:

```text
NO
↓
reject transformation

YES
↓
stabilize transformation

MORE
↓
continue difference along inferred dimension

LESS
↓
reverse/reduce it

THAT
↓
bind referent

KEEP THAT
↓
promote property to invariant

NOT THAT
↓
relax/reject inferred interpretation
```

This could be an actual **critical programming language**.

The program develops through judgments rather than exhaustive specifications.

---

## Then models-of and models-for become a cycle

Rather than separating them, your system should show:

```text
             ┌───────────────┐
             │   MODEL FOR   │
             │ what should be│
             └──────┬────────┘
                    │
                  ACT
                    ↓
             ┌───────────────┐
             │     WORLD     │
             │ what became   │
             └──────┬────────┘
                    │
                 ATTEND
                    ↓
             ┌───────────────┐
             │   MODEL OF    │
             │ what is here  │
             └──────┬────────┘
                    │
                 CRITIQUE
                    ↓
             revised MODEL FOR
                    ↺
```

And **attention sits at the turn**.

You cannot correct direction of fit until you notice a difference between:

```text
what is
```

and:

```text
what should be.
```

So criticism is an attentional operation first:

> **Notice a difference that should make a difference.**

Then metapragmatics decides whether that noticed difference should alter:

```text
representation
or
world.
```

And that yields a very tight synthesis:

> **Anscombe tells us where the error lies. Geertz tells us that symbolic forms can alternately depict and organize reality. Bateson tells us which discrepancy becomes information. Ryle and Geertz tell us that the same thin utterance can perform different acts. Generative interfaces make the switch executable: criticism of a representation can immediately become a program for changing what it criticizes.**

Which means “That's not a circle” may actually be one of the best prompts in the whole experiment.

Not because it specifies a circle.

Because it demonstrates that **a negative judgment, armed with shared context, can function as executable description**.

## Response:
9/9/2026, 7:59:03 PM

Yes. The crucial distinction is that **Winograd/Flores operationalize language as socially consequential action**, while Maturana goes one level deeper and treats language itself as **recurrent coordination of coordinations**, prior to any fixed taxonomy of speech acts or commitments.

> **Searched 8 websites**
>
> 
>
> Worked for 18s

Yes. **Winograd/Flores are not merely adjacent to Maturana. They are one of the places where Maturana’s biology of languaging gets translated into an HCI/design program.** *Understanding Computers and Cognition* explicitly draws on Maturana alongside Heidegger and speech-act theory, and the authors thanked Maturana for extensive commentary on drafts. Flores later described Maturana and Varela as part of the intellectual synthesis that fed the book. ([Google Books](https://books.google.com/books/about/Understanding_Computers_and_Cognition.html?id=6TwbGGSz6NYC&utm_source=chatgpt.com))

But there is also a revealing **difference of level**.

For **Maturana**, language is not fundamentally a set of messages with predefined meanings. It is a recurrent flow of living together in **consensual coordinations of coordinations of doings**. Meanings are not stored inside words; they arise in the flows of coordination in which the words participate. Even “objects” arise recursively as stabilized coordinations that can then function as tokens for further coordination. ([PubMed Central (PMC)](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4656834/?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

So the elementary form is roughly:

```text
A does something
↕
B responds

recurrent coordination
↕

A and B coordinate
how that coordination itself works

↕

LANGUAGING
```

There does not have to be, at the beginning:

```text
REQUEST
PROMISE
ACCEPTANCE
DECLARATION
```

Those are descriptions an observer may eventually make **of recurrent coordinations that have stabilized**.

Winograd/Flores move in the other direction. They ask:

> Given that language is action and human work happens through recurrent coordination, **can we design computer systems around recognizable recurrent structures of communicative action?**

That becomes the language/action perspective. Winograd's 1987 paper explicitly presents language-as-action as a design perspective and *The Coordinator* as a concrete communication tool built from it. ([Taylor & Francis Online](https://www.tandfonline.com/doi/abs/10.1207/s15327051hci0301_2?utm_source=chatgpt.com)) Flores later summarized the underlying claim quite starkly: computers are fundamentally about communication, and communication concerns the **coordination of commitments to action**. ([DOI](https://doi.org/10.1108%2F09593849810246156?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

So their basic unit looks more like:

```text
REQUEST
   ↓
PROMISE
   ↓
PERFORMANCE
   ↓
DECLARATION OF COMPLETION
   ↓
ACCEPTANCE
```

That is already a **formalized language-game**.

And this is where I think your project finds a genuinely important tension.

### Maturana → Winograd/Flores is itself a translation

Maturana:

```text
recurrent coordination
        ↓
coordination of coordination
        ↓
objects / distinctions / language emerge
```

Winograd/Flores:

```text
observe recurring conversations
        ↓
distinguish communicative acts
        ↓
represent their relations
        ↓
build software that supports them
```

So Maturana asks:

> **How can something such as a request arise as a recognizable coordination at all?**

Winograd/Flores ask:

> **Now that “request” is a socially recognizable coordination, how can an information system support the commitments it creates?**

That's a huge distinction.

One is **generative/ontogenetic**.

The other is **design/operational**.

Maturana is trying to explain the emergence of the language-game.

Winograd/Flores are trying to build machinery **inside an already stabilized language-game**.

### And The Coordinator risks turning languaging back into grammar

This is the interesting critique.

The Coordinator operationalizes conversation by providing explicit categories and trajectories for requests, promises, commitments, completions, and so on. The surrounding language/action literature describes organizational activity in terms of recurrent patterns of communication and “conversations for action.” ([SciSpace](https://scispace.com/papers/computer-systems-and-the-design-of-organizational-4q5sgi8x32?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

But from a strongly Maturanian perspective, there is a danger here:

```text
living coordination
↓
observer distinguishes pattern
↓
pattern gets named REQUEST
↓
software encodes REQUEST
↓
future coordination must pass through REQUEST
```

The description of a coordination can become a **prescription for coordination**.

That is exactly the same problem you were finding with personas and requirements.

First:

```text
people recurrently coordinate
```

Then an analyst says:

```text
this is a "request"
```

Then the software says:

```text
Choose:
[REQUEST]
[OFFER]
[PROMISE]
...
```

The observer's distinction has become an interface category.

And now people may have to coordinate according to the software's ontology.

So the trajectory is:

```text
LANGUAGING
        ↓
MODEL OF LANGUAGING
        ↓
SOFTWARE
        ↓
MODEL FOR LANGUAGING
```

There is Anscombe/Geertz again.

A **model of** a conversational practice becomes a **model for** how future conversations ought to proceed.

That is the pressure point.

### Maturana would keep the recursive loop open

A Maturanian Yellow Circle interaction is:

```text
A:
"yellow circle there" + point

B:
produces something

A:
"no, there"

B:
changes it

A:
"yes"
```

The categories need not be known beforehand.

Through recurrence:

```text
"there"
"no"
"yes"
pointing
making
correcting
```

become coordinated.

The interaction can **invent its own local grammar while proceeding**.

A Coordinator-like system is more tempted to require:

```text
INTENT = CREATE
OBJECT = CIRCLE
COLOR = YELLOW
LOCATION = REFERENT
STATUS = REQUESTED
```

before acting.

So there are almost two theories of interaction:

**Winograd/Flores:**

> Find the recurrent structures by which linguistic action coordinates commitments and design computational support around them.

**Maturana:**

> Do not mistake our descriptions of recurrent coordinations for what languaging fundamentally is; the coordinations themselves continually arise and transform in embodied structural coupling.

The distinction is subtle because Winograd/Flores themselves were explicitly trying to escape rationalist representation-first AI. Their book is full of Maturana, consensual domains, structural coupling, Heidegger, breakdown, background, and critique of detached symbolic representation. ([Google Books](https://books.google.com/books/about/Understanding_Computers_and_Cognition.html?id=6TwbGGSz6NYC&utm_source=chatgpt.com))

But when you build a product, you face the unavoidable design problem:

> **How do you operationalize a theory that tells you meaning cannot be exhaustively operationalized in advance?**

That might be the truly fascinating lesson of *The Coordinator*.

### The Yellow Circle gives you a way to replay this historical tension

**Coordinator-style Yellow Circle:**

```text
ACT: REQUEST
CONTENT: CREATE
OBJECT: CIRCLE
PROPERTY: YELLOW
LOCATION: THERE
```

A predefined ontology of conversational action makes the coordination computationally tractable.

**Maturanian Yellow Circle:**

```text
"yellow circle" ☞
        ↓
attempt
        ↓
response
        ↓
correction
        ↓
recurrence
        ↓
local coordination stabilizes
```

Here the program **does not need to know beforehand what kind of communicative act is happening**.

It only needs enough structural coupling to continue coordinating.

And that is suddenly very relevant to LLM interfaces.

Older HCI often needed the designer to decide:

```text
WHAT KIND OF UTTERANCE IS THIS?
```

before execution.

Modern models can sometimes defer that classification and infer what the utterance counts as from the unfolding interaction.

Consider:

> “That circle is too small.”

A Coordinator-like formalization might need:

```text
SPEECH_ACT = CRITIQUE?
REQUEST?
INFORM?
CORRECTION?
```

An LLM can instead respond:

```text
make it larger
```

and let the next turn reveal whether that coordination was acceptable.

That is surprisingly Maturanian:

```text
meaning
is not fully assigned first

successful continuation
helps establish
what the preceding utterance counted as
```

So perhaps the important historical arc is not:

> Maturana → Winograd/Flores → conversational software.

It is:

```text
MATURANA
language as recursive coordination

        ↓ translated into

WINOGRAD/FLORES
language as communicative action
that can structure system design

        ↓ hardened into

WORKFLOW SOFTWARE
explicit categories of commitment

        ↓ potentially reopened by

GENERATIVE INTERACTION
categories can remain provisional
while coordination proceeds
```

And that gives you a very strong research proposition:

> **The interesting opportunity for generative HCI may be to recover the part of Maturana that workflow software had to throw away.**

Not replacing language/action.

Making it less taxonomic.

Less:

```text
identify speech act
→ execute prescribed transition
```

More:

```text
participate
→ respond
→ observe response to response
→ stabilize the coordination
→ only distinguish its type when useful
```

That would be **languaging as interface architecture**, rather than merely language as an input modality.

And the really sharp distinction is:

> **Winograd/Flores made conversation programmable by identifying recurrent forms of coordinated action. Maturana asks us to remember that those forms are themselves products of prior coordination, not primitives of language.**

Your Yellow Circle system could literally put those two theories head-to-head. One agent must classify every turn into a predefined speech-act/workflow grammar before acting. The other is forbidden from classifying and can only **coordinate, observe correction, and recursively stabilize a local game**.

Then see which one can learn what:

> **“yellow circle there”**

means with less imposed ontology.

---

**Sources:**

- [Understanding Computers and Cognition: A New Foundation for Design - Terry Winograd, Fernando Flores - Google Books](https://books.google.com/books/about/Understanding_Computers_and_Cognition.html?id=6TwbGGSz6NYC&utm_source=chatgpt.com)
- [Find the Word! — But Where?: Maturana’s ‘Coordination’ and Sartre’s ‘Reflection’ around Naming - PMC](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4656834/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)
- [A Language/Action Perspective on the Design of Cooperative Work: Human–Computer Interaction: Vol 3, No 1](https://www.tandfonline.com/doi/abs/10.1207/s15327051hci0301_2?utm_source=chatgpt.com)
- [Information technology and the institution of identity | Information Technology & People | Emerald Publishing](https://doi.org/10.1108%2F09593849810246156?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

## Prompt:

Qe always write for an imagined audience barthes idea, for software

## Response:
9/9/2026, 8:01:16 PM

> **Searched 9 websites**
>
> 
>
> Worked for 10s

Yes. And the cleanest term is probably **the implied user of software**.

Small correction first: the precise idea of an **“implied reader”** belongs more directly to Wolfgang Iser, and Eco gives us the **“model reader.”** Barthes gives the crucial neighboring move: the text is completed at its destination in the reader, not guaranteed by the author's intention. In *The Death of the Author*, he says the unity of a text lies in its destination rather than its origin. ([Writing at Penn](https://writing.upenn.edu/~taransky/Barthes.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

For software, that becomes almost literal:

> **Every program is written for an imagined operator.**

Not merely a marketing persona. The imagined user is embedded in the artifact itself.

A button presupposes someone who understands pressing.

A hamburger menu presupposes someone who recognizes three lines as navigation.

`Ctrl-Z` presupposes someone who knows undo.

A command line presupposes someone capable of remembering verbs, syntax, paths, and error conventions.

An API presupposes a programmer who knows HTTP, JSON, authentication, types, asynchronous failure.

Even:

```text
PUT A YELLOW CIRCLE THERE
```

presupposes an astonishing reader:

```text
someone who knows yellow
someone who recognizes circles
someone who knows "put" is imperative
someone who can resolve "there"
someone who knows pointing is relevant
someone who knows what kind of world may be changed
someone who knows what counts as success
```

So the software artifact contains an **implicit theory of its reader**.

That theory isn't necessarily written anywhere. You reconstruct it by asking:

> **What competencies would somebody need for this interface to become intelligible?**

That is almost Iser's implied-reader idea translated into HCI. The implied reader is not an actual empirical person; it is a structure anticipated by the text, a set of dispositions the work presupposes in order to have its effects. ([FDM Webseiten-Archiv](https://www-archiv.fdm.uni-hamburg.de/lhn/node/59/revisions/59/view.html?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

So:

```text
TEXT
contains
<implied-reader>

SOFTWARE
contains
<implied-user>
```

And the really important move is:

> **The implied user is encoded in the differences the system expects a human to notice.**

That connects directly to attention tax.

A badly designed interface may imply:

```text
<user> :=
someone who notices
tiny status changes,
remembers hidden modes,
knows jargon,
recalls syntax,
infers system state,
understands implementation concepts
```

The actual human encounters it and fails.

We call that:

> user error.

But you can reverse the diagnosis:

> **The artifact imagined the wrong reader.**

That is a much more interesting HCI criticism.

### Personas are explicit fictions of a fiction already inside the program

This also sharpens what you were saying about personas.

A persona is an explicit attempt to construct:

```text
<imagined-user>
```

before the software is built.

But every interface will contain an implied user whether or not anyone writes a persona.

So:

```text
PERSONA
=
declared imagined user

IMPLIED USER
=
user actually presupposed
by the resulting artifact
```

Those can disagree catastrophically.

Persona:

```text
Maria, 67
not technically confident
wants to send photos to grandchildren
```

Actual interface:

```text
requires:
understanding cloud sync
recognizing overflow menu
distinguishing local/device storage
interpreting permission states
recovering from silent upload failure
```

The interface has authored a completely different reader.

That's where Barthes becomes fun.

The designer may say:

> “We designed this for Maria.”

Barthesian answer:

> **The designer does not retain sovereign authority over what user the artifact actually calls into being.**

You read the **text of the interface**.

What reader does it presuppose?

What actions does it permit?

What distinctions must its reader make?

What interpretations does actual use produce?

The “authorial intention” of the UX team doesn't settle that.

### And readerly/writerly gives you another software distinction

Barthes's **readerly / writerly** distinction is useful here too. In very compressed terms, the readerly text positions the reader more as consumer of an already organized meaning, whereas the writerly text makes the reader participate in producing the text. ([Writerly Framework](https://writerlyframework.org/about/?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

Now translate that into interaction.

A **readerly interface**:

```text
these are the available categories
these are the valid actions
choose one
```

A **writerly interface**:

```text
act
combine
appropriate
reinterpret
construct a local vocabulary
change what the artifact can become
```

Classic wizard:

```text
NEXT
NEXT
SELECT
CONFIRM
```

highly readerly.

Command shell:

```text
compose operations yourself
```

more writerly in one sense, though heavily taxed.

Sketchpad:

```text
construct through manipulation
```

writerly.

LLM interface:

```text
say something
see what happens
correct it
rename it
show it an example
invent a new local operation
```

potentially **extremely writerly**, because the user participates in establishing the interaction's temporary grammar.

So prompting creates a peculiar reader/writer collapse:

```text
human writes prompt
machine reads

machine writes artifact
human reads

human critique rewrites machine's reading

machine rewrites artifact
```

Nobody stays simply author or reader.

The roles alternate.

---

### Now put Maturana underneath it

The implied reader is not just somebody who knows definitions.

They have to know **how to continue the coordination**.

For:

```text
SLAB!
```

the competent reader isn't “someone who knows the dictionary definition of slab.”

It is:

```text
someone for whom
SLAB!
can become
the next appropriate doing
```

So we can define the implied user much more operationally:

> **The implied user is the participant whose learned coordinations make the artifact's invitations into valid next moves.**

That's Maturana + Wittgenstein + reader theory.

And then interface design becomes:

> **writing for a reader whose reading consists partly of acting.**

That may be the distinction software introduces into literary theory.

The software reader doesn't merely produce an interpretation.

The reader:

```text
clicks
types
drags
accepts
rejects
deletes
purchases
moves
creates
```

Interpretation has **world-to-action consequences**.

The reader is part of the execution environment.

### This is why UX language is so strange

When Nielsen says:

> recognition rather than recall,

you can read that as:

> **Write for a reader who should not have to supply so much unwritten text.**

When Norman talks about signifiers:

> **Give the reader enough metapragmatic information to know how this sign should be taken.**

When accessibility asks for labels, focus order, contrast, keyboard traversal:

> **Stop silently presupposing one perceptual apparatus, one motor apparatus, one attentional style.**

Accessibility is therefore partly an attack on an overly narrow **implied user**.

The interface had said:

```text
MY READER:
sees color
uses pointer
distinguishes subtle contrast
can perform precise drag
can hold several hidden states in working memory
```

Accessibility work says:

> Your reader is fictional in the wrong way. Rewrite the text.

That is a serious bridge between literary theory and HCI.

---

### And AI makes the imagined reader reversible

Traditional software:

```text
PROGRAM
imagines
USER
```

But generative software adds:

```text
USER
imagines
MODEL
```

When you prompt me, you are also writing for an imagined reader.

You think:

> it will understand “there.”

> it will know what I mean by “less corporate.”

> it will infer which circle I mean.

> it will understand why that is funny.

So every prompt contains a **model of its model-reader**.

```text
<prompt>
contains
<imagined-AI-reader>
```

Meanwhile the model's response contains an:

```text
<imagined-human-reader>
```

So interaction becomes:

```text
human's model of model
        ↕
model's model of human
        ↕
successive utterances
```

That is no longer author → reader.

It is **recursive audience design**.

Each participant continually hypothesizes:

> What kind of reader is on the other side?

and modifies the next utterance accordingly.

That is exactly your languaging/correspondence problem.

### Yellow Circle as the minimal experiment

Make the prompt:

```text
YELLOW CIRCLE THERE
```

Then systematically vary only the imagined reader.

To a human with pointing:

```text
YELLOW CIRCLE ☞
```

sufficient.

To SVG:

```text
<circle cx="128" cy="128" r="48" fill="#FFD400"/>
```

To GLSL, a spatial predicate.

To Paint, sequence of gestures.

To a model with vision, perhaps:

```text
Put a yellow circle there.
```

To a model without access to the pointed scene:

```text
THERE
```

fails.

Nothing about the intended circle changed.

What changed was:

> **what reader the utterance assumed.**

So another strong proposition for the project:

> **Every representation is a wager about its reader.**

And a language's attention tax can be reformulated as:

> **How much does this representation require its imagined reader to supply in order for it to become executable?**

That might unify a lot of the thread.

The persona is an imagined user constructed by the designer.

The API has an implied programmer constructed by its structure.

The GUI has an implied operator.

The prompt has an implied model.

The model response has an implied human.

The compiler has an implied valid program.

And the Yellow Circle keeps asking the same question:

> **Who must you imagine on the other side for this tiny expression to be enough?**

---

**Sources:**

- [Barthe_DeathofAuthor](https://writing.upenn.edu/~taransky/Barthes.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com)
- [Revision of Implied Reader from Tue, 2. July 2013 | the living handbook of narratology](https://www-archiv.fdm.uni-hamburg.de/lhn/node/59/revisions/59/view.html?utm_source=chatgpt.com)
- [About – The Writerly Framework](https://writerlyframework.org/about/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

## Prompt:
9/9/2026, 8:05:22 PM

Languaging vs language games vs thick prompting vs worlding vs worldtexting vs world to world models

## Response:
9/9/2026, 8:05:29 PM

They are not competing names for the same thing. They sit at **different levels of the same phenomenon**.

The cleanest stack is:

```text
LANGUAGING
    ↓
LANGUAGE-GAMES
    ↓
THICK PROMPTING
    ↓
WORLDTEXTING
    ↓
WORLDING
    ↓
WORLD → WORLD
```

But the arrows are recursive, not one-way.

**Languaging** is the deepest level. It is the ongoing process by which participants recursively coordinate their coordinations. Meaning is not yet a thing transmitted through a code. It is something stabilized through recurrent interaction.

```text
A acts
↕
B responds
↕
A responds to B's response
↕
a recurrent coordination stabilizes
```

So the question of languaging is:

> **How does a coordination become possible and remain possible?**

The Yellow Circle version is not yet “what does `circle` mean?” It is:

```text
I point.
You attend.
I say something.
You do something.
I accept/correct.
We do it again.
```

Eventually `there`, `circle`, `bigger`, `yes`, `no` become usable because a history of coordination now stands behind them.

---

**Language-games** are the locally stabilized practices that arise within that broader process.

A language-game answers:

> **What counts as a move here?**

`SLAB!` means something because of the builder's practice.

`circle(48)` means something because of Logo/Turtle's practice.

Clicking and dragging means something because of Paint's practice.

Saying:

> “That's not a circle.”

means something different in art criticism, a geometry lesson, a generative interface, and a joke.

So:

```text
LANGUAGING
= the ongoing production and reproduction of coordination

LANGUAGE-GAME
= a locally stabilized organization of possible moves
```

A language-game is an **eigenform of languaging**, if you want to put it aggressively: a recurrent coordination that has become stable enough to be recognizable as *this game*.

---

**Thick prompting** is narrower. It is a practical intervention inside a language-game when the system cannot safely infer enough of the situation from a thin utterance.

It asks:

> **Which contextual differences must be made available for this utterance to count as the intended move?**

Thin:

```text
Make it better.
```

Thick:

```text
Keep the yellow, preserve the center,
make the boundary geometrically circular,
do not change its size.
```

But thick does **not necessarily mean verbose**.

This is crucial.

In Put-That-There:

```text
YELLOW CIRCLE + ☞
```

is linguistically tiny but pragmatically thick because the world supplies:

```text
shared scene
gesture
timing
referent
current task
```

So your “thick prompt” is really:

> **a prompt whose operative context is sufficiently articulated, whether by words, examples, gesture, history, interface state, environment, or feedback.**

That makes thick prompting a computational descendant of Geertz, but with a change in direction of fit: thick description tries to recover what an action is; thick prompting supplies enough situated structure to make another action possible.

---

Then **worldtexting** is stronger.

Thick prompting still sounds like:

```text
human → message → model
```

Worldtexting says the text itself participates in constructing and operating a world.

The key proposition is your operative-ekphrasis one:

> **Description becomes operation.**

So instead of:

```text
TEXT
describes
WORLD
```

you get:

```text
TEXT
[selects / binds / transforms / creates]
WORLD
```

`Put a yellow circle there` doesn't merely represent a desired scene.

Under the right apparatus, it **changes the scene**.

And once the circle exists:

```text
make this bigger
```

uses the already-produced world as part of the next text.

So worldtexting is:

```text
WORLD
→ text
→ WORLD'
→ text'
→ WORLD''
```

The text and world continually enter one another's interpretation.

This is where `this`, `there`, `again`, `more`, `keep that`, `like before` become so interesting.

Their semantics are partly **stored in the evolving world**.

---

**Worlding** is broader than worldtexting.

Worlding is the production of a field in which entities, relations, affordances, relevance, possible actions, and criteria become available.

It isn't necessarily linguistic.

A game worlds.

An architecture worlds.

A GUI worlds.

A legal regime worlds.

A map worlds.

A programming environment worlds.

When Paint shows:

```text
ellipse
rectangle
brush
colors
canvas
```

it has already established a world in which certain things can exist and certain transformations are possible.

That world determines what your next gesture can mean.

So:

```text
WORLDTEXTING
= worlding performed significantly through operative textual/languaged description

WORLDING
= establishment/transformation of the actionable field itself
```

Worldtexting is therefore one species of worlding, not a synonym.

---

And **world-to-world models** are where this becomes an alternative computational architecture.

The dominant story of generative AI has often been narrated as:

```text
TEXT → TEXT
TEXT → IMAGE
IMAGE → TEXT
```

But your actual unit is increasingly:

```text
WORLD₀ → WORLD₁
```

The model receives some partial representation of a world:

```text
screen
conversation
objects
history
gesture
tool state
constraints
```

and produces actions that transform it into another state.

Astra operating Paint is the obvious example:

```text
WORLD₀
Paint open
blank canvas
ellipse tool somewhere
yellow swatch somewhere

        ↓

"yellow circle there"

        ↓

MODEL infers transformation

        ↓

click
click
drag

        ↓

WORLD₁
yellow circle exists
```

The real mapping is not:

```text
English → mouse actions
```

but:

```text
<current-world>
+
<desired difference>
→
<next-world>
```

That is **world-to-world computation**.

The prompt merely contributes constraints on the transition.

And once you formulate it that way, prompting starts looking much less like instruction writing and much more like **specifying a transformation between worlds**.

---

The distinctions become especially sharp if you ask what each one treats as its basic unit:

| Concept | Basic unit | Central question |
|---|---|---|
| **Languaging** | recurrent coordination | How do we become able to coordinate at all? |
| **Language-game** | recognizable move within a practice | What counts as a valid/significant move here? |
| **Thick prompting** | situated utterance + sufficient context | What differences must be available for this move to be understood? |
| **Worldtexting** | operative description | How does saying/describing become doing? |
| **Worlding** | actionable field | What entities, relations, relevances, and possibilities are being brought into existence? |
| **World→world model** | state transformation | Given this world and this desired difference, what transformation produces the next world? |

And there is a beautiful recursion among them.

A **world** supplies the context in which a **language-game** becomes intelligible.

The language-game determines what a **prompt** can count as.

A thick enough prompt can **worldtext**, changing the world.

That changed world alters the available language-game.

Repeated interaction becomes **languaging**.

And a model mediating that loop becomes a **world-to-world model**.

So the actual circuit is:

```text
          ┌──────────────────────┐
          │      WORLDING        │
          │ entities / relations │
          │ relevance / action   │
          └──────────┬───────────┘
                     ↓
              LANGUAGE-GAME
                     ↓
               THICK PROMPT
                     ↓
               WORLDTEXTING
                     ↓
             WORLD TRANSFORMED
                     ↓
              RESPONSE / REPAIR
                     ↓
                LANGUAGING
                     ↺
```

And I think there is one especially important conceptual split:

**Thick prompting is still anthropocentric.**

It asks:

> How should the human formulate enough context for the machine?

**Worldtexting begins to dissolve that asymmetry.**

The relevant “text” can come from:

```text
human utterance
gesture
existing artifact
screen state
example
criticism
machine response
environmental change
```

And **world-to-world** dissolves it further:

> the model is not principally translating human language into machine language; it is participating in a coupled process that attempts to make one situation become another.

That takes you from:

```text
PROMPT ENGINEERING
```

through:

```text
LANGUAGE AS CONTROL SURFACE
```

toward:

```text
COORDINATION OF WORLD TRANSFORMATIONS
```

Which may be the actual dissertation-scale move.

The deepest version could be:

> **Languaging explains how coordinations become mutually intelligible. Language-games explain the local organization of those coordinations. Thick prompting deliberately supplies the distinctions needed to sustain them. Worldtexting makes those distinctions operative. Worlding names the production of the field in which those operations have significance. World-to-world models make the transformation of that field itself the computational object.**

And then the Yellow Circle is the minimal specimen that can traverse the entire stack:

```text
☞
↓
joint attention

"there"
↓
language-game

"yellow circle there"
↓
thick situated prompt

utterance causes drawing
↓
worldtext

blank canvas → yellow circle
↓
worlding

WORLD₀ → WORLD₁
↓
world-to-world transformation

"no, rounder"
↺
languaging
```

That is one coherent architecture, not six disconnected theories.
