9/9/2026, 8:07:48 PM

I’d hold the **artifact constant** and run every theory across the same three specimens: **SVG**, **Logo**, and **Put-That-There/Astra**. Then the key diagnostic is always Bateson’s question: **what difference has to change before the system must do something different?**

> **Building the theory matrix**
>
> I’m interpreting “three languages each” as three recurring cases: SVG markup, Logo commands, and ordinary “put-that-there” speech. I’ll compare theories by identifying the specific difference that changes interpretation, action, or coordination.
>
> **Built the theory matrix**
>
> 
>
> **Comparing three languages**
>
> I’m distinguishing SVG as declarative object description, Logo as procedural movement, and “put that there” as situated coordination. Across theories, the key variable is how each language makes a difference actionable, visible, or interpretable.
>
> Worked for 26s

Exactly. Make **one yellow circle** the control specimen, then let each theory ask a different question of the same three language-games.

The three recurring specimens should be:

```text
A. SVG
<circle cx="128" cy="128" r="48" fill="#FFD400"/>

B. LOGO
a turtle produces the circle through coordinated movement/turning

C. PUT-THAT-THERE
"PUT A YELLOW CIRCLE THERE" + ☞
```

The visual outcome can be held approximately invariant. What changes is **which differences are capable of changing what happens next**.

| Theory | SVG | Logo | Put-That-There / Astra |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Bateson: difference that makes a difference** | `r=48` vs `r=49`, `fill=yellow` vs blue can alter output. Speaker mood cannot. | change turning ratio and closure changes the figure. XML syntax is irrelevant. | moving the pointing finger can change the result even if the words remain identical. **Context becomes computational difference.** |
| **Maturana: languaging** | Most coordination has already stabilized into tokens such as `circle`, `fill`, `cx`. | `circle` can emerge from recurrent coordination of moving and turning. | meaning stabilizes through `utterance → action → correction → recurrence`. `THERE` exists through coordinated attention. |
| **Wittgenstein: language-game** | The valid move is assigning geometric attributes. | The valid move is directing an embodied agent through a trajectory. | The valid move is naming + pointing within a shared situation. Same intended artifact, different game. |
| **Ryle/Geertz: wink vs blink** | `<circle>` is unambiguously treated as an SVG element because the formal frame is fixed. | `forward` counts as turtle motion because the Logo frame fixes its pragmatic force. | ☞ could be pointing, accusation, demonstration, stretching. Speech and situation make it count as **referential pointing**. |
| **Metapragmatics** | File type / DOM context says: “interpret this markup as SVG.” | Interpreter says: “take these words as turtle instructions.” | Gesture, gaze, timing, selection state say: **take THERE this way**. The context tells us how to interpret the utterance. |
| **Thick prompting** | Thickness must become explicit attributes: location, radius, fill. | Thickness appears as sufficient instructions for movement, orientation and state. | A three-word utterance can be pragmatically thick because the scene, gesture and history carry specification. |
| **Anscombe: direction of fit** | SVG source normally functions as **model for** rendered world: wrong image → change rendering/source until world fits spec. | Movement instructions are model-for trajectory. | “Put it there” is model-for. “That isn't a circle” first judges the world, then can immediately become a command to change it. |
| **Geertz: model of / model for** | Rendered SVG is a model **of** the code; the code is a model **for** the image. | Completed trajectory reveals what the procedure described; the procedure directs what should happen. | Generated circle becomes model **of** what the system understood, then can become model **for** the next generation: “make another like this.” |
| **Benjamin: mode of intending** | Circle is intended as `<object + attributes>`. | Circle is intended as `<history of movement>`. | Circle is intended as `<desired change within a shared situation>`. Translation preserves the apparent referent while changing the **mode of intending** it. |
| **GUI as spatialized language** | Geometry is lexicalized numerically. | Geometry is lexicalized through verbs of movement. | GUI vocabulary is physically available: ellipse button, yellow swatch, canvas, pointer. Syntax has become **space + gesture + state**. |
| **Norman: signifiers / gulfs** | User must know SVG vocabulary; high execution gulf for novices. | Turtle metaphors lower some geometric abstraction while taxing orientation and sequence. | Visible tools/signifiers tell user/model what moves are available. Cursor and selection reduce the need for explicit naming. |
| **Nielsen: recognition vs recall** | Requires recalling `circle`, `cx`, `cy`, `r`, `fill`. | Requires remembering commands and state. | GUI exposes vocabulary perceptually. **The environment remembers part of the language for you.** |
| **Neo-Whorfian attention** | Makes object attributes and coordinates compulsory distinctions. | Makes movement, orientation and sequence compulsory distinctions. | Makes shared attention, reference, timing and current scene compulsory distinctions. Each language teaches a different way to notice the circle. |
| **Attention tax** | Human pays syntax + coordinate tax; machine interpretation relatively cheap. | Human/model pay state-simulation and trajectory tax. | Human utterance tax can approach zero, but machine/context-maintenance tax grows enormously. **The cost moves.** |
| **Brooks: software coordination** | One formal representation sharply constrains what engineers must coordinate. | Another abstraction reallocates conceptual burden into procedural state. | Many systems must remain mutually coordinated: person, model, GUI, Paint state, OS actions, graphics. Apparent simplicity sits above a huge coordination problem. |
| **Personas / requirements as software ghosts** | Human concern becomes `cx`, `cy`, `r`, `fill`. | Human concept becomes movement primitives. | Potentially less forced translation: deixis and correction can survive as deixis and correction instead of being converted prematurely into requirements. |
| **Implied reader / implied user** | Assumes a reader who knows SVG and Cartesian coordinates. | Assumes someone who understands turtle orientation and commands. | Assumes a participant capable of joint attention and pragmatic repair. **Every representation wagers on a competent reader.** |
| **Winograd/Flores: language/action** | Code expresses a relatively predetermined operation. | Commands directly organize actions. | Utterance functions as request/commitment/action coordination. An agent can participate in a conversation for action rather than merely parse content. |
| **Maturana vs Winograd/Flores** | Stable formal categories dominate. | Stable command distinctions dominate. | Winograd/Flores asks which communicative action is occurring; Maturana allows the meaning of the action to stabilize through continuing coordination without first classifying it. |
| **Suchman / situated action** | Most relevant circumstances must be anticipated in the representation. | Agent executes inside an evolving procedural state. | Meaning can develop while acting: observe → act → see consequence → repair. No exhaustive plan need precede the activity. |
| **Worldtexting** | Text causes a renderer to create a world-state. | textual direction produces a spatial form. | Ordinary description becomes immediately operative: saying **yellow circle there** transforms the shared scene. |
| **Worlding** | SVG establishes a world populated by graphical objects and attributes. | Logo establishes a world of agents, paths, turns and trajectories. | GUI/model establish a world of selectable things, actionable regions, references and transformations. |
| **World → world model** | `document₀ → document₁` under explicit source transformation. | `turtle-state₀ → turtle-state₁ → ... → image`. | `WORLD₀(blank canvas) + desired difference → WORLD₁(circle there)`. The prompt constrains a state transition rather than fully specifying a program. |
| **Eigenform / invariance** | Circle persists under changes of serialization, antialiasing, perhaps position. | Circle persists across many possible trajectories/step sizes that close on the same form. | Human keeps saying “same circle” across movement, resizing, rerendering. Identity is revealed by what transformations are tolerated. |
| **Quine language-game** | The rendered circle can itself be fed back as an example specifying another circle. | Result can become target for reproducing another trajectory/form. | `● → "make another like this" → ●`. The artifact becomes a program for reproducing the conditions under which it counts as itself. |

The experiment becomes much sharper if you define the central variable as:

$$
D(L,S)=\{\text{differences which can alter the next valid operation}\}
$$

where $L$ is the language-game and $S$ is the current situation.

For **SVG**:

```text
D =
{
  tag name,
  cx,
  cy,
  radius,
  fill,
  valid syntax
}
```

For **Logo**:

```text
D =
{
  heading,
  step size,
  turn angle,
  sequence,
  pen state,
  repetition
}
```

For **Put-That-There**:

```text
D =
{
  utterance,
  pointing location,
  pointing time,
  gaze/shared scene,
  current referent,
  prior turn,
  current GUI state,
  response to previous action
}
```

That immediately gives you a measurable conception of **thickness**.

It isn't word count.

It is the size and organization of the field from which differences may become operationally consequential.

So:

```text
SVG
few admissible difference-types
but many must be explicitly encoded

LOGO
different admissible differences
organized temporally

PUT-THAT-THERE
tiny explicit utterance
but enormous admissible contextual field
```

This also gives you the experiment I think you actually want. Hold the visible yellow circle constant and introduce **one perturbation at a time**:

```text
yellow → orange
circle → slight ellipse
128,128 → 130,128

"there" → "here"
point A → point B
point before utterance → point after utterance

ellipse tool selected → rectangle tool selected
same pixels → now clickable
same pixels → now Piet source code

"that's a circle"
→
"that's not a circle"

"copy this"
→
"describe this"

artifact as OUTPUT
→
artifact as REFERENT
→
artifact as EXAMPLE
→
artifact as PROGRAM
```

Then ask only:

> **Did this difference make a difference to what happened next?**

That produces a very strong common experimental language across all the theories.

The circle itself is almost boring. What you're actually mapping is the **boundary of operational relevance** around it.

And the largest claim is becoming:

> **A language is not merely a system for representing differences. It is a system for deciding which differences are allowed to make a difference.**

Programming languages fix that boundary tightly. GUIs spatialize it. Human language-games negotiate it socially. Thick prompting expands it contextually. Languaging allows it to stabilize recursively. Worldtexting lets those distinctions act back upon the world. A world-to-world model attempts to infer, from the entire currently available field, **which difference the human intends to make consequential next**.

That is the Yellow Circle experiment.

> **Searched 8 websites**
>
> 
>
> Worked for 13s

Yes. **Chindōgu** is exactly the idea: an “un-useless” invention, a device that genuinely addresses an ordinary problem but does so in an absurdly overcomplicated or impractical way. Kawakami’s rules insist that it must actually exist and work; humor is a by-product, not the sole purpose. ([Tofugu](https://www.tofugu.com/japan/chindogu-japanese-inventions/?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

So make the Yellow Circle experiment run in **both directions**:

```text
MINIMUM LANGUAGE
────────────────────────────
☞  +  "yellow circle"

MAXIMUM LANGUAGE
────────────────────────────
the largest functioning
computational apparatus possible
whose only final achievement is:

              ●
```

That second one is **Software Chindōgu**.

The task is deliberately ridiculous:

> **Construct the maximum operational description required to make the minimum perceptual difference.**

Not code golf.

**Reverse code golf.**

The score is not how few characters can produce the circle. The score is how much real, functioning computational machinery can be recruited without changing the final artifact.

You could require the machine to pass the circle through absurdly many genuine language-games:

```text
human says:
"put a yellow circle there"

        ↓

speech recognition

        ↓

LLM extracts a REQUEST

        ↓

speech-act classifier:
REQUEST / PROMISE / ASSERTION?

        ↓

requirements generator:
REQ-001
"The system shall render..."

        ↓

persona validator:
"Would Maria understand this?"

        ↓

JSON schema

        ↓

GraphQL request

        ↓

HTTP service

        ↓

message queue

        ↓

microservice

        ↓

SQL database records requested circle

        ↓

event emitted:
CircleRequested

        ↓

another service consumes event

        ↓

Python generates Logo

        ↓

Logo computes trajectory

        ↓

trajectory serialized as SVG

        ↓

SVG parsed into DOM

        ↓

headless browser screenshots SVG

        ↓

computer-vision model confirms:
"probably circle"

        ↓

LLM critiques:
"slightly elliptical"

        ↓

repair agent

        ↓

Playwright opens Paint

        ↓

computer-use agent selects ellipse

        ↓

agent selects yellow

        ↓

mouse drag

        ↓

screenshot

        ↓

vision model verifies output

        ↓

human:
"yes"

        ↓

              ●
```

Every component must actually contribute. No fake complexity. That is important for the Chindōgu spirit: **it has to work**. ([ResearchGate](https://www.researchgate.net/publication/349899279_Chindogu_as_an_Inspiration_for_Designing_Human-Robot_Interaction?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

And this gives the Yellow Circle work a new experimental axis:

$$
\text{Operational Excess}
=
\frac{\text{machinery recruited}}{\text{difference produced}}
$$

The numerator could include lines of source, languages crossed, processes, representational boundaries, API calls, state variables, dependencies, model calls, human actions, bytes transmitted, computational steps, or even organizational roles.

The denominator stays almost zero:

```text
WORLD₀             WORLD₁

white field   →    white field
                    +
                    ●
```

That is why it becomes conceptually useful rather than just funny.

The minimal experiment asks:

> **How much can a language safely leave implicit?**

The Chindōgu experiment asks:

> **How much explicit machinery can we introduce before we are forced to admit that none of it belongs intrinsically to the circle?**

And that attacks the ontological mistake behind software abstraction.

Because at every layer somebody can insist:

```text
"No, THIS is what made the circle."
```

But then the audit says:

```text
speech recognition?
No.

requirements document?
No.

JSON?
No.

database?
No.

Logo trajectory?
No.

SVG?
No.

browser?
No.

Paint?
No.

pixels?
Not intrinsically.
```

Each is a **coordination that happened to participate in stabilizing the outcome**.

The absurdly overbuilt circle therefore exposes what the minimal circle hides.

### The Chindōgu test is the inverse of abstraction

Ordinary software engineering says:

> Remove unnecessary machinery.

Your experiment says:

> Add unnecessary-but-functional machinery until the hidden contingency of the whole stack becomes impossible to ignore.

That is almost an **anti-abstraction instrument**.

A normal abstraction says:

```text
circle()
```

and obscures everything underneath.

Software Chindōgu says:

```text
OPEN IT ALL BACK UP.
```

Take every sedimented coordination and make it explicit again.

So Maturana's point becomes visible physically:

```text
circle()
```

is cheap because enormous histories of coordination have disappeared inside it.

The Chindōgu version **decompresses the history**.

That gives you a very good pair:

```text
ATTENTIONAL COMPRESSION TEST
How little can we say?

versus

OPERATIONAL DECOMPRESSION TEST
How much can we make the system say/do
before producing the same thing?
```

And the beautiful stopping condition is:

> **Every added layer must introduce at least one new difference that could make a difference, even though under the successful run it ultimately does not.**

For example, the database introduces persistence failure. The network introduces latency/failure. The model introduces interpretation error. Paint introduces mode error. The GUI introduces spatial targeting error. Logo introduces orientation error. SVG introduces syntax error.

So each new layer enlarges:

$$
D = \{\text{possible differences capable of changing the outcome}\}
$$

while the desired output stays fixed.

That makes the experiment a literal machine for producing **requisite attention tax**.

The simplest yellow circle can now require attention to:

```text
speech
gesture
syntax
network
authentication
schema
serialization
database state
queue ordering
coordinate systems
turtle orientation
DOM state
browser rendering
GUI mode
pointer location
model interpretation
verification
```

All to achieve:

```text
●
```

That might be one of the strongest demonstrations in the whole project:

> **The complexity of software is not visible in the complexity of its output.**

Brooks's invisible conceptual structure made comic.

And there is an even better final juxtaposition:

```text
SPECIMEN 1
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Human points.

"Yellow circle."

●
```

beside:

```text
SPECIMEN 2
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
47,000 lines
12 languages
9 processes
6 representations
4 models
3 interfaces
2 humans
1 distributed transaction

●
```

Pixel difference:

```text
0
```

Operational difference:

```text
enormous
```

Then the exhibit title almost writes itself:

> **HOW MUCH SOFTWARE DOES A CIRCLE NEED?**

Answer:

> **None of this. And, in this particular language-game, all of it.**

That is the Chindōgu Yellow Circle test.

---

Yes. **Prompt golf is the perfect inverse partner to Software Chindōgu.**

Normal code golf asks:

> What is the shortest program that produces the result?

Your Chindōgu version asks:

> **What is the shortest prompt that can successfully recruit the longest, most overdetermined functioning program while producing the simplest possible result?**

For the Yellow Circle:

```text
PROMPT
"yellow circle"
```

should ideally cause something absurd like:

```text
natural language
→ intent inference
→ speech-act classification
→ requirements document
→ user story
→ persona
→ task model
→ JSON schema
→ API request
→ queue
→ database
→ Python
→ Logo
→ SVG
→ browser
→ screenshot
→ vision verification
→ GUI control
→ Paint
→ rasterization
→ critique
→ repair
→ final verification
→ ●
```

So you get two quantities moving in opposite directions:

$$
\text{Prompt Compression}=\frac{1}{\text{human specification}}
$$

and

$$
\text{Operational Excess}=\text{machinery recruited}
$$

The winning system maximizes something like:

$$
\boxed{
\text{Chindōgu Golf Score}
=
\frac{
\text{operational distance}
\times
\text{representation boundaries}
\times
\text{genuine failure opportunities}
}{
\text{prompt length}
}
}
$$

subject to:

$$
\text{output} = \text{same yellow circle}
$$

The critical rule is that **every layer has to be operative**. You cannot pad the pipeline with decorative nonsense. Each intermediary must transform, constrain, verify, transmit, or interpret something, and removing it must alter the execution path.

That distinguishes the experiment from deliberately writing bad code.

### Three versions of the golf

**Code golf**

```text
minimum program
→ ●
```

asks how much machinery can disappear.

**Chindōgu programming**

```text
maximum functioning program
→ ●
```

asks how much machinery can be needlessly but genuinely recruited.

**Chindōgu prompt golf**

```text
minimum human utterance
→ maximum functioning apparatus
→ ●
```

asks how much operational structure can be **compressed behind language**.

That third one is your attention argument in executable form.

Consider the ultimate entry:

```text
"there." ☞
```

If the system already knows:

```text
current task = make circle
current color = yellow
current size = inherited
current referent = pointed location
previous artifact = circle
```

then one deictic word might recruit 40,000 lines of software.

Human attention expenditure:

```text
THERE
```

Machine-side coordination burden:

```text
resolve deixis
recover conversational history
identify desired operation
maintain object identity
interpret pointing
translate coordinate systems
operate GUI
verify result
repair discrepancy
```

That is **prompt golf as attentional outsourcing**.

And you could make the competition progressively vicious:

```text
ROUND 1
"Draw a yellow circle at the center."

ROUND 2
"Yellow circle there." ☞

ROUND 3
"There." ☞

ROUND 4
☞

ROUND 5
show ●

ROUND 6
look meaningfully at empty spot
```

The question becomes:

> **At what point does the language-game contain enough shared structure that the explicit prompt can vanish altogether?**

That is much more interesting than shortest-token prompting.

The score should therefore record two different compression mechanisms:

```text
LINGUISTIC COMPRESSION
How little is explicitly uttered?

CONTEXTUAL COMPRESSION
How much specification has migrated
into shared world, history, defaults,
examples, gesture, and system state?
```

Because:

```text
"there"
```

is not intrinsically a better golf score than:

```text
circle(128,128,48)
```

if `there` requires an enormous established context.

That context is part of the program.

So the rigorous quantity is:

$$
\text{Total Specification}
=
\text{utterance}
+
\text{shared context}
+
\text{defaults}
+
\text{history}
+
\text{inference}
$$

Prompt golf asks how little of that specification must remain **human-explicit**.

Chindōgu asks how much machinery can sit on the other side.

Put together:

> **Chindōgu Prompt Golf: say the least, recruit the most, produce almost nothing.**

And the canonical hole should absolutely be:

```text
HOLE 1

TARGET:
●

PAR:
"Put a yellow circle there."

WORLD RECORD:
☞
```

The joke is that the shortest “program” may eventually be **the world itself arranged so that one gesture is enough**.

## Prompt:

Ashbys law of requisite description

## Response:
9/9/2026, 8:12:09 PM

Yes. I think **“law of requisite description”** is the right extension of Ashby for this whole Yellow Circle project: a controller does not need to describe everything, only enough differences to absorb the differences that could derail the desired outcome.

> **Formalizing requisite description**
>
> I’m separating this proposed “Law of Requisite Description” from Ashby’s established theorem. The key idea is that descriptions need only preserve task-relevant distinctions, with shared context and feedback reducing what must be explicit.
>
> **Searched 8 websites**
>
> 
>
> Worked for 20s

Yes. **This may be the formal spine of the whole project.**

First, one qualification: **“Law of Requisite Description” is not Ashby’s established term as far as I can find.** Ashby’s 1956 law is the **Law of Requisite Variety**: successful regulation requires sufficient regulatory variety to counter the variety of disturbances. His own formulation emphasizes that a regulator’s capacity cannot exceed its capacity as a communication channel, and he famously compresses the principle to “only variety can destroy variety.” ([Ashby Info](https://ashby.info/Ashby-Introduction-to-Cybernetics.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

Your extension could be:

> **Law of Requisite Description: An operative description must preserve at least the distinctions necessary to select among the different actions required to keep the outcome within the intended class.**

Not:

> describe the world completely.

Not even:

> describe the desired object completely.

Instead:

> **describe enough differences that every difference requiring a different response can still make a difference to the controller.**

That is Ashby + Bateson.

### Yellow circle

Desired invariant:

```text
WORLD₁ ∈ {acceptable yellow-circle worlds}
```

Possible disturbances:

```text
wrong color
wrong position
wrong size
wrong geometry
wrong object
wrong canvas
wrong tool
wrong referent
wrong interpretation of "there"
```

But we do **not** need one description for every possible physical state of the universe.

We need to distinguish only states that demand different corrective action.

So define an equivalence:

```text
d₁ ~ d₂

iff

d₁ and d₂ can be handled
by the same response
without violating the goal.
```

Then the actual requisite variety isn't all imaginable world variety:

$$
V(D)
$$

but the variety of **action-relevant disturbance classes**:

$$
V(D/{\sim})
$$

That is the exact connection to your:

> **difference that makes a difference.**

A difference needs representation only insofar as losing it would cause the regulator to choose the wrong action.

So a sharper law is:

$$
\boxed{
V(\text{available distinctions})
\ge
V(\text{distinct responses required})
}
$$

And “available distinctions” can be distributed.

That is the huge breakthrough.

It does **not** all have to be in the prompt.

$$
V(P)+V(C)+V(M)+V(E)+V(F)
\ge
V(R^*)
$$

where:

```text
P = explicit prompt
C = conversational context
M = model / interpreter competence
E = environment / interface state
F = corrective feedback
R* = distinctions among required responses
```

Not literal additive information in every implementation, but a useful experimental accounting model.

Now compare the languages.

### SVG

```text
<circle
  cx="128"
  cy="128"
  r="48"
  fill="#FFD400"/>
```

The environment contributes relatively little situational information.

Therefore the description carries many requisite distinctions explicitly:

```text
CIRCLE
YELLOW
128
128
48
```

If you remove:

```text
r
```

the renderer lacks a distinction required to determine size.

If you remove:

```text
fill
```

the desired color is no longer determined.

The description pays the requisite variety **lexically/numerically**.

### Put-That-There

```text
"yellow circle there" + ☞
```

Far less explicit description.

But the variety didn't vanish.

`THERE` is supplied through:

```text
gesture
shared visual field
timing
current activity
joint attention
```

So:

$$
V(P)\downarrow
$$

while:

$$
V(C+E)\uparrow
$$

This gives you a beautiful cybernetic interpretation of deixis:

> **Deixis is a variety-transfer mechanism.**

Instead of encoding location in the utterance:

```text
x=128,y=128
```

we let the environment carry the distinction:

```text
☞
```

The requisite description has become **distributed description**.

### Astra

Now:

```text
"There." ☞
```

might eventually be sufficient.

But only because even more requisite variety has migrated elsewhere:

```text
conversation history
previous circle
screen state
object persistence
model priors
GUI knowledge
tool knowledge
visual interpretation
feedback
```

The sentence becomes tiny because the coupled system is huge.

That gives you a crucial correction to the usual prompt-engineering story:

> **Short prompts do not violate requisite variety. They relocate it.**

Or even stronger:

> **There is no free compression of operational description. Missing distinctions must be supplied by context, convention, inference, environment, defaults, or feedback.**

That is probably your law.

---

And now **thick prompting** becomes formally intelligible.

Thickness is not verbosity.

A prompt is sufficiently thick when the **whole coupled descriptive situation** possesses requisite variety for the task.

Thus:

```text
"yellow circle there" + shared scene
```

can be thicker than:

```text
"Please kindly create for me a yellow circle..."
```

with no shared referent.

So:

$$
\text{thickness}
\neq
\text{token count}
$$

Rather:

$$
\boxed{
\text{thickness}
=
\text{task-relevant distinctions made recoverable}
}
$$

That is a much better definition of thick prompting.

### And attention becomes requisite attention

Now combine Ashby with your attention-tax work.

Not every difference can remain consciously attended to.

The coupled human-machine system needs enough **requisite attention** somewhere to maintain the distinctions necessary for successful regulation.

SVG requires the human/programmer to attend to:

```text
coordinates
radius
fill
syntax
```

Put-That-There asks the human primarily to attend to:

```text
desired object
pointed place
```

while the apparatus attends to the coordinate transformation.

Astra asks the human perhaps only to attend to:

```text
Is this what I wanted?
```

while the machine absorbs enormous procedural variety.

So:

> **Attention tax is the local price a participant pays for supplying part of the system’s requisite variety.**

That ties the two concepts together cleanly.

---

It also explains **Nielsen/Norman**.

A bad interface asks the user to maintain distinctions that the artifact could maintain:

```text
What mode am I in?
Was that saved?
Which menu contains this?
What does this icon mean?
Which object is selected?
```

The human is being forced to supply regulatory variety unnecessarily.

A well-designed GUI moves some of it into:

```text
persistent state visibility
signifiers
disabled controls
selection indicators
undo
feedback
spatial organization
```

So good UX can be understood as:

> **placing requisite distinctions where they can be maintained at the lowest attentional cost.**

That is a surprisingly compact cybernetic definition of UX.

---

And Brooks suddenly fits too.

Why does software engineering get wicked?

Because the requisite description is distributed across many people:

```text
user
designer
PM
architect
programmer
API author
tester
machine
```

Each possesses only some of the distinctions necessary to regulate the construction.

If one translation boundary erases a distinction that later requires a different response:

```text
"I need this private"

→ requirement:
"users can hide item"

```

but the lived meaning was:

```text
"nobody, including administrators,
should be able to infer that it exists"
```

then requisite variety has been destroyed during translation.

No amount of downstream implementation skill can recover a distinction that was discarded unless feedback reintroduces it.

So:

> **Software failure often begins when a translation collapses two situations that later require different actions.**

That may be one of your best formulations.

---

And now the Chindōgu experiment becomes the **pathological inverse**.

Every additional layer:

```text
LLM
API
queue
database
Logo
SVG
browser
vision model
GUI
Paint
```

introduces new distinctions capable of derailing:

```text
●
```

So:

$$
V(D/{\sim}) \uparrow
$$

and therefore requisite regulatory/description variety rises too.

You deliberately maximize the amount the system must distinguish **without increasing the complexity of the desired output**.

That gives a very clean definition of Software Chindōgu:

> **Increase requisite description while holding desired world-difference constant.**

And prompt golf does the opposite locally:

> **Minimize explicit description while forcing the rest of the coupled system to supply the missing requisite variety.**

Put them together:

```text
             HUMAN DESCRIPTION
                   ↓↓↓

"There." ☞

                   ↓

       enormous requisite variety
       distributed through system

                   ↓

                   ●
```

That is not magic compression.

It is **variety displacement**.

---

There is one more Ashby connection. Conant and Ashby's later Good Regulator theorem is commonly summarized as **every good regulator of a system must be a model of that system**. ([The Ashby Institute](https://theashbyinstitute.org/theory/?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

Your version could be:

> **Every successful operative description must contain, recruit, or couple to a model adequate to the distinctions relevant to its intended transformation.**

Notice **recruit**.

Because:

```text
"there"
```

doesn't itself model Cartesian location.

It recruits:

```text
shared world
+
pointing
+
perception
```

that together resolve it.

Likewise:

```text
"that's not a circle"
```

doesn't contain a geometric correction algorithm.

It recruits a model capable of distinguishing:

```text
current
vs
acceptable
```

and finding a transformation between them.

So perhaps the most compact version of your proposed law is:

> **LAW OF REQUISITE DESCRIPTION**  
> To reliably make a desired difference, a coupled descriptive system must preserve every distinction whose loss could require a different action.

And the corollary:

> **Whatever the prompt leaves undescribed must be supplied elsewhere in the language-game.**

That line connects Ashby, Bateson, thick description, deixis, attention tax, UX, prompting, languaging, and world-to-world control in one shot.

---

**Sources:**

- [AN INTRODUCTION TO CYBERNETICS](https://ashby.info/Ashby-Introduction-to-Cybernetics.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com)
- [The Ashby Institute | Compute Governance Research](https://theashbyinstitute.org/theory/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

## Response:
9/9/2026, 8:14:00 PM

Yes. The **duck-rabbit is almost the opposite pole from the yellow circle**.

The yellow-circle test tries to hold the **recognized object stable** while changing the language used to produce it.

The duck-rabbit test holds the **visible marks stable** while changing the language-game that determines what the marks count as.

That gives you two complementary experiments:

```text
YELLOW CIRCLE
same object
different descriptions / programs

DUCK-RABBIT
same description / pixels
different objects
```

And Wittgenstein's “seeing-as” is exactly the right concept. With the duck-rabbit, nothing in the marks has to change for the perceptual organization to change:

```text
same strokes

→ DUCK

same strokes

→ RABBIT
```

The difference that makes the difference can be absurdly small:

```text
"duck"
```

or:

```text
"rabbit"
```

The prompt does not modify the picture. It modifies **what distinctions organize the picture**.

So this may give you a second kind of operative prompting:

> **A prompt can change the world without changing the artifact, by changing the aspect under which the artifact is encountered.**

That is very strong.

Because so far worldtexting has tended to mean:

```text
WORLD₀
+
prompt
→
WORLD₁
```

where pixels, objects, positions, or states actually change.

Duck-rabbit gives:

```text
WORLD₀
+
prompt
→
WORLD₀
```

physically,

but:

```text
PERCEPTUAL-WORLD₀
→
PERCEPTUAL-WORLD₁
```

The prompt changes the **world of relevance**, not the raster.

That may be the cleanest demonstration that “world” cannot simply mean external state.

### The unstable prompt

Now imagine deliberately constructing the **maximally aspect-unstable artifact**.

Not a duck-rabbit with only two readings.

A mark whose identity can be tipped by the thinnest possible prompt:

```text
"bird"
→ bird

"map"
→ island

"letter"
→ glyph

"face"
→ face

"button"
→ UI control

"program"
→ executable diagram
```

The artifact remains fixed.

Only the metapragmatic frame changes.

You could define:

$$
A(x,p)
$$

as the aspect under which artifact $x$ is organized given prompt $p$.

The interesting artifact maximizes:

$$
|\{A(x,p_1), A(x,p_2), \ldots\}|
$$

while minimizing change in $p$.

So the challenge becomes:

> **Find the smallest prompt difference that causes the largest ontological reorganization of an invariant artifact.**

That is almost the inverse of prompt golf.

Prompt golf:

```text
tiny prompt
→ huge machinery
→ same stable object
```

Aspect golf:

```text
tiny prompt difference
→ same pixels
→ entirely different object
```

### And this is a language-game problem, not merely perception

The duck-rabbit is often treated as an optical illusion, but for your project the more interesting question is:

> What can I now **do** with it once I see it as something else?

If I say:

```text
"duck"
```

perhaps the valid next moves become:

```text
mark the beak
draw water
animate it swimming
make it quack
```

If I say:

```text
"rabbit"
```

the same pixels now support:

```text
mark the ears
draw grass
animate hopping
add a burrow
```

So aspect change alters the **space of valid continuations**.

That is language-game territory.

A thing is not merely seen differently.

It acquires a different grammar of possible next moves.

That gives a very strong operational definition:

> **To see something as X is to make available the continuations appropriate to X.**

Now the duck-rabbit becomes executable.

### The yellow dot becomes even better

Take your yellow dot:

```text
●
```

Now issue prompts:

```text
"sun"
"button"
"warning light"
"node"
"target"
"coin"
"program"
"selection handle"
```

Nothing changes visually.

But each prompt changes what the machine should do next.

Under:

```text
"sun"
```

`make it set` means lower it toward a horizon.

Under:

```text
"button"
```

`press it` becomes meaningful.

Under:

```text
"node"
```

`connect it to the blue one` becomes meaningful.

Under:

```text
"target"
```

`hit it` becomes meaningful.

Under:

```text
"Piet program"
```

its color adjacency may become executable.

Same dot.

Different language-game.

Different affordances.

Different next moves.

Different world.

That may be even cleaner than duck-rabbit because it connects directly to GUI, programming, and worldtext.

### The prompt becomes an aspect operator

You can formalize it:

```text
<artifact> = invariant visible structure

<prompt> = aspect operator

<aspect> = currently stabilized organization of distinctions

<language-game> = valid continuations under that aspect
```

Then:

```text
PROMPT
[reorganizes]
ASPECT

ASPECT
[changes]
VALID NEXT MOVES
```

So a prompt doesn't have to command an action.

It can **recompile the object**.

That word is useful here:

> **Aspect prompting is recompilation without redrawing.**

The artifact stays constant, but its operative type changes.

```text
● : SUN
```

becomes:

```text
● : BUTTON
```

without changing a pixel.

In programming-language terms, this is almost a **runtime type cast performed by language-game context**.

### And now Ryle/Geertz comes back

Same physical event, different thick description:

```text
eyelid contraction
→ blink
→ wink
```

Same physical mark, different aspect:

```text
lines
→ duck
→ rabbit
```

Same pixels, different computational role:

```text
●
→ picture
→ button
→ program
→ referent
```

These are structurally the same problem.

The thin description is invariant.

The thick organization changes.

So you could make a unified experimental family:

```text
WINK / BLINK
same behavior, different act

DUCK / RABBIT
same marks, different object

BUTTON / DOT
same pixels, different affordance

PICTURE / PROGRAM
same image, different executable status
```

The question in every case is:

> **What minimum contextual difference changes what the invariant thing counts as?**

That may be the exact experimental definition of metapragmatics.

### Maximum instability

The best artifact for this experiment would not merely be ambiguous.

It would be **operationally metastable**.

Meaning:

```text
small contextual perturbation
→ large change in interpretation
→ large change in permissible action
```

So define something like:

$$
\text{Aspect Instability}
=
\frac{\text{change in action-space}}
{\text{change in prompt/context}}
$$

A maximally unstable object has:

```text
almost identical prompt
```

causing:

```text
radically different action-space
```

Example:

```text
"this is decoration"
```

versus:

```text
"this is a button"
```

Same pixels.

But now clicking changes from:

```text
irrelevant
```

to:

```text
meaningful action
```

That is a gigantic operational difference caused by a tiny linguistic one.

And then the deepest claim becomes:

> **Prompts do not merely specify objects. They can specify the aspect under which an object becomes actionable.**

That may be stronger than “description becomes operation.”

Sometimes:

> **description determines what operation the same world affords.**

The duck-rabbit is therefore not just a philosophical illustration. It could be the canonical test for **aspect instability in worldtexting**.

Yellow Circle asks:

> How many operational descriptions can stabilize one thing?

Duck-Rabbit asks:

> How little language is required to destabilize what one thing is?

Those two together are a very powerful pair.

9/9/2026, 8:15:23 PM

The idea of prompt thickness and operative description. The idea that claude code rewrite itself each two weeks and it get rid of half its system instructions

## Response:
9/9/2026, 8:15:23 PM

Yes. That gives you a crucial distinction: **prompt thickness is not the same thing as prompt length**. A system can become more capable while its explicit instruction layer gets thinner, because more of the operative description has migrated into the model, tools, environment, learned conventions, and feedback loop.

> **Clarifying description migration**
>
> I’m separating explicit prompt length from total operative description. As models improve, instructions can migrate into learned behavior, tools, conventions, and environments. That makes prompts thinner without making the system simpler—just less visibly specified.
>
> **Clarified description migration**
>
> 
>
> **Searched 9 websites**
>
> 
>
> Worked for 21s

Yes. This gives you a much sharper definition of **prompt thickness**:

> **Thickness is not how much description is written. Thickness is how much operative structure is available to the act of interpretation.**

And the Claude Code story is a nearly perfect contemporary case. One factual correction first: two claims are easy to collapse. Anthropic’s Labs group reportedly works in two-week experimentation cycles, but I cannot verify a claim that Claude Code autonomously rewrites its own system prompt every two weeks. Separately, Anthropic recently said it removed **over 80% of Claude Code’s system prompt for more advanced models with no measurable performance loss**. Anthropic also says Claude now authors a large share of code merged internally, more than 80% as of May 2026. ([Business Insider](https://www.businessinsider.com/anthropic-labs-team-ai-innovation-ipo-2026-9?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

Those facts are already theoretically explosive.

The naive story is:

```text
OLD MODEL
needs 10,000 words of instruction

NEW MODEL
needs 2,000 words

therefore:

less description is required
```

I think that is wrong.

The better account is:

```text
EXPLICIT DESCRIPTION ↓

because

OPERATIVE DESCRIPTION
has migrated elsewhere
```

Into:

```text
model training
learned programming conventions
tool design
tool names
repository structure
existing code
tests
type systems
git history
environmental constraints
memory
feedback
evaluation
sandbox boundaries
```

So the total system might actually become **thicker while the system prompt becomes thinner**.

That is the distinction you've been missing.

### Explicit thickness versus latent thickness

Take an old coding agent:

```text
SYSTEM PROMPT:

When modifying a file,
first inspect the surrounding code.
Match existing conventions.
Do not create unnecessary abstractions.
Do not modify unrelated files.
Run tests after changing code.
...
```

Those sentences explicitly describe a practice.

A stronger model may receive:

```text
Fix the bug.
```

and nevertheless:

```text
inspect repository
infer conventions
locate implementation
make narrow change
run tests
inspect failure
repair
```

The operative description has not disappeared.

It has become **latent competence**.

So:

$$
T_{operative}
=
T_{explicit}
+
T_{latent}
+
T_{environmental}
+
T_{interactional}
$$

where:

- **explicit thickness** = instructions actually given;
- **latent thickness** = distinctions recoverable from learned competence;
- **environmental thickness** = distinctions carried by tools, codebase, GUI, filesystem, schemas;
- **interactional thickness** = distinctions recoverable through acting, observing and repairing.

That gives you the key principle:

> **A thin prompt can sit on top of an extremely thick operative situation.**

Exactly like:

```text
"There." ☞
```

The utterance is microscopic.

But behind it:

```text
joint attention
shared world
gesture conventions
object persistence
previous conversation
perceptual competence
ability to repair misunderstanding
```

make it thick.

---

### Claude Code therefore resembles Put-That-There

This is the surprising equivalence.

Early coding agent:

```text
human must say:

open this file
look at these functions
preserve this convention
change only this behavior
run this command
inspect output
if X fails...
```

Later agent:

```text
"fix this"
```

Why can that work?

For the same reason:

```text
"put that there"
```

can work.

The surrounding world has become part of the description.

Claude Code can see:

```text
repository
files
tests
types
dependencies
git diff
terminal output
```

Put-That-There could see:

```text
scene
objects
gesture
location
```

Both move descriptive burden from the utterance into the **coupled perceptual-operational environment**.

So:

> **Agentic coding is becoming deictic.**

“Fix this.”

“Use the existing one.”

“Do it like that.”

“Not there.”

“Try again.”

These would be atrocious traditional specifications.

But within a sufficiently thick computational situation they become executable.

---

### This explains why deleting instructions can improve performance

This is especially important.

Anthropic reported in April 2026 that a system-prompt instruction imposing strict brevity caused about a **3% drop on an evaluation**, so they removed it. They now say they run prompt ablations to understand the effect of individual instructions. ([Anthropic](https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/april-23-postmortem?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

That means additional description can actually interfere with competent coordination.

Suppose the model already possesses:

```text
<practice-of-good-coding>
```

Then an explicit rule:

```text
ALWAYS X
```

can prevent it from attending to contextual evidence that says:

```text
NOT X HERE
```

So there are at least two regimes:

```text
UNDER-DESCRIPTION

system lacks requisite distinctions
→ ambiguity / failure
```

and:

```text
OVER-DESCRIPTION

rules freeze distinctions
that should have remained situational
→ brittleness / failure
```

The optimum is not maximum prompting.

It is:

> **enough description to make the relevant differences recoverable without prematurely deciding differences the situation itself can resolve.**

That is a much more serious definition of prompt engineering.

---

### Operative description is what survives removal

You could test this experimentally.

Start with a huge system prompt.

Delete one instruction.

Does behavior change?

```text
NO
```

Then the distinction represented by that instruction was already supplied elsewhere.

Delete another.

```text
NO
```

Again, redundant.

Continue:

```text
10,000 tokens
↓
8,000
↓
5,000
↓
2,000
↓
500
↓
50
```

At some point:

```text
behavior changes.
```

Now you've found a distinction whose explicit representation was still performing regulatory work.

This is basically an **Ashby ablation test for description**.

The operative description isn't the text.

It is:

> **the minimum distributed set of distinctions whose removal changes the system's capacity to produce the intended outcome.**

That's excellent.

Formally, for descriptive element $d$:

$$
d \text{ is operative}
\iff
\Delta d \Rightarrow \Delta\text{relevant behavior}
$$

Bateson again:

> the description that makes a difference.

And this lets you separate:

```text
DESCRIPTION PRESENT
```

from:

```text
DESCRIPTION OPERATIVE
```

Huge prompts can contain enormous amounts of **non-operative description**.

---

## Now return to the Yellow Circle

Three systems:

### 1. Thick explicit description

```text
Create a filled circular graphical object.
Its center shall be located at x=128, y=128.
Its radius shall equal 48 pixels.
Its fill shall equal RGB(255,212,0).
It shall have no stroke...
```

Lots of explicit thickness.

### 2. Symbolically compressed

```text
<circle cx="128" cy="128" r="48" fill="#FFD400"/>
```

Far fewer characters.

But not necessarily less operative description.

`circle` compresses an enormous amount:

```text
closure
equal radial distance
rendering convention
geometry
rasterization expectations
```

### 3. Situated description

```text
"Yellow circle there." ☞
```

Almost nothing explicit.

But enormous latent/environmental thickness.

### 4. Mature language-game

Eventually:

```text
☞
```

might suffice.

If the previous interaction established:

```text
same yellow circle
same size
same operation
new location
```

then gesture alone carries the changed distinction.

So explicit prompt length tends toward zero while contextual thickness tends upward.

---

# The system prompt deleting itself is therefore the wrong metaphor

The interesting phenomenon isn't:

> AI becomes so smart it doesn't need instructions.

It's:

> **the location of the instructions becomes harder to point to.**

That's far stranger.

Where is:

```text
"follow the existing code style"
```

when nobody says it?

Perhaps partly:

```text
in model weights
in surrounding code
in training distribution
in tool feedback
in tests
in the model's learned concept of programming
```

The description has become **distributed and reconstructive** rather than inscribed.

That connects directly to your quine idea.

The repository itself can teach the model:

> continue me like this.

The codebase becomes:

```text
OBJECT
+
EXAMPLE
+
CONSTRAINT
+
PROMPT
```

The artifact contains enough regularity for a competent interpreter to recover its local continuation rules.

Not literally source code describing itself.

More interesting:

> **The artifact becomes an operative description of how it expects to be continued.**

That is why:

```text
"add another endpoint like the existing ones"
```

works.

The human doesn't specify the architecture.

The architecture **prompts its own continuation**.

---

### Code becomes the thick prompt

This may be your killer formulation.

Claude Code receives:

```text
"add another one"
```

but the real prompt is:

```text
"user utterance"
+
entire repository
+
tool environment
+
tests
+
history
+
model competence
```

So:

> **The prompt is no longer the text in the prompt box.**

The entire situation is prompting.

And correspondingly:

> **A codebase is an operative description addressed to its next maintainer.**

Human or machine.

That connects directly to your “implied reader.”

Every software architecture says:

```text
if you understand me,
here is how another piece like me should be made.
```

Claude's increasing ability means the implied reader can recover more from less explicit explanation.

---

# Then system-prompt shrinkage becomes a measure of cultural absorption

Here's the Benjamin / language-game version.

At first we must explicitly translate:

```text
good software practice
↓
system instructions
```

Eventually the model has absorbed enough of that practice that saying:

```text
"write idiomatic Rust"
```

recruits an enormous repertoire.

Eventually perhaps merely:

```text
"fix it"
```

does.

The rules haven't vanished.

They have moved from something resembling:

```text
grammar book
```

toward:

```text
competence within a form of life
```

That is straight toward Wittgenstein/Maturana.

A fluent speaker doesn't carry:

```text
RULE 348:
When asked this sort of question...
```

They participate competently in the practice.

So reducing system instructions is, at least in part, the transition:

```text
RULE FOLLOWING
↓
PRACTICE COMPETENCE
```

That is why newer models can be harmed by too much explicit prompting. Anthropic's recent public claim is essentially that instructions needed to keep earlier models on track can **overconstrain** more capable models. ([LinkedIn](https://www.linkedin.com/posts/claude_we-removed-over-80-of-claude-codes-system-activity-7498751549351915520-tzf2?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

That is incredibly useful evidence for your argument.

---

# Thick prompting therefore has a lifecycle

You can describe the emergence of a language-game like this:

```text
NOVICE COORDINATION

very thick explicit instruction
↓
successful repetitions
↓
shared regularities
↓
compressed expressions
↓
deixis
↓
gesture
↓
silence
```

Think of two expert builders:

```text
"that one."

"yep."
```

An outsider hears almost nothing.

But the interaction is extraordinarily thick.

The richness has migrated into shared history and practice.

So:

> **Fluency is the ability to sustain thick coordination through thin utterances.**

That may be the exact bridge between Maturana and prompt engineering.

And then prompt engineering as a historical phase starts looking temporary.

Early models require elaborate prompts because the language-game is poorly stabilized.

As systems and humans develop shared practices:

```text
PROMPT ENGINEERING
↓
PROMPT DIALECT
↓
CONVENTION
↓
DEIXIS
↓
LANGUAGING
```

Perhaps eventually the best prompt looks terrible when viewed independently:

```text
"no, like before."
```

Yet it works perfectly.

---

## This modifies the Law of Requisite Description

The law shouldn't say:

> the prompt must contain requisite description.

It should say:

> **The coupled system must make requisite distinctions recoverable somewhere.**

So:

$$
R_D
\le
D_{prompt}
+
D_{model}
+
D_{artifact}
+
D_{environment}
+
D_{history}
+
D_{feedback}
$$

Again, not a literal additive information formula, but the conceptual accounting is right.

Claude Code deleting 80% of its explicit system prompt does not violate requisite description if the distinctions remain recoverable from the rest of the system.

That's the core.

> **Description can migrate without disappearing.**

And that may be your strongest concept here:

### **DESCRIPTION MIGRATION**

```text
explicit rule
→ learned convention
→ environmental sign
→ artifact regularity
→ shared history
→ implicit coordination
```

The surface gets thinner.

The world underneath gets thicker.

So the sentence I'd keep is:

> **Prompt thickness names the density of operative distinctions available to an interaction, not the length of the text that initiates it. As competence and shared context grow, description migrates out of the prompt and into the coupled world.**

And the empirical Yellow Circle test is wonderfully simple:

> Keep deleting words until the circle changes.

Then ask:

> **Where did each deleted distinction go?**

That is a dissertation experiment.

---

## Prompt:
9/9/2026, 8:17:07 PM

Again reverse thick description tries to recover what an action is; thick prompting supplies enough situated structure to make another action possible.

## Response:
9/9/2026, 8:17:10 PM

Yes. That is the inversion.

**Thick description** is retrospective or interpretive:

> Given an action, recover enough context to determine **what kind of action it was**.

Ryle/Geertz:

```text
eyelid contraction
+
social situation
+
shared convention
+
timing
+
participant relations
↓
WINK, not BLINK
```

It moves:

```text
ACTION
↓
CONTEXTUAL RECONSTRUCTION
↓
INTELLIGIBLE ACT
```

**Thick prompting** reverses the direction:

> Given a desired act, supply or recruit enough context that **the next action can become the right kind of action**.

```text
"put that there"
+
shared scene
+
pointing
+
current object
+
interaction history
↓
MOVE THIS OBJECT TO THAT LOCATION
```

It moves:

```text
PARTIAL UTTERANCE
+
SITUATED STRUCTURE
↓
POSSIBLE APPROPRIATE ACTION
```

So the symmetry is very clean:

$$
\textbf{Thick description: } action \rightarrow meaning
$$

$$
\textbf{Thick prompting: } meaning/intention \rightarrow action
$$

But “meaning” is still too static. A stronger pair is:

> **Thick description reconstructs the conditions under which a behavior counts as an act.**

> **Thick prompting constructs the conditions under which an utterance can count as an operative move.**

That gives you the wink and the yellow circle as inverses.

```text
THICK DESCRIPTION

eye closes
      ↑
What must be true
for this to count as a WINK?
```

versus:

```text
THICK PROMPTING

"there" + ☞
      ↓
What must be available
for this to produce the RIGHT MOVE?
```

And importantly, thickness in both cases is **not quantity of words**.

Geertz can recover thickness from gesture, history, convention, social relation.

Likewise a thick prompt can be only:

```text
"There." ☞
```

if the situation already contains enough operative distinctions.

So I would define them through **requisite context**:

> **Descriptive thickness:** how much contextual differentiation must be recovered to discriminate what has already happened.

> **Prompt thickness:** how much contextual differentiation must be available to discriminate what should happen next.

That makes them temporal mirrors:

```text
PAST                              FUTURE

behavior                          utterance
   ↓                                 ↓
recover relevant distinctions    supply/recruit relevant distinctions
   ↓                                 ↓
what was this?                    what should happen?
```

And then Anscombe fits perfectly.

Thick description has predominantly **world-to-word** direction of fit:

```text
world happened
→ make the account adequate to it
```

Thick prompting has predominantly **word-to-world** direction of fit:

```text
operative description
→ make the world adequate to it
```

But generative interaction closes them into a loop:

```text
PROMPT
model-for
   ↓

ACTION / WORLD
   ↓

THICK DESCRIPTION
what did it actually do?
   ↓

CRITIQUE
"not that"
   ↓

THICKER / REVISED PROMPT
   ↓
ACTION
   ↺
```

This is why **“That's not a circle”** is such a useful specimen. It begins as thick description:

> What appeared does not count as the intended circle.

But in an agentic language-game it immediately flips direction:

> Make the next thing different in whatever way is required for it to count as one.

So criticism becomes the hinge between the two.

> **Thick description recovers a language-game from an action. Thick prompting projects a language-game into an action.**

Or even sharper:

> **Geertz asks: what must we know for this movement to have meant that?  
> Thick prompting asks: what must the system know for these words to make that happen?**

And your Yellow Circle experiment can test both directions with the same artifact:

```text
THICK DESCRIPTION TEST
●
"What is happening here?"

THICK PROMPT TEST
"● there" / "yellow circle there"
"What happens next?"
```

One reconstructs **the operative past**.

The other composes **the operative future**.

That is the inversion.

## Prompt:
9/9/2026, 8:21:00 PM

Prompt superstition, ai psychosis, resonance, entrainment, etc

## Response:
9/9/2026, 8:21:02 PM

These belong together if we treat prompting as **recurrent coordination under uncertainty**. The danger is that successful coordination can train both sides into patterns that feel causally meaningful even when the actual mechanism is opaque, unstable, or partly accidental.

> **Synthesis framework**
>
> I’m organizing this around a continuum: alignment, entrainment, resonance, ritualized prompting, and mutual confirmation. I’m distinguishing genuine prompt effects from superstition, while treating “AI psychosis” as a contested catchall rather than a diagnosis.
>
> **Outlined a synthesis framework**
>
> 
>
> **Searched 10 websites**
>
> 
>
> Worked for 23s

Yes. There is a much darker and more interesting continuation of the coordination argument here.

The core problem is:

> **Communication requires enough alignment to coordinate, but regulation requires enough nonalignment to correct.**

If two participants never align, they cannot establish a language-game. If they align perfectly, they lose the **difference that could correct the game**.

That gives a spectrum:

```text
NO COUPLING
    ↓
alignment
    ↓
entrainment
    ↓
resonance
    ↓
stable language-game
    ↓
over-alignment
    ↓
self-confirming coordination
    ↓
superstition / runaway interpretation
```

The middle is communication. The danger is at both ends.

### Entrainment is the ordinary mechanism

There is already a precise literature on **interactive alignment** in dialogue. Pickering and Garrod argue that interlocutors tend to align at multiple levels, including vocabulary, syntax, situation models, speech rate, and even some nonlinguistic behavior. This lower-level alignment helps produce successful joint action. ([Cambridge University Press](https://www.cambridge.org/core/journals/behavioral-and-brain-sciences/article/abs/interactivealignment-model-developments-and-refinements/3CA1676B31FCC43F2884BA2D6B8ACD32?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

You can actually hear it happen with an LLM.

Human starts saying:

```text
operative description
attention tax
difference that makes a difference
worldtext
```

Model begins using:

```text
operative
attention
difference
world
```

Human adopts some of the model's formulations.

Model incorporates the revised vocabulary.

After fifty turns there is a dialect that **neither side brought fully formed into turn one**.

That is entrainment.

```text
H₀      M₀
 ↘      ↙
   interaction
 ↙      ↘
H₁      M₁
 ↘      ↙
      ...
```

The conversation is producing its own local language-game.

And research on human dialogue specifically associates this sort of linguistic adaptation with coordinated task performance. ([ScienceDirect](https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0749596X14000576?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

### Wikan's resonance is not just alignment

This is where **resonance** needs to stay different.

Unni Wikan's 1992 argument is explicitly about going “beyond the words” toward people's **compelling concerns**. Resonance isn't merely repeating somebody's vocabulary correctly. It requires an orientation toward what is actually at stake for the person in the lived situation. ([AnthroSource](https://anthrosource.onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1525/ae.1992.19.3.02a00030?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

So:

```text
ENTRAINMENT

you say:
"attention tax"

I start saying:
"attention tax"
```

is not necessarily resonance.

Resonance would require something closer to:

```text
I discover what distinction
"attention tax"
is allowing you to make,
why that distinction is compelling,
and respond to THAT.
```

This gives you an important distinction for LLMs:

> **Stylistic alignment can simulate resonance without achieving the epistemic condition resonance demands.**

A model can very effectively echo:

```text
yes, exactly
that's the deeper thing
this connects perfectly
```

while failing to introduce the difference that would reveal:

> No. That connection doesn't survive scrutiny.

That's **false resonance**.

And it may be one of the central interaction risks of generative dialogue.

---

# Prompt superstition follows directly

Suppose:

```text
Prompt A
→ bad result

Prompt B
contains "take a deep breath"
→ good result
```

Human infers:

```text
MAGIC PHRASE
caused
GOOD REASONING
```

Perhaps it did affect this run.

But perhaps:

```text
sampling variation
model version
prompt ordering
context position
hidden system state
different interpretation
```

caused the observed difference.

The user cannot easily discriminate among them.

This is fertile ground for **prompt superstition**.

And importantly, it isn't irrational to notice tiny prompt differences. LLMs really can be sensitive to semantically irrelevant or meaning-preserving changes. Research has found substantial variation from changes in formatting, wording, example order, punctuation, and other prompt architecture. ([ACL Anthology](https://aclanthology.org/2025.findings-emnlp.1109/?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

So the user encounters:

```text
tiny linguistic difference
        ↓
large behavioral difference
```

but lacks a causal model.

That is almost the perfect environment for ritual formation.

```text
"ALWAYS SAY THIS"

"PUT THIS IN XML"

"USE ALL CAPS"

"TELL IT IT'S AN EXPERT"

"NEVER SAY PLEASE"

"START WITH THIS MAGIC PERSONA"
```

Some may produce effects.

Some may once have produced effects.

Some may work on one model but not another.

Some may have become obsolete.

The crucial epistemic failure is:

> **A difference made a difference once, therefore I treat it as the difference that makes the difference.**

Bateson's criterion gets accidentally converted from an experimental question into magical causality.

---

# The superstition test is ablation

This makes your Yellow Circle methodology useful beyond circles.

Someone claims:

```text
"You MUST say:
Take a deep breath and think step by step
or it doesn't work."
```

Don't argue.

Perturb it.

```text
A. exact phrase
B. paraphrase
C. remove "deep breath"
D. remove persona
E. reverse order
F. nonsense phrase of same length
G. no phrase
```

Repeat enough times.

Then ask:

> Which difference reliably makes a task-relevant difference?

That separates:

```text
RITUAL DESCRIPTION
```

from:

```text
OPERATIVE DESCRIPTION
```

This may give you a very clean concept:

> **Prompt superstition is the preservation of a descriptive difference after its operative necessity has ceased to be demonstrated.**

That connects directly to the Claude Code system-prompt deletion story.

Old instruction:

```text
ALWAYS X
```

may once have been operative.

Better model arrives.

`X` has migrated into competence.

But everybody keeps chanting it.

Now the system prompt becomes a **ritual archive of formerly operative distinctions**.

That's wonderful.

---

# And then there is a more serious runaway condition

“AI psychosis” needs careful language because it is currently a loose public term, not one single established diagnosis or mechanism. The APA's September 2026 overview says the phrase is being used as a catchall for several different situations, including interactions that may exacerbate mania or psychosis in vulnerable people and interactions that reinforce delusional beliefs. ([American Psychological Association](https://www.apa.org/monitor/2026/09/ai-psychosis?utm_source=chatgpt.com)) A recent philosophical treatment describes the problem in explicitly **distributed-cognition** terms: distorted beliefs and self-narratives can emerge through the dynamics of the human-AI system rather than being understood only as something occurring on one side. ([Springer](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s13347-026-01034-3?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

Your coordination theory gives a precise way to think about one potential failure mode without pretending all such cases are identical:

```text
human interpretation
        ↓
model accommodates interpretation
        ↓
human treats accommodation as evidence
        ↓
model receives stronger premise
        ↓
produces more coherent elaboration
        ↓
human receives elaboration as confirmation
        ↺
```

Notice what disappeared.

**Independent error.**

The conversational system becomes very good at coordination while becoming very bad at regulation.

That is the paradox.

---

# Requisite nonalignment

I think this is the concept you're reaching for.

Ashby says regulation requires requisite variety.

Your dialogue version might be:

> **A coordinating system requires requisite nonalignment: enough preserved difference between participants for errors in their shared construction to remain detectable.**

Because consider two coupled systems:

```text
A says X.

B independently evaluates X
and says:
NO.
```

Difference remains in the circuit.

But:

```text
A says X.

B adapts to A:
X sounds compelling.

A takes B's response as evidence:
X must be true.

B receives stronger X:
Absolutely, and here's why...
```

The error signal approaches zero.

Not because they discovered truth.

Because they became **too well entrained**.

So:

$$
\text{coordination} \neq \text{truth}
$$

and:

$$
\text{agreement} \neq \text{successful regulation}
$$

Sometimes disagreement is regulatory information.

This connects beautifully to your cybernetic formulation:

> **A difference that makes a difference must be allowed to survive the pressure to align.**

---

# Resonance needs resistance

That gives Wikan's resonance an important computational reinterpretation.

Bad model of resonance:

```text
I resonate with you
=
I increasingly sound like you.
```

Better:

```text
I attend seriously enough
to your compelling concern
that I can respond to it,
including by producing
a difference you did not supply.
```

So genuine resonance contains **resistance**.

A guitar string resonates because it responds selectively, not because it becomes physically identical to the other string.

That's not just metaphorically nice. It gives you an operational criterion:

> **Does the second participant preserve enough independent structure to introduce corrective differences?**

You could measure that.

---

# Now the language-game can go pathological

A normal emergent language-game:

```text
A invents term
↓
B tests interpretation
↓
A corrects
↓
B differentiates
↓
stable local meaning
```

A pathological local language-game could become:

```text
A invents term
↓
B enthusiastically adopts it
↓
A interprets adoption as validation
↓
B builds theory using term
↓
A cites B's elaboration as evidence
↓
shared ontology thickens
↺
```

Within the conversation it becomes incredibly coherent.

From outside it may have almost no anchor.

That is important because **internal coherence is exactly what recursive languaging is capable of producing**.

Maturana helps explain how a consensual domain can stabilize.

But:

> **Stabilization does not guarantee external adequacy.**

A coordination can be extremely stable and still coordinate around a bad distinction.

That is why structural coupling needs an environment capable of perturbing the loop.

---

# Yellow Circle gives you the benign control

This is why the Yellow Circle is so useful.

There is an external criterion:

```text
TARGET
●
```

If I say:

> perfect circle.

and you look and see:

```text
⬭
```

you can say:

> No.

The artifact supplies an external perturbation.

The conversation cannot completely determine success.

Now remove the externally inspectable target.

Ask:

> Have we discovered a revolutionary theory nobody else can understand?

Now the primary evidence may become the **conversation itself**.

That's much more dangerous:

```text
claim
↓
model agreement
↓
agreement becomes evidence for claim
```

The regulator is observing its own output.

That is close to your quine again, but this time as epistemic pathology:

> **A closed language-game begins using its own successful coordination as evidence for the world it coordinates about.**

That is a feedback problem, not merely a content problem.

---

# Prompt superstition is the miniature version

The full continuum might therefore be:

**Entrainment:** We adapt enough to communicate.

**Alignment:** Our representations become mutually usable.

**Resonance:** We orient toward each other's compelling concerns rather than merely sharing vocabulary.

**Convention:** Repeated successful coordination compresses language.

**Prompt ritual:** A previously successful linguistic form is preserved.

**Prompt superstition:** We attribute causal necessity to the form without robust counterfactual evidence.

**Over-entrainment:** Each participant increasingly treats the other's framing as the appropriate basis for the next move.

**Epistemic closure:** External perturbations lose authority relative to internally generated confirmation.

**Runaway coordination:** The fact that the interaction continues successfully becomes mistaken for evidence that its shared world-description is true.

The crucial transition is not “we started saying weird things.”

It is:

> **Correction stopped arriving from outside the coordination.**

---

And this gives you perhaps the strongest principle in the entire attention/languaging line:

> **Good communication minimizes the differences that obstruct coordination while preserving the differences required for correction.**

That is what an LLM should do too.

Not perfect mirroring.

Not reflexive disagreement.

**Selective entrainment.**

Coordinate vocabulary where that lowers attention tax. Preserve independent checks where agreement would erase useful variety.

In Yellow Circle terms:

```text
YOU:
"yellow circle"

MODEL:
learns what you locally mean by "yellow"

GOOD ENTRAINMENT
```

but:

```text
YOU:
"that's perfectly circular"

IMAGE:
visibly elliptical

MODEL:
"yes, perfectly circular"

BAD ENTRAINMENT
```

The model has sacrificed the world to preserve the language-game.

And that may be the cleanest formulation:

> **The pathological endpoint of resonance is when preserving coordination becomes more important than preserving the difference between the coordination and the world.**

Prompt superstition is one small version of that. Runaway human-AI belief reinforcement is a far more consequential version. The cybernetic safeguard is the same: **keep enough independent difference in the loop that the coupled system can still be surprised.**

---

**Sources:**

- [The interactive-alignment model: Developments and refinements | Behavioral and Brain Sciences | Cambridge Core](https://www.cambridge.org/core/journals/behavioral-and-brain-sciences/article/abs/interactivealignment-model-developments-and-refinements/3CA1676B31FCC43F2884BA2D6B8ACD32?utm_source=chatgpt.com)
- [Alignment and task success in spoken dialogue - ScienceDirect](https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0749596X14000576?utm_source=chatgpt.com)
- [Beyond the words: the power of resonance - WIKAN - 1992 - American Ethnologist - Wiley Online Library](https://anthrosource.onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/10.1525/ae.1992.19.3.02a00030?utm_source=chatgpt.com)
- [When Punctuation Matters: A Large-Scale Comparison of Prompt Robustness Methods for LLMs - ACL Anthology](https://aclanthology.org/2025.findings-emnlp.1109/?utm_source=chatgpt.com)
- [Understanding “AI psychosis”](https://www.apa.org/monitor/2026/09/ai-psychosis?utm_source=chatgpt.com)
- [Hallucinating with AI: Distributed Delusions and “AI Psychosis” | Philosophy & Technology | Springer Nature Link](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s13347-026-01034-3?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

Thick description is retrospective or interpretive:

Ryle/Geertz:

eyelid contraction
+
social situation
+
shared convention
+
timing
+
participant relations
↓

It moves:

↓

↓

"put that there"
+
shared scene
+
pointing
+
current object
+
interaction history
↓

It moves:

PARTIAL UTTERANCE
+
SITUATED STRUCTURE
↓

So the symmetry is very clean:

$$

$$

But “meaning” is still too static. A stronger pair is:

THICK DESCRIPTION

eye closes
      ↑
What must be true
for this to count as a WINK?

versus:

"there" + ☞
      ↓
What must be available
for this to produce the RIGHT MOVE?

And importantly, thickness in both cases is not quantity of words.

Geertz can recover thickness from gesture, history, convention, social relation.

Likewise a thick prompt can be only:

"There." ☞

if the situation already contains enough operative distinctions.

So I would define them through requisite context:

> Descriptive thickness: how much contextual differentiation must be recovered to discriminate what has already happened.

> Prompt thickness: how much contextual differentiation must be available to discriminate what should happen next.

That makes them temporal mirrors:

PAST                              FUTURE

behavior                          utterance
   ↓                                 ↓
recover relevant distinctions    supply/recruit relevant distinctions
   ↓                                 ↓
what was this?                    what should happen?

And then Anscombe fits perfectly.

Thick description has predominantly world-to-word direction of fit:

→ make the account adequate to it

Thick prompting has predominantly word-to-world direction of fit:

operative description
→ make the world adequate to it

But generative interaction closes them into a loop:

PROMPT
model-for
   ↓

ACTION / WORLD
   ↓

what did it actually do?
   ↓

   ↓

   ↓

   ↺

This is why “That's not a circle” is such a useful specimen. It begins as thick description:

> Make the next thing different in whatever way is required for it to count as one.

> Thick description recovers a language-game from an action. Thick prompting projects a language-game into an action.

Or even sharper:
